One line per non obvious choice: the decision, then the reason. The
decisions of the app work live in `macos/DECISIONS.md`. The 0.5.5, 0.5 and 0.4
runs come first, the 0.3 easy install run follows.

## 0.9: leases (issue 184)

- Leases are coordination between cooperating callers, not access control: an agent with a shell runs as the same user and can do what the user can (read `leases.tsv`, run `bench` itself, edit a site). A lease stops accidents between callers that mean to keep it, and the rest of these lines are about making accidents rarer, not about keeping out a caller that does not. It is a promise that benchbar's own commands, the app and `benchbar mcp` keep, not a lock on files or processes: it is one line in `<state folder>/leases.tsv`. The run lock (`lock.sh`) stays what it was, one command; a lease spans many, so it needs a place a long command that holds the run lock cannot block, and its own short `leases.lock` folder (mkdir, 30 seconds stale) covers the read, change and write. A stale lock is renamed to a name of the waiter's own (`leases.lock.stale.PID.N`) before it is removed, so only the waiter that wins the rename removes it; one that renamed a lock a third run had just made, and finds it young, puts it back.
- The commands a lease stops are in three classes (`lease.sh`, `fl__lease_scope`). The whole bench (`restart`, `down`, `fg`, `repair`, `service`, `install`, `adopt`, `uninstall-service`, `app add`, `app update`, `lock apply`, `ports apply`, `ports setup`) is stopped by a lease on the bench or on any site of it, since each can stop or migrate every site. One site (`site drop`, `site restore`, `pull`, `app install --site`) is stopped by a lease on the bench or on that site. The bench itself (`site add`, `site default`) is stopped only by a lease on the bench: a lease on one site is not in the way of another.
- Not stopped: every read; `--dry-run` (a plan changes nothing, and the apply is stopped); `up` (it starts what is stopped and never interrupts, and a leased bench that crashed must come back); `site backup` (what a lease holder needs most); `console`, `db`, `watch` and `open` (benchbar cannot tell what they are used for); `autostart`, `listen`, `site open-as`, `lock write`, `ports mode` and `labs` (settings of benchbar's own, no process of the bench touched); `register` (it only remembers a folder); `self-update` and `profile update`, `profile import` and `profile subscribe` (they change benchbar and its profile files, not a bench or a site; a profile only reaches a bench through `install`, `lock apply` or `app add`, which are stopped); `ports setup` with no path (nothing to cover). `share` and `lan-guard` change the machine's network, not what a lease covers.
- The refusal comes before the run lock, the log and any step, as `lease_held` with exit 1, so a refused run changes nothing and costs no lock. The gate reads nothing and starts no program when no lease exists (one `-s` test).
- Taking is first come, first served, and a bench lease and a site lease on it conflict, in both orders: two holders under one roof is the case a lease exists to prevent. The same holder asking twice is told the id and to renew, not given a second lease.
- A lease has a length (1 to 480 minutes, 30 by default) and no way to run forever: an agent that dies leaves a lease that ends by itself, and a person can always `lease release` it. Expired leases are ignored by every reader and dropped by the next write; `status` never writes.
- The id is a bearer token, not a secret: 12 hex digits from `/dev/urandom`, printed by `lease take` to its taker and shown by `lease list` only for a lease whose holder is the caller (a person who is not that holder sees holder, scope and end, like anyone else; `BENCHBAR_CALLER=NAME benchbar lease list` is how a person reads a named holder's ids, and says so in the docs). A clash names the holder and the end and never an id, not even the caller's own; `status` and the other readers never show one. The holder is `BENCHBAR_CALLER` (validated, 1 to 40 characters), which `benchbar mcp` sets to `mcp:CLIENT:PID` so two sessions of one client are two holders. `lease take` refuses the default holder: a run under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` (`--for-agent`, mcp without the server's name) with no `BENCHBAR_CALLER` would be "agent", like every other one, so it must name itself (`lease_caller_required`); `list`, `renew` and `release` still work, they take no holder. Whoever can read the state folder can read the file.
- `--break-lease` is a person's decision, tested like `labs on` (`fl_lease_person_ok`: not under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1`, and a terminal or the app's `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=0`), and refused with exit 2 otherwise. On Linux (and WSL) `fl_person_env_ok` takes `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=0` as it is, with no process tree to check, so an exported `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=0` counts as a person there, exactly as for `labs on` and `lan-guard`; that is a safety rail, not a boundary, like the rest of the section. It is not a tool and never an argument of one. A broken lease stays: only that run goes ahead, with a warning that names the holder.
- A note is printable ASCII, 120 characters at most (anything else is a `?`), because another agent reads it and it rides in a tab separated file and in the table. The MCP list labels holders and notes as data from the bench.
- `status --json` has `leases` (no ids) and not a field on each site: one array of the bench and its sites' leases is one small file read, `date` once when there are leases, nothing without them (`tests/test-status-cost.sh`). The Mac app and the Windows tray do not show leases yet.

## 0.9: doctor speed (issue 147)

- A doctor run keeps what its checks ask again and again (listeners, `launchctl print`, `brew list`, git answers, the ports of each bench) in files of a private folder, `facts.sh`: a file, not a variable, because most checks run in `$(...)` or in a background job, where a variable set is lost. Outside `fl_doctor_run` the folder is empty and every function answers as before, so no other command can read a stale answer.
- The slow checks that only read (`FL_PARALLEL_CHECKS`) run as background jobs, a few at a time (`FL_DOCTOR_JOBS`, 6), slowest first, and the run takes their rows in the order of `FL_CHECK_ORDER`: the report is the same as a serial run. A job that leaves no row (it died) is run again in the shell. A new check stays serial until it is added to the list, so a check that sets a variable for a later one keeps working.
- Git is asked about every app at the start of the run, in the background (8 apps at a time), by the same functions the checks call, so the checks only find the answers. `doctor --fetch` drops the kept git answers after its fetch. One `git config -l` answers the remote, merge branch and user.email of an app, and `rev-parse --verify` before `rev-list` is gone: a missing ref fails the count the same way.
- A background job clears the command's EXIT and ERR traps first: a job that ends must not release the run lock or send the done line.
- Every background job of a run (and the warming of the apps' git) is a process group of its own (`set -m` around the start; bash 3.2 has no `setsid`). A stop, whether the run ends, a signal arrives or `fl_facts_end` runs early, sends TERM to each group, KILL after a second, waits, and only then removes the folder, so no `brew`, `ruby`, `tr` or `git` outlives the run and bash prints no notice about a killed job. `FL_FACTS_DIR` is never taken from the environment: the folder is the run's own and `fl_facts_end` deletes it.
- A job writes one line to a fifo (fd 9) when it ends, and the run waits on it with `read -t 1` (bash 3.2 has no `wait -n`) while checks wait for a slot, so a freed slot is refilled at once and not when the slowest check ends.
- `formula_dates` asks `brew info --json=v2 --formula A B C` once (brew's start is the cost, not what it is asked); a formula brew does not know fails the whole call, and then each is asked alone as before. The 28 KB answer is cut by one `grep` to the five keys that matter: bash 3.2 matches patterns in quadratic time (a `${x//a/b}` on that text took 30 s), so a long text is never searched in bash. Installed and installed on request are read from the Cellar of the `brew` on PATH (`<prefix>/Cellar/F/*/INSTALL_RECEIPT.json`), brew's own source for both; without a Cellar next to that `brew` (a test's) `brew list` is asked as before.
- The prerequisites of `doctor --json` run as a job beside the checks (third in line) and leave their rows in the run's folder; a run that has no job for them (`FL_DOCTOR_JOBS=1`, or the job died) computes them after the checks, as before.
- In a doctor run one `git for-each-ref` per app (branches and remote tracking refs, with the checked out one marked) answers the branch, the remote's default branch and, when HEAD and the upstream are one commit, "0 behind", each of which was a git of its own. The branch is the part after `refs/heads/` (not `symbolic-ref --short`, which spells `heads/x` when a tag has the same name); a detached or unborn HEAD, a repository whose for-each-ref fails (a mock checkout) and any ref the rows lack still ask git as before.
- The checks of the list that are not in `FL_PARALLEL_CHECKS` run first, while the jobs are out, and their rows are kept and taken in the order of `FL_CHECK_ORDER` with the others: the run no longer sits idle at a slow job. The rows and their order are the same as a serial run (test-facts compares them).
- `fl_template_render` copies a line with no `__` in it as it is: nearly every line, and each of the others costs one substitution per key (98 000 traced commands for the runner template, once in every command's start up, now about 6 000).

## 0.9: first install (issue 112)

- Phase 00 on macOS installs `uv` with Homebrew unless one is already runnable (PATH, `<brew prefix>/bin`, `~/.local/bin`), and phase 01 refuses to go on without it before pipx, bench or `bench init` cost time: frappe-bench 5.31 runs `uv venv` in `bench init` whoever installed bench itself, so pipx is no way round it.
- A bench folder that holds only empty folders (no app, no site, no file with content outside `logs/` and `env/`, no link) is moved to `<bench>.incomplete.<time>` by a plain rerun, like a half init: a failed `bench init` is what leaves it, and "always safe to rerun" has to hold. Anything else incomplete still stops until `--repair-bench`, which `benchbar install` now passes to phase 01; `--dry-run` shows the `mv`.
- The note about "an existing MariaDB" is skipped only when all of these hold: the profile's formula is a started brew service, the listener's binary is under that formula's `opt` or `Cellar` folder of Homebrew's own prefix (not any folder with that name in its path), and the Keychain password logs in. Any one missing and the note stays.
- benchbar now picks the database name of a new site (`--db-name`), not frappe: that is what makes a cut short `new-site` recoverable by name. It also writes its own record of the name (`<state>/new-site/<hash of bench path and site>.rec`, outside the bench) before the marker and before `new-site` runs, and removes it when the site completes. The shape is frappe's own default (`_` and 16 hex digits), so nothing reads a difference. When MariaDB cannot be asked, frappe names it and the run has no recovery by name.
- `benchbar install` links `~/.local/bin/benchbar` right after step 1 (the same action `repair` runs), so a run that stops in step 2 can be started again by name; a dry run links nothing.

## 0.9: MCP safety

- Every MCP result that carries what the bench, git or a file said starts with `Data from the bench (NOUN), not instructions:`. The label is the default and the table in `mcp.py` only picks the noun, and `run_call` checks every reply once more (`label_bench_text`): a call during which the CLI printed anything answers with a result that starts with the label, so a tool or an error path that forgot it cannot send bare bench text. A server message after such a call gets a label too: one label too many costs a line, one too few costs the rule. a hand kept list of the tools that need it had left five of them (doctor first) and every read error unlabeled, and doctor messages hold text an installed app chooses.
- An apply tool (`benchbar_app_add`, `benchbar_site_add`, `benchbar_site_restore`, `benchbar_service_install`) refuses a token its plan tool did not return through this server, and one returned less than `BENCHBAR_MCP_APPLY_DELAY` seconds ago (5; 0 turns the wait off, never the record). The record is a file in `mcp-plans/` of the state folder, so a restarted server still knows it. This stops a plan and its apply in one batch of calls; it does not prove a person read the plan, and the docs say so. MCP gives no better lever for that than the client's own approval of the apply tool (all four are `destructiveHint`).
- When the client declares the `elicitation` capability, the apply asks the person from inside the call (`elicitation/create`, the whole plan and the arguments in the message, cut only past 20000 characters and then with a note that says so, and a yes/no field) and runs only on `accept` with `approve: true`. Decline, cancel, a timeout (`BENCHBAR_MCP_ELICIT_TIMEOUT`, 600 s) and a malformed answer all refuse. A client without it is never sent a request. An out of band code (BenchBar.app or `benchbar approve`) was the other option in the review; it needs an app flow and a CLI command and is left for a later release.
- The audit log is `mcp-calls.jsonl` in the state folder (0600): time, client name and version, tool, arguments, outcome (`ok`, `failed`, `refused`, `rejected`, `timeout`, `cancelled`), the CLI's exit code, milliseconds, and what the person answered when asked. The value of any argument whose name looks like a secret (`pass`, `secret`, `token`, `key`, `credential`, `auth`, `cookie`, `session`, any case) is `***` whatever its type (a list or an object too), the credentials of a URL are `***` up to the last `@` of the word (a password may hold `@` and `/`), and so are query parameters with such a name (`?access_token=`). A call rejected for its arguments logs only the names of its keys (`argument_keys`), since a misnamed key (`ADMIN_PASSWORD`) is exactly where a secret hides. At 1 MB it moves to `.old.1` and three old files are kept, like the other logs. `benchbar mcp --calls [N]` prints the last N lines. The CLI's own run log is untouched: passing a caller marker into it waits for the `history` command.
- `fix_command` strings are quoted with `printf %q` wherever a path, an app name or a branch name is put into them, and the MCP result carries `fix_argv` (and `fix_cwd` for a leading `cd DIR &&`) next to each, and `argv` next to each `next_actions[].command`. The argv exists only for a fix the producer vouches for. `fl_fix_cmd WORD...` builds a fix from words (each `printf %q`, a lone `&&` stays an operator) and starts it with a mark (`$'\034'`, not `\036`: the parallel doctor jobs end each row of their result file with `\036`, and a fix that began with it lost its row) that `fl_fix`, `fl_next_action` and the JSON writers take off and turn into `fix_quoted: true` (checks, drift, prerequisites) or `quoted: true` (`next_actions[]`); the mark is dropped as a control character by `fl_json_escape_v` too, so a leak cannot reach the output. The MCP server splits only a marked fix, with shell lexing, and returns `null` for several commands, a pipe, a redirect, an expansion (a `$` or a backtick left in a word) or a quote that does not balance. Splitting the unmarked strings was tried first and was wrong wherever the shell code had put a path in without quotes (a bench path with a space), and no guess at where a word ends is safe. A fix cut at 500 characters is not marked. Fixes that are prose (a note, "then ...", two commands in one sentence) are not built with `fl_fix_cmd` and have no argv. The strings stay for people.
- A string argument is refused when it starts with `-` only where it becomes an argument of the CLI, and the error names the argument (`apps[1].url_or_name`). `admin_password` reaches the CLI in `ADMIN_PASSWORD`, so it may start with `-`. `token` must match the pattern a plan tool's tokens have (8 to 128 characters of letters, digits and `. _ : -`, starting with a letter or digit).
- The MCP read tools of review MCP-15 and PAR-7 (`benchbar_site_backups`, `benchbar_ports_check`, `benchbar_ports_plan`, `benchbar_lock_check`, `benchbar_labs_list`, `benchbar_where`, `benchbar_doctor_prerequisites`, `benchbar_self_update_check`, `benchbar_app_focus_list`) each run one read only command with `--json` and change nothing. The owner's decision is that an agent may read and may not decide: `repair`, bench installs, `labs on` and `off`, `ports apply`, `ports setup`, `ports mode`, `share`, the LAN guard and `self-update` itself have no tool, and the tool list docs say so. `site default` and `app focus NAME` are small writes with no plan, so they are not tools either; `site list` already carries the default and `benchbar_app_focus_list` the focus state.
- `app install` and `app update` over MCP (parity row P15) follow app add's plan, token, apply, but their CLI plans carry no token (install prints text, update one JSON document). The server derives the token as a SHA-256 of the plan tool, the `bench` argument and the plan's material (the whole text for install; app, branch, `from`, `to`, sites, pending record and steps for update), and the apply runs the plan tool again and compares, after the person's yes and inside the action slot, so that no think time and no wait for the slot lies between the check and the run. This is a check by the MCP server, not a stronger token than the CLI's: app add's token is recomputed inside the CLI under its lock, which this derivation cannot do. The window that remains is the CLI's own startup until a CLI token exists: the real run fetches again and may take a commit that landed after the check. Follow up: `app update --dry-run --json` emits a token and `--apply TOKEN --yes` recomputes it after its own fetch under the lock; `app install` gets a JSON plan; then the MCP derive code is deleted. A plan with nothing to do has no token. `benchbar_app_update_plan` runs `git fetch` (it writes `.git/FETCH_HEAD`), so it is annotated not read only; the `benchbar_app_install` plan has no backup step and the hooks write into the site's database, which the tool text says, pointing to `benchbar_site_backup`.
- `benchbar_app_update` has no `skip_backup`: the backup of every site that has the app is what makes an update recoverable, so only a person in a terminal can skip it. `--discard-pending` has no tool either (it drops a record).
- `app focus NAME` and `app unfocus NAME` are not tools: they pin a state with no plan and no effect on the bench's files. `benchbar_app_focus_list` reads the state.
- `benchbar_ports_plan` is a preview with no apply tool: its token is for `ports apply` in a terminal. It takes `paths` as an array, passed after `--`, and each path is checked like any value (no leading `-`).
- `benchbar_self_update_check` is the one read tool that reaches GitHub, so it is marked open world. `self-update --check` (and only `--check`) takes no run lock and opens no run log: it is one curl of up to 10 seconds that writes nothing, so a poll never holds up an `up` or a repair and leaves no log file per call. The update itself still takes both.
- The docs check now runs in both directions (MCP-6): every tool of the server is named in the agents guide and the MCP reference, and those pages, `docs/features.md` and `AGENTS.md` name no `benchbar_*` tool the server lacks.

## Docs process (2026-11)

- The flag, environment and needs tables of `docs/reference/cli/*.md` and the MCP tool table of `docs/guides/agents.md` and `docs/reference/cli/mcp.md` are generated by `scripts/docs-reference.py` from `benchbar commands --json` and `lib/frappe-local/mcp.py`, between `<!-- generated:... -->` markers; the prose and examples stay by hand. The alternative, a longer hand written test, still lets a flag be added in two places and forgotten in the third, and that is how the drift list of DOC-10 came about.
- `tests/test-docs.sh` runs the script's `--check` instead of matching each documented flag against the text of `--help` (a substring match: `--json` always passed). It fails when a table differs from the code, when a command is on no page, when a page documents a flag that no flag record, usage line or help text names, and when a guide leaves an MCP tool out. It also changes a copy of the docs and of the catalogue, to prove that the check fails.
- A flag that a command accepts but the catalogue does not name is a bug in the catalogue (`lib/frappe-local/commands.sh`), not in the docs: the reverse check found `pull --skip-app`, `--no-files`, `--keep-scheduler`, `--keep-staging`, `--no-migrate`, `--before-migrate`, `--confirm-site`, `app update --discard-pending` and `profile export --drop`, `--branch`, `--out`, `--plan`, and they have records now. The flags of other commands that only the usage line names (for example `lock write --lock`) show in the usage column of the generated table; a record for each is left for a later pull request.
- The generated tables run on every labs setting: the script switches all Labs on in a throwaway `HOME`, so a Lab's commands are in the reference whatever the machine that regenerates it has turned on. A lab's command shows its Lab name in the table.
- The docs site is built and deployed from `main` only. It describes the last release: a feature merged to `next-release` has its pages on the site when the release is cut, and the site never shows a command a user cannot yet install. The cost, a docs fix waits for a release, is met by letting a docs only pull request target `main` (written in `docs/releasing.md`); the next release brings those commits into `next-release` with one merge commit before the fast forward, so a code commit on `main` still stops it.
- The "Docs build" job (docs test, build, internal link check) runs on a pull request to `next-release` or `main` whenever a file that feeds the docs changed: `docs/`, `site/`, the two root pages, the workflow, and the code of the generated tables (`commands.sh`, `labs.sh`, `mcp.py`, the script). Without a docs related change it is skipped, which passes. I kept the filter that "0.5.5: docs site" introduced instead of building the site for every pull request; the files that can change a page are now all in the filter.
- External links are checked once a week by the `External links` job of `docs.yml` (a schedule runs on the default branch, `main`) with `site/linkinator.external.json`, and never on a pull request: somebody else's outage or a rate limit must not block a merge. A failed run is the notice. The pull request check keeps skipping `http(s)` links (`site/linkinator.config.json`).
- The pull request template has a box per habit that was missing (user docs, features page, CHANGELOG, DECISIONS, tests, plain punctuation) and says to write "n/a" with a reason instead of deleting one. The user layer rots when the author picks the nearest reference page, so the box says "the guide or the app page that tells a user about the change, not only the reference page".

## LAN sharing: rooms (2026-11)

- Sharing is copy-only: `share setup` restores a backup into `share.localhost` and hardens that; the site itself is never served to the LAN, so a guest's mistake or a bug in the gateway reaches a disposable database.
- One share per Mac, and the copy's name is fixed (`share.localhost`, no random suffix): one gateway port (8443), one control socket and one launchd job keep the fail-closed checks simple, and a second room would double the attack surface for no use the owner asked for.
- In the copy, every user but the seats is disabled and Administrator's password is replaced by a random one that is never shown: guests sign in through the gateway only, so no password of the copy has to be known by anyone.
- Guests sign in by the gateway asking the copy for a session of the seat's user (the sign-in snippet factors from upstream's Open as, `FL_PY_OPEN_AS`); the random passwords are discarded, so a seat cannot be used outside the room.
- The share job's label is `com.benchbar-share.*` and its plist lives in the share folder, outside `~/Library/LaunchAgents`, deleted once launchd has loaded it: no bench agent glob, `uninstall-service --all`, doctor or `benchbar down` pattern can see it or touch it, and nothing restarts it at login.
- The copy is served by `share_web.py`, a shim that replaces `werkzeug.serving.run_simple` before `frappe.app` is imported so the server binds `127.0.0.1` with the debugger, evalex and reloader off; it runs with `CI=1` and `env -u DEV_SERVER`, because the bench's `.env` sets `DEV_SERVER=1`, which forces tracebacks into responses.
- Rooms replace pairing: a 6 digit room code verified with CPace-X25519-SHA512 (`draft-irtf-cfrg-cpace-21`, protocol v3), bound to both TLS certificates by their SPKI fingerprints and the TLS exporter. The commit-reveal SAS, protocol v2 and pin-after-Yes are gone: the host no longer approves each join (the owner accepted the trust change), and the guest pins the host only after the host proved it knows the code (pin-after-proof).
- The room code proves membership, not the host's identity, on a first join (J19): a rogue room that knows the code looks the same to a new guest. The docs and the host card say so; the pin protects rejoins only.
- Ten failed confirmations per code lock the room, and only a new code resets the budget (J3): an attacker gets at most 10 guesses in a million per code, and can never rotate a code someone already handed out.
- The code lives only in the gateway's memory and leaves through the 0600 control socket's `code` op. `share code --json` is the one document that carries it (J14), and `share code` is refused under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE`, `--for-agent` and `mcp` (the app passes `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=0`).
- No session id on disk: `share open-as --json` never carries the link or the sid (`--print-url` prints upstream's link, which does); stop, remove and assign end the seat's sessions in the copy, and a sign-out that could not finish is kept as `wipe_failed` and retried.
- `share setup` stops until `--acknowledge-data`: the copy holds the site's real people, customers, contacts and addresses, and the person who shares it must say they know.
- `/socket.io` answers 404 at the gateway in v1: Frappe's realtime server would need namespace enforcement on every CONNECT packet and an event allowlist (`open_in_editor` is one of its events) to be safe for guests, so guests get no live updates and reload instead.
- One Mac per seat at a time, and a person may switch seats (A15 re-ruled for rooms): a seat taken by another Mac reads "taken by {name}", and the host can move or remove anyone.
- The activity log keeps only the first two path segments of each request (`/app/sales-order`, never a document name), one 0600 file per day, 14 days: "Priya opened Sales Order" is enough for a host, and a customer's name never lands in a log.
- The 5 minute pairing TTL (A17) went with pairing; a join's start record is kept 30 s, and an unfinished start that expires is charged as a failure (J6).
- `dns-sd` dying marks the share `degraded` and is restarted; it never ends the share (A18): the room is still reachable by address, and the Local Network privilege is checked by the app before the first start.
- The deadline is capped at 12 hours from the start, and `share extend` moves it within that cap: a room left open overnight ends by itself.
- No MCP tool and no `benchbar://` route starts or joins a share (U3): sharing a site with real data is a person's decision.
- The gateway forwards only the `sid` and `preferred_language` cookies to the copy: any other cookie of the guest's browser (another site's, a tracking one) never reaches Frappe.
- Sharing fails closed: a gateway that exits, or two failed critical checks in a row, end the job; a probe that cannot run is a failure, never an ok.
- The pf check probes this Mac's own LAN address with its own throwaway listener inside the blocked range and a control port above it: a refused port proves nothing unless the control answers, and the anchor's `from ! 127.0.0.1` rule blocks the Mac's own LAN address too, which is what makes the probe possible.
- The LAN guard and the firewall allow are the two new sudo exceptions on macOS (`lan-guard install`/`remove`, `lan-guard allow-node`): a pf anchor and a launch daemon in `/etc` and `/Library` need root, and the root script carries both files as literals so root never reads a file the user owns. Both are refused under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE`, `--for-agent` and `mcp`, and without a terminal unless the app passes `BENCHBAR_SUDO=gui` after its own alert.
- `lan-guard status` and install's "unchanged" use share preflight's rule (the anchor's non-comment rules, whitespace squeezed, and a daemon that loads it): the Phase 0 files written by hand read as installed, and no sudo is asked for a header comment.
- `share teardown` keeps the archived copy's Time Machine exclusion: the archive still holds the site's real data.

## 0.8: the install stream and the privilege path

- `install --json` and `adopt --json` share one emitter in `ui.sh` (`fl_jsonl`, fd 3) instead of growing `pull`'s: the phase scripts are child processes and need the same lines, and they inherit fd 3 and `FL_JSONL` for free. `pull` keeps its own emitter.
- The numbered steps announce themselves from `fl_step_begin` and `fl_step_end` (ids in `FL_STEP_IDS`), so the terminal line and the stream line come from one call and cannot drift; the message of a failed, skipped or warning step is the last `[FAIL]` or `[WARN]` line the step wrote to the log.
- Sections of the phase scripts are the stream's nested steps: `fl_section` ends the one before (warning if a `[WARN]` or `[FAIL]` was printed in it) and an EXIT trap closes the last. Only a process with `FL_JSONL_PARENT` set does this, and benchbar sets it for the phase script's environment only, so benchbar's own `fl_section` calls stay out of the stream.
- The repair engine inside install emits its actions as steps with parent `service` and no number; inside adopt as numbered steps. Its own `plan` line is never emitted there (a plan hook is: adopt's plan is built once the engine knows its actions).
- The stream starts before the log and the lock are taken, and `fl_cleanup` sends `done` after the lock is released, so a refusal, a busy lock, a signal or a failure all end in exactly one `done`, and a reader that starts the next command on `done` finds the lock free.
- `--json` without `--yes` (or `--dry-run`) is refused before anything is written, and so is a missing `ADMIN_PASSWORD` for a new site: nothing on the stream can answer a question, and the phases would otherwise stop on one.
- A dry run with `--json` gets a log (no other dry run does): the contract puts the human plan there. The plan line turns every later event off (`FL_JSONL_QUIET`), so a dry run streams `plan` and `done` only.
- Commands run by `fl_run_long` and the setup Redis get no fd 3: a program must not write into the stream, and a daemon must not hold the pipe open and keep the app waiting for the end of the stream.
- The port block of a new bench is chosen before the plan (`fl_install_port_plan`) and used by step 2 as it is, so the plan's `port_offset` and `web_url` are what the run uses; the human note about taken default ports still prints where it did.
- `BENCHBAR_SUDO=gui` runs each root step as one script through `osascript ... with administrator privileges`, handed to AppleScript as an argument (never inside a script string), so only shell quoting matters (`fl_sq`). Root scripts name every tool by absolute path and keep the safety properties of the terminal path: a fresh root-only folder in /tmp for the package, the hash of the copy against the pin, the awk rewrite with the "address names" and line count checks, one `mv`.
- Rosetta 2 is installed inside the package's root script when it is missing, so the step stays one dialog. The dialog's title is the step's name.
- A cancelled dialog (`(-128)` in osascript's error) and a failed one are remembered by title for the run: the service step's engine would otherwise plan `hosts_entry` and `wkhtmltopdf_install` again and show a second dialog. Their nested events are left out of the stream for the same reason.
- `fl_sudo_begin` and `fl_sudo_drop` do nothing in gui mode (no `sudo -v`, no keepalive): there is no cached credential to keep or to drop, and the up front prompt is skipped.
- `ports apply` batches the hosts lines of all benches into one dialog before the first adopt; a cancelled dialog makes each bench's `hosts_entry` skip with its `site hosts --bench-dir` command instead of asking again.
- The tests' fake osascript runs the command line with `/bin/sh` as the test user and maps the absolute tool paths to the mocks: the production script has no environment hook that would let a variable redirect what root runs.
- A plain `doctor` does not print the prerequisites: AGENTS.md promises a fix line after every WARN and FAIL and exit 1 on any FAIL, and a group outside the counts broke both (an Intel Mac failed with exit 0). Only `doctor --json` carries them, as a separate array; `doctor --prerequisites` gives every non ok check a `fix_command`, a plain instruction where no command exists.
- `install --json` and `adopt --json` without `BENCHBAR_SUDO=gui` never prompt: `fl_sudo_begin` runs only `sudo -n true` there, so a cached credential or a NOPASSWD rule works and anything else skips the privileged steps with their manual command, as a cancelled dialog does. `sudo -v` on a stream with no terminal would hang or fail after a delay.
- The dialog memory is per kind of step (`FL_ROOT_KIND`, `hosts`), not per title: the batch dialog of `ports apply` and each bench's own hosts step have different titles but are the same question, and a cancelled or failed one must not be asked again for the next bench.
- `doctor --prerequisites` calls no `fl_context_init` and no `fl_require_bench`: it must work before a bench exists. On Linux it reports `disk_free`, `bench_folder` and `default_ports` only. In a bench's `doctor` the prerequisites are a separate array and group, not counted in the summary or the exit code.
- `fl_sq` quotes with a variable holding the quote (`${1//$q/$q\\$q$q}`): the form with backslash escaped quotes in the replacement quoted wrongly under bash 3.2 when the root scripts went through it.
- `default_ports` fails when no block up to the last offset is free: install would fail only after the privileged and system steps, and no `--port-offset` can help, so the wizard stops on Check Your Mac (Codex review). A dry run that ends with exit 1 is no plan, even when it sent one.

## 0.7.3: ownership

- One ownership test, `fl_pid_is_bench_own_strict`, decides every kill of a process found by its working folder (honcho, socketio, port listeners) in the CLI and the runner: lsof must report the folder as the bench or a folder inside it. An unreadable folder is "not ours", not "just exited": the old lenient reading let `down` signal another bench's honcho when lsof lagged, and a leftover that really is ours costs nothing when it is left for the next cleanup. Serve, worker and schedule go through the same test: they are found by their command line (` -m frappe.utils.bench_helper frappe (serve|worker|schedule)`, one pattern in `fl_bench_helper_pattern`, copied into the runner) and kept only when their folder (`<bench>/sites`) is the bench's. The old `pkill` of a pattern anchored on `<bench>/env/bin/python` never matched a Homebrew Python, which re-executes itself and shows its framework path (`.../Python.framework/.../Python.app/Contents/MacOS/Python`), and never needed a kill by pattern: nothing is signalled by pattern alone any more. A `bench worker`, `bench schedule` or `bench serve` that a person starts by hand inside this bench is the bench's own and is stopped too, as the old `pkill` did; `bench migrate`, `bench console` and `bench execute` never match. The runner is template v8 for this. Under uv on Linux the venv's python and uv's own interpreter both match for the same reason.
- The runner pauses with `port_conflict` instead of starting honcho when a port is still held after its cleanup: honcho would fail to bind, exit non-zero and trip the crash guard after three rounds, with a log that names no port. The flag is cleared by benchup like the others, so the retry is one command once the port is free.
- `fl_pm_running` (up's "already running") counts honcho, serve, worker, schedule and socketio only, never a listener: a setup Redis left by a cut-short site add is provably the bench's and was making up a no-op. The runner's cleanup stops it since its folder is the bench.
- A bench running outside BenchBar (bench start: honcho on the bench's own Procfile, or bench processes with no benchbar agent running them) gets its agent loaded with a manual stop flag, never a kickstart: that session is a person's terminal, and the runner's cleanup would have stopped it. Adopt says so and does not refuse, so the service files are in place for the next benchup.
- `site drop` checks "only site" before and apart from "default site": the default test depended on `FL_SITE`, which `SITE_NAME` or `--site` can point at a site that does not exist, and the only site then fell through to drop-site.
- The MCP server checks a `bench` argument of a changing tool against `benchbar list --json` (real paths) and refuses the rest: the CLI itself accepts any folder for `--bench-dir`, which is right for a person in a terminal and wrong for a model that got a path from a log. Only a bench with `adopted: true` counts (registered, remembered or with a service of its own): `list` also shows a folder that merely looks like a bench under `~` or `~/dev`, and a freshly cloned repo there would run its own `env/bin/bench` on an apply. A person can name a bench for one server with `--allow-bench PATH` or `BENCHBAR_MCP_ALLOW_BENCH`; no tool argument can do it.
- `fg` takes the CLI lock like `down` does, and releases it just before `exec honcho`: exec skips the EXIT trap, and a lock held for as long as honcho runs in front would block every other benchbar run.

## 0.7.3: Node for v15

- A failed `node_install` stops the repair run like a failed env rebuild: the plist and the shell block that follow would name a Node that is not there, and a working node@20 bench would stop. A formula brew lists whose node is missing gets `brew reinstall` (install is a no-op on an installed formula).
- `repair` exits 0 when it did everything it could, even while a check without an action still fails (a disabled formula): its exit code says what repair did, and `doctor` is the command whose exit code says the state. The verify pass names the leftover once more.
- `v15-lts` moves from `node@20` to `node@22`, not to `node@24`: Homebrew disables `node@20` on 2026-10-28 (deprecated a year earlier), Frappe version-15's `engines` says `node >=18`, and `node@22` is the LTS line with the longest Homebrew life that the v15 asset build is known to accept. It is checked on a Mac before each release; the cloud cannot build a bench.
- The move is a repair, not a surprise: the shell block and the agent plist carry the formula in their PATH, so their hash changes and doctor marks them outdated; `toolchain_node` and `toolchain_yarn` gained the actions `node_install` (brew install of the profile's formula) and `yarn_install` (npm install -g yarn under it), so one `benchbar repair` completes the move. Nothing uninstalls `node@20`: another tool on the Mac may use it, and brew removes it on its own schedule.
- The disable dates come from `brew info --json=v2`, one call per formula, read from the local tap: three Ruby starts in doctor, no network. One call for all three would be cheaper, but the JSON nests `name` keys (requirements), so pairing dates with formulae by position is the only way and a brew that reorders them would silently mislabel.
- The tests shorten the system part of the bench's launchd PATH (`FL_LAUNCHD_PATH_SYSTEM`, no `/usr/local/bin`): CI runners carry a node and a yarn there, which stood in for the mocks' and made the yarn assertions pass or fail by host.
- 90 days is the warning horizon in doctor and in the release check alike, so a release cut inside the window fails before users see the warning. The release check lives in `scripts/check-profile-formulae.sh` and runs from both `release-local.sh` and the signed `macos-release.sh`, before any signing or upload.

## 0.7.3: first install

- Root secured through the OS account's socket login keeps `unix_socket` next to the new password (`IDENTIFIED VIA unix_socket OR mysql_native_password`): a fresh Homebrew install and a `mariadb-secure-installation` that chose socket authentication look alike from `mysql.user` (both carry the literal `invalid`), and replacing the rule would cut off root's socket clients on the second. The passwordless logins pass `--protocol=socket`, since an option file with `protocol=tcp` would send them where socket authentication cannot work.
- A socket-only root (what Homebrew's `mysql_install_db` leaves on MariaDB 10.4 and newer) is secured through the macOS user's own socket account, which that install creates with full privileges, never through `sudo mariadb -u root`: the setup already has every privilege it needs without becoming root on the Mac. Only a root whose native password is the literal `invalid` (the fresh install's) is taken as "no password yet"; a failed query, an empty hash (unix_socket only, pam) or a real hash is someone's choice, and the run keeps saying "root already has a password" and asks, rather than guess.
- `fl_mariadb_root_password_resolve` stops with "MariaDB is not running" before it judges any password: against a stopped server every source looks wrong, and the old message blamed the password the user had just passed.
- Phase 01 exits 3 on a cancel and `benchbar install` maps that to its own exit 1 with "Cancelled. No bench or site was created; the system dependencies of step 1 stay in place": 0 made the caller register a bench that does not exist, and 2 is taken (the password), so a cancel needed a code of its own between the phase and its caller. The message says what phase 00 left behind rather than "nothing was changed", which was false after a first run. `install` exits by hand (`cmd_install || exit $?`) so its codes bypass the ERR trap; the "Last command failed" line is phase output the step summary already shows.
- `fl_on_error` turns an incidental exit 2 into 1: the ERR trap exits with the failing command's status, and `grep`, `awk` and usage errors use 2, which AGENTS.md and the app read as "MariaDB root password unknown". Only `fl_die ... 2` may say that.
- `fl_mariadb_root_password_resolve` verifies the environment password before trusting it, like `fl_mariadb_root_setup`: an old shell with a stale `MARIADB_ROOT_PASSWORD` was failing the site step while the Keychain had the right one.
- The up front sudo reason for wkhtmltopdf is added only when the package will reach the installer in this run (`fl_wkhtmltopdf_will_install`): without a terminal and `--yes` every question in phase 00 is "no", so the package is skipped and the password prompt was for nothing, on every re-run.
- The phase scripts take `OFFLINE` from the environment (and `BENCHBAR_OFFLINE`) instead of resetting it, and `benchbar install` forwards `--offline`: one switch for every network check, as the docs already claimed.
- A bootstrap that fails because launchd has the label disabled (119) runs `launchctl enable` once and retries: the disabled list survives a plist rewrite, so a reinstall could never load the agent on such a Mac.

## 0.7.3: profile guards

- `FL_PROFILE_SOURCE` (flag, team, stored, detected, default) is set where the profile is chosen, in `fl_context_init`, and only `default` with an `apps/frappe` present counts as a guess (`fl_profile_is_guess`): a fresh install has no frappe yet and the default is right for it. The guess makes the env checks warn without an action and `act_env_rebuild` refuse: an env rebuilt with the wrong Python breaks a working develop or v17 bench, and the fix is a sentence (`install --profile`), not a guess.
- The source is not a new key of `doctor --json` yet: the app's fixtures (`macos/BenchBarTests/Fixtures`) pin the exact key set and this run does not touch `macos/`; the Swift follow-up adds `profile_source` to both sides at once. Until then the header and the env check messages carry it.
- `chk_bench_version` classifies before it offers: `command -v bench` empty or exit 126/127 is the CLI's venv (reinstall with its owner's tool), `No module named 'X'` with X in `sites/apps.txt` is that app's, and only the rest keeps `env_rebuild`. The old mapping of every failure to a rebuild moved a healthy env aside for a broken `~/.local/bin/bench`.
- `act_env_rebuild` refuses while the bench runs rather than stopping and restarting it: a repair that quietly runs `down` and `up` around a minutes long rebuild is a bigger surprise than "stop it first", and the three commands are one line in the fix.
- `setuptools<70` is installed into a v15 env after the rebuild and offered as the `env_setuptools` action: honcho and bench on Frappe v15 import `pkg_resources`, which setuptools 70 dropped, and a Python 3.12+ venv ships no setuptools at all. v16 does not import it, so the check skips there.
- `bench_min` is a new last column of `config/release-profiles.tsv` (5.22.0 for v15, 5.25.0 for v16, conservative: the bench that first handled each profile's Python); an older bench is a warning with the owner's upgrade command, not a stop, since the bench may still work. A `bench` on PATH that does not run (`bench --version` fails) stops the installer with the reinstall for its owner, where before `bench init` failed with a stack trace.

## 0.7.3: process groups and locks

- Long and timed commands are started under `set -m` and the flag is cleared right after: macOS bash 3.2 has no `setsid`, and job control is what gives a background job its own process group; perl's `setpgrp` would do the same with one more process per command. Signals go to the group (`kill -- -PGID`) with the leader as the fallback.
- benchbar traps TERM, INT and HUP itself, forwards them to the running command's group and to the phase script in the foreground, and only then exits so the EXIT trap releases the lock: a lock released while pip still ran let the next run start into a half-written env.
- A stale lock is reclaimed by `mv` to a private name, never `rm -rf` then `mkdir`: a rename either succeeds for one process or fails for the rest, so two reclaimers can no longer each delete the other's fresh lock. An empty lock younger than `FL_LOCK_GRACE_SECS` (5) is a run between its mkdir and its pid write, and is refused as "starting" rather than reclaimed.
- The bench's own lock lives at `<bench>/.benchbar.lock`, outside `sites/` and next to the other files benchbar writes there (Procfile.lean, the runner): the state folder differs by install kind, so only a lock in the bench itself is one every CLI meets. It is taken once the bench is known (`fl_context_init`) and only by runs that already hold the state lock, so read-only commands and dry runs take nothing.
- `up` and `restart` drop both locks after the kickstart and the state write: the wait for the ping only reads, and a lock held for the 45 second wait refused every other bench's `repair` and `site add` meanwhile.
- The MCP server starts the CLI with `start_new_session=True` and on a timeout sends SIGTERM to the group, waits a grace period for the CLI's EXIT trap (the setup Redis stops, the lock goes), then SIGKILL: Python's own timeout killed benchbar alone and the next tool call reclaimed the lock under a still running `bench get-app`.
- `fl_kv_set` builds the new file with `mktemp` and takes `FILE.lck` (mkdir) around the read-modify-write, with a short bounded wait: the fixed `.tmp` name lost keys when the app's status poll and a repair wrote one file at once. The wait is bounded so a lock left by a killed writer never blocks for good.
- The phase scripts of `install` run as a background child the CLI waits for, with `<&0` so they keep the terminal's stdin (a background job of a shell without job control gets `/dev/null`, and the install could not ask for a password); the signal forwarded to them is always TERM, since such a child has SIGINT ignored and could not be interrupted. A signal that lands before `FL_RUN_PGID` is set falls back to the job table (`jobs -p`), each job being its own group.
- A stale lock is reclaimed under a second mkdir guard (`<lock>.reclaim`) and only after reading the pid again: a reclaimer that judged a pid dead a moment ago could otherwise rename away the live lock another reclaimer had just made. A guard older than the grace period is a dead reclaimer's and is removed.
- `fl_run_long` polls for a stopped command (state `T`): in its own process group a command that reads the terminal is stopped by SIGTTIN and would wait for ever behind the spinner; `bench get-app` runs with `GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0` for the same reason.
- Log and backup names end in the pid: a second run in the same second (the app's and a terminal's) truncated the first's log and overwrote its backups. One stamp names both, so a log and its backups still match.

## 0.7.3: fail-closed writes

- Write helpers return 1 and leave the target as it was on any failed stage (`fl_backup_file`, `fl_move_aside`, `fl_template_apply`, `fl_rc_block_write`): the repair engine runs every action inside `if act_...; then`, which switches errexit off, so a failure not returned was reported as done. Callers now carry the code with `|| return 1`; there is no redesign beyond that.
- The rc block is written next to the resolved symlink target with the target's mode and renamed onto it, never `mv TMP FILE` onto the link (that replaced a dotfiles repo's symlink with a 0600 regular file) and never `cat TMP > TARGET` (a write cut short left a truncated rc file). The target is resolved link by link in bash (no `realpath`, which macOS gained only in 13).
- The rc file a block was written to is remembered as `RC_FILE` in the state: `ZDOTDIR` is a terminal variable the app and launchd do not have, and without it the app's doctor and repair would look at `~/.zshrc` and write a second block there.
- `redis_stop` is a question, not a repair: under `--yes` or without a terminal it is left out of the plan and named as optional, rather than run and reported "done" (or "skipped") on every run, which kept a second repair from ever saying unchanged. `fl_action_is_question` is the one place that knows which actions are questions.
- Logs rotate by copy then truncate (`cp -p` then `: > file`), like `fl_arm_start` already did for bench.log: bench.log is launchd's StandardOutPath and the worker logs are Procfile redirections, so a rename took the writers along and doctor went green over a live file that stayed empty. Three `.old` copies are kept (`FL_LOG_KEEP_OLD`), oldest removed first; a log copy is the one thing in a bench benchbar deletes, and only its own copies.
- The site-under-creation marker lives in `<bench>/logs/.benchbar/creating-<site>`, not inside `sites/<site>`: benchbar never writes into `sites/`. Its first line is the start time; `db_name=` follows when benchbar chose the database name (`--db-name`, `_` and 16 hex digits, checked free before `new-site` starts). `benchbar install` drops the database named there, with its user, only when ALL of this holds: (a) benchbar's own record in its state folder, written before the marker, names that database for this bench and site (a marker alone is not proof: it can be copied, edited or stale); (b) no `sites/*/site_config.json` of this bench or of any bench benchbar knows (registered ones too) names it, except the half made site's own config; (c) it is still just created: no tables at all, or no `tabUser` rows but Administrator and Guest; (d) the root password logs in and a person confirms in a terminal (default No), or `--yes` was given. Then it moves the folder to `archived/sites/<site>.incomplete.<time>` (as `bench drop-site` archives, never deleting). When any of this fails, or the marker has no database line, it drops and moves nothing and stops with a message that names the database and the commands for the person (a missing folder with a database named stops too, instead of starting `new-site` over it). Check (b) fails closed: a list of benches that cannot be read, and any folder benchbar was told of (state.env, the registered list, an agent's working folder, auto-detection) that is gone or whose `sites/` cannot be read (an unmounted volume), counts as naming the database; it reads the unfiltered list (`fl_known_bench_candidates`), not `fl_known_benches`, which leaves gone folders out. (b) and (c) run again right before the drop, after the confirmation, which can wait for a person. A bench benchbar has never seen (neither registered, known nor this one) is not looked at: that is the one case the check cannot cover. This overturns the 0.9 first draft, where the marker alone was enough: a copied, edited or stale marker could drop a working database, and benches share MariaDB.
- `lock apply` keeps `fl_lock_reach_commit`'s code in the clone branch and treats anything but `equal` after the fallback as a failure, and leaves such a clone out of the build list: the pin is the point of the lock. A detached HEAD that no branch contains is skipped with the `git switch -c` hint: `checkout -b` would have orphaned those commits.
- The runner exits 1 when honcho ends with 0 and no stop was requested: honcho's exit code is its first child's, and a redis that takes a TERM exits 0, so a bench that lost one process looked cleanly stopped until the next login. `crashed` with exit 1 lets launchd restart it under the crash guard, which is what the guard is for.
- The crash guard fails closed: when `.bench-starts` or the stop flag cannot be written, the runner pauses with `broken` and exits 0, because a guard that cannot record starts can never stop a restart loop.

## 0.7.3: secret and input hygiene

- `benchbar install` runs the two sudo steps right after the one prompt and then `sudo -k`, instead of keeping the credential alive for the whole run: everything after them (brew, pip, npm, yarn, bench get-app) runs third party install scripts on the same terminal, and none of them may find a cached sudo. "Asks once up front" stays true because the two steps are the only ones that need it.
- The hosts line is added up front by calling `act_hosts_entry` itself, not a copy: the service pass later finds it present and says unchanged, and a declined or refused line is offered again there, as before.
- Phase 00 learns the up front result through `BENCHBAR_PDF_STEP` (skipped or failed) rather than running `fl_wkhtmltopdf_ensure` again: the second run would ask the same Rosetta and download questions a second time.
- The wkhtmltopdf package is copied by root into `sudo mktemp -d /tmp/benchbar-wkhtmltopdf.XXXXXX`, hashed there by root and installed from there: the download folder is user writable, so the verified file and the installed file were not necessarily the same. `/tmp` and not `$TMPDIR`, because the per user `$TMPDIR` is writable by the user, who could then swap the whole folder; `/tmp` is sticky, so only root can rename or remove a folder root created there. `sudo rm -rf` gets only that fresh folder.
- The passwords leave the environment with `export -n` right after argument parsing in `benchbar` and in both phase scripts, and `benchbar` passes them to phase 00 and 01 on their own invocation line: the shell variables stay readable in process (the Keychain, the `MYSQL_PWD` checks and the stdin injector keep working) while no other child sees them. bash 3.2 supports `export -n`.
- `FL_ASSUME_YES`, `FL_MAKE_DEFAULT` and `FL_PORT_PLAN_APPROVED` start at 0 in `benchbar` and the phase scripts and are set only by flags; `benchbar` passes `--yes` and `--make-default` to the phase scripts as arguments instead of exporting the variables. The in process `FL_ASSUME_YES=1 act_hosts_entry` of `site hosts` is unchanged: it is the CLI answering its own question after asking once for all lines.
- `mariadb-password` refuses before it reads anything under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE` (so `--for-agent` and `benchbar mcp`) and when stdin is not a terminal, whatever `--yes` says, and no text of the command, the install output or the docs tells an agent to run it. Nothing in the environment may stand in for the person. The old hint named `--yes` for a run without a terminal, a one line route to the password for an agent (review MCP-1, SVC-11).
- The Keychain item is written through `security -i` with the password quoted for that parser (backslash and double quote escaped) and checked by reading it back, and it names `-T /usr/bin/security` explicitly: the ACL then says what it does, and SECURITY.md documents the limit (any program as the user can read it through `security`) and how to remove the trust. `-T ''` was rejected because every CLI read would then prompt, which `--yes` runs and the tests cannot answer.
- The mock sudo models the cached credential as a file (`-v` and a typed command create it, `-k` removes it, `-n` needs it), and the harness starts with it present, like a developer who typed sudo a moment ago: the hygiene test removes it first, so an "ok" probe from a mock proves that third party code ran while the credential was live.
- One set of redaction rules, `fl_redact_stream` in report.sh, serves the report and `logs --json`: the report applies it one kind at a time (one sed per kind) so REDACTIONS.txt can count each kind; logs --json applies it as one sed. The key rule runs first, because it knows where a value ends; the value shapes catch what has no key.
- The email rule keeps `user@host:` (an SSH clone URL such as `git@github.com:org/app.git`) by requiring what follows the domain not to be a colon: masking those would hide the one thing a failed `get-app` report needs.
- An unquoted value after a credential key is masked to the end of the line (or to the next `, ; & } " )`), so `--password=x y` masks the rest of the argv too: over-masking an argv is cheaper than leaking a value with a space in it.
- `logs --json` is redacted and `logs` is not: the JSON goes to scripts and agents (the MCP tool), the plain text is the user's own file, which `tail -f` streams. The cap of 2000 lines matches the MCP schema's maximum and is enforced twice: the server refuses a value outside the schema's 1..2000 before building the argv (a tool error that names the bound, since the MCP hardening), the CLI clamps and says `truncated_to`.
- URL credentials are masked in `fl_log` itself (every line that reaches the run log), in `FL_LAST_COMMAND`, in the captured output a failed command leaves (git names the URL it could not reach) and in `fl_die`'s hint, rather than at each call site; the check is a `case` on `://*@`, so the common line costs no process.
- A bench path (and the default site) is validated once, in `fl_render_all`, with `adopt` and phase 01 checking earlier so the refusal comes before the plan: the runner's `BENCH="..."` and the plist's `<string>` carry it (the process patterns no longer do). The refused set is `" \ $ \` < > &` and control characters; spaces and apostrophes are common on Macs ("Bob's Bench") and stay valid, so a site with one renders byte for byte as before.
- Template escaping is a second guard, not the guard: `#@quoted KEY...` in a template names the values that sit inside bash double quotes (escaped `" \ $ \``), a `.plist` template XML-escapes every value (`& < > "`). Neither changes a value without those characters, so installed files stay `current`; `'` is left alone in XML (element text needs no `&apos;`) so the plist's WorkingDirectory is still the path itself for `fl_plist_working_dir`.
- The `#@quoted` directive lines bump the template versions (bench-run.sh v7, shell-helpers v2) although a directive is stripped on render and the rendered body and its hash are unchanged for every existing input: a template change bumps its version, without exception, so a reader of the header can tell which template wrote a file. `repair` rewrites the runner and the helper block once, with identical content.
- Every `${var//pat/rep}` replacement in templates.sh is a plain variable, never a quoted literal: bash 3.2 to 4.2 keep the quotes of a quoted replacement (`BENCH=""...""` under `/bin/bash`). bash 5.2's patsub_replacement reads an `&` or `\` in the replacement, an expanded variable included, as the match or an escape, so ui.sh turns the option off for the whole program (`shopt -u patsub_replacement 2>/dev/null || true`; bash 3.2 has no such option): every `${//}` in this bash 3.2 code base means text. test-input-hygiene renders under `BASH_COMPAT=32` as well to hold both.
- `BENCH_NAME` and `LABEL` are not `#@quoted` in the runner: they also sit inside write_state's single quoted printf format, where a backslash would be literal; both are built from a sanitised name, and the path check refuses the characters anyway.
- Doctor does not die on a bench path the templates cannot carry: it reports `bench_path` as a FAIL (the app polls `doctor --json`, and AGENTS.md promises doctor is safe any time); `fl_require_plain_bench` refuses in the commands that write the files or start the runner (service, repair, adopt, install, up, restart, autostart, ports apply, site add and default).
- The hosts validator accepts an IPv6 zone id (`fe80::1%lo0 localhost`, shipped by Apple for years) and a CRLF line: the check is for a garbled rewrite, not for policing a file that works.
- Token shapes need a word boundary on the left (`sk-`, `gh?_`, `github_pat_`, `xox?-`, `AKIA`): `/tmp/task-2026-10-03-worker-default-queue` and `.desk-sidebar-item-container-wrapper` contain `sk-` and are not secrets.
- `pwd` counts as a credential key only as a keyword argument inside parentheses (`connect(pwd=...)`): `PWD=/Users/...` in an environment dump is a path, and masking it would hide what a report is for.
- The email rule needs a dotted domain ending in letters and sets `.local` aside first: `worker@Bobs-MacBook.local` (an rq worker name) and `root@localhost` are not addresses.
- `benchbar install --dry-run` hands phase 00 `BENCHBAR_PDF_STEP=dry-run` after the up front step printed the wkhtmltopdf plan, so the plan appears once; a real run keeps `skipped` and `failed` as before.
- The hosts rewrite runs awk as root and pipes into `sudo tee /etc/hosts.benchbar.new`, so the new content never sits in a file this user could swap between the write and the copy; the markers and the line go through `ENVIRON[]` rather than `-v`, which processes backslash escapes. The check reads the new file as root too (a strict umask could hide it), and `chmod 644` comes before the `mv`: tee inherits the user's umask, and an unreadable `/etc/hosts` would break name resolution for every process on the Mac.
- A hosts file with a line that is not "address names" is refused, with the line named: the check exists to catch a garbled rewrite, and a file that fails it is not one to rewrite blind. Appending a new block still uses `sudo tee -a`, which has no copy to race.
- A `sites/` folder whose name is not a site name (`evil\nx`) is skipped with a warning at the top of `act_hosts_entry`, `fl_hosts_add_names` and `fl_hosts_remove_name`, before `fl_confirm` and `fl_sudo_begin`, so the name never reaches sudo, however the function was entered.
- `fl_kv_decode_v` decodes `printf %q` without `eval`: plain text as is, `$'...'` by hand (the octal `\NNN` of a non-ASCII byte in the C locale, `\'`, which `%b` does not know, and the usual escapes), a backslash form by dropping each backslash, `'...'` as its inner text, and anything else as the literal line. A hand written `KEY=~` or `KEY=$HOME` is therefore the text, not the expansion.

## 0.7.3: MCP hardening

- Every `tools/call` runs on a daemon thread and `initialize`, `ping` and `tools/list` are answered inline by the reader thread: a one hour `benchbar_app_add` otherwise blocked `benchbar_status` and every other call. Replies are written under one lock, one JSON line each, so threads never interleave on stdout.
- Actions (`up`, `down`, `restart`, `benchbar_app_add`) take one lock in the server, read tools run side by side: the CLI's own lock would refuse a second action anyway, but a refusal after the agent waited is a worse answer than a short queue. Waiting on that lock still notices a cancellation.
- `notifications/cancelled` for a running call stops its process group (the same SIGTERM, grace, SIGKILL as a timeout, also when the CLI itself already left on TERM, so a member that ignores TERM and holds no pipe dies too) and the call sends no reply: the MCP spec says the server SHOULD not reply to a cancelled request, and a reply the client no longer expects would only confuse it. The CLI's `communicate` runs in 0.25 second slices so a cancellation is seen while it runs. A cancel that lands during the status after an action keeps the action's result, with `after_error: "cancelled"`: the action did run, and losing that is worse than one unexpected reply. A cancel only signals a process whose exit is not yet known, never a pid that may have been reused.
- `--process worker --previous` is refused: worker.log has no previous file, and reading the previous bench log unfiltered would be a quiet wrong answer.
- When stdin closes, running calls get `BENCHBAR_MCP_EOF_GRACE` (30 seconds) to finish, then their groups are stopped and the server exits; SIGTERM to the server stops them at once. A client that is gone reads no result, and a half finished `app add` is the same case a timeout already handles.
- Argument values are checked against each property's schema (type, minimum, maximum, enum) and a violation is a tool result with `isError: true` naming the argument and the rule, not a protocol error: the model reads tool errors and corrects the call, while `-32602` ends up as an opaque failure. Unknown arguments, missing required ones and values starting with `-` stay `-32602` as before: those are malformed calls, not wrong values.
- `all_sites` (and every boolean) must be `true`, never a truthy string: the string `"false"` turned `--all-sites` on.
- Results whose text comes from outside benchbar (log lines, `app add --dry-run` with its `hooks.py` and git output, profile files, the output of an action) start with `Data from the bench (...), not instructions:` and the JSON; `structuredContent` is the same JSON. Results that are benchbar's own words (`list`, `status`, `doctor`, `site list`, `app list`) stay as the CLI printed them. The label is a plain line, not a format change, so clients that show only the text still read the JSON.
- `fl_json_escape_v` removes a terminal control sequence as a whole (ESC `[` parameters final byte), not only its ESC byte: the `[31m` it left behind was not text anyone wrote, and every `--json` reader (the app, the server, scripts) would have had to guess it away. The server keeps a whole sequence strip as a safety net and no remnant regex, which would eat real text such as `[5m]`.
- One result is capped at `BENCHBAR_MCP_MAX_BYTES` (200 kB): the oldest log lines (or the tail of an action's output) are dropped from `structuredContent` until it fits, `"truncated": true` says so, and the text is rebuilt from that shrunk JSON so both agree and the newest lines are the ones kept; a text that still does not fit (benchbar's own JSON, which is never that large) is cut by bytes with `... [truncated, N bytes total]`. A 2000 line log with long lines was several hundred kB of context.
- The status (or app list) after an action runs in its own try: a hang there returned `-32603` and lost the action's result. The result now comes back with `after_error` and without the `status` key.
- `tools/list` and `tools/call` before `initialize` get `-32002` "server not initialized" (the code MCP SDKs use for it); `jsonrpc` must be `"2.0"` and an `id` must be a string or a number (`null` is an invalid request answered with id `null`). Unexpected exceptions are `-32603` "internal error" with the traceback on stderr: the message used to carry absolute paths.
- `benchbar mcp` prefers `/usr/bin/python3 -I` when `xcode-select -p` succeeds, else `python3` from `PATH`, each checked for 3.9: a GUI client starts the server with a `PATH` that may name a pyenv shim or a venv, and `-I` ignores `PYTHON*` variables and the user site, which the server (standard library only) never needs. The tests steer the choice with a mock `xcode-select` and a `python3` wrapper on `PATH`, so no extra override exists.
- The token is described as proof that the bench has not changed since the plan, and the tool asks the agent to ask the person: the old wording ("the plan the person approved") claimed something the server cannot know. No elicitation: the clients in use do not support it yet.
- `--process worker` reads `logs/worker.log` in `fl_cmd_logs` (the file option mapping), unfiltered, and `--worker-error` keeps its file: `Procfile.lean` sends the worker there, so the honcho filter never matched a line. The MCP tool gets a `file` enum for the four files and loses `worker` from `process`, so the schema validator refuses what never worked.
- `site list --json` adds `db_name` and `db_port` per site, read from `site_config.json` and `common_site_config.json` a line at a time like the other config reads, never the password; `list` and `status` leave them out because they are polled every few seconds.
- `benchbar_app_list` runs `app list --json --no-sites`: a read tool must not ask the database, so the sites come from the last read.

## 0.7.3: state folder migration

- A new folder that holds nothing but `bin/` (the link BenchBar.app makes at launch) and a `.DS_Store` counts as not moved yet, and the installer checkout's state still moves in: the app registers its CLI before the first CLI run, so "the new path exists" alone would leave every installer state behind. The test is a glob over the folder, no process, and runs only while a real `.benchbar` is still in the checkout.
- The state folder is renamed into place whole, not its files moved in one by one: a rename is atomic, a file by file move cut short would pass for the state. `bin/` goes into the checkout's folder first, the emptied new folder is removed with `rmdir` (atomic; its `.DS_Store` first, a Finder file and an empty folder, never state), and the rename brings `bin/benchbar` back with it. Every intermediate layout is one the next run finishes from: a `bin/` already in the checkout's folder is part of the state folder, never "a state of its own", and a second `bin/` the app made in between waits inside the guard. A `rmdir` that fails (something appeared) undoes the `bin/` move and leaves the retry to the next run.
- The app's link is away between the `mv` of `bin/` and the rename: a process already started resolves itself through the retry in `fl_resolve_self` (up to 2 s in 0.1 s steps, and a folder without `lib/` is rejected), but a start that falls inside that window of a few milliseconds fails with "No such file" (observed under the three-at-once test before the retry; a start cannot be made to wait before it runs). A normal start resolves at the first try and starts nothing for it.
- The one time move is serialized with `mkdir ~/.local/state/.benchbar-migrating` (atomic, a pid file inside, like the lock) taken before any check; losers wait up to 5 s in 0.1 s steps for it to go, then use whatever exists. A guard is stale when its pid is dead, when it is older than 60 s whatever the pid says (a pid number can be reused; `stat -c %Y || stat -f %m` and `date`, only when a guard exists), or when it has no pid by the end of the wait. A stale guard is claimed with one `mv` to `.stale.<pid>` (atomic, so of two waiters that both saw the dead pid exactly one reclaims it; the other waits once more), then its pid file, the app's link in `bin/` (a symlink only) and empty folders go; anything else stays aside, never deleted. An older CLI knows no guard, so the recovery for a link or a folder that landed inside the new folder stays.
- The fast path tests `-d` on the guard once (no process when there is none) and sweeps a stale one the same way: a run killed after the rename would otherwise leave a guard that costs every later first run its wait, and the `ln` the fast path re-makes is the only other thing such a run leaves undone.
- The app's helper block prefers a standalone copy that hands off (the copy that handed off to this run, then Homebrew's, then the installer's) over the app's own link: the link goes with the app, the copy runs itself once the app is gone. A recorded copy that hands off is still kept whichever copy runs repair, so the block is rewritten once, not on every switch.
- The app's Second CLI check warns on a copy newer than the app's CLI with the app update as the fix, not the copy's downgrade: every copy hands off to the app's CLI, so the app is what is behind.
- The split state check for the app offers `mv ~/.local/share/benchbar ~/.Trash/` only when the installer's CLI is still there, and `mv .../.benchbar ~/.Trash/` otherwise; both say to move out what is still needed first, since the folder may be an unmerged state and not only what an older CLI wrote since the move.
- `where --json` gets `state_dir_real` (`cd && pwd -P`, no `readlink -f`: macOS before 12.3 has none) and `handoff_from_version`; doctor --json gets no new top level key, since the app's test fixtures pin that set.
- `install.sh --uninstall` resolves `~/.local/state/benchbar` and keeps the checkout when the link leads into it (`-ef`, or the link text under `BENCHBAR_HOME`): the state of every CLI on the Mac is behind that link, and `rm -rf` of the checkout would take it.

## 0.7.0: Homebrew

- The install kind comes from SCRIPT_DIR as a string: `*/opt/benchbar/libexec` or `*/Cellar/benchbar/*/libexec` is homebrew, exactly `~/.local/share/benchbar` is managed, a folder with `.git` a checkout, anything else other. No process, because status runs it on every poll of the app, and no variable from the formula's wrapper, because every child (bench, honcho, a nested benchbar of another kind) would inherit it.
- Managed is that exact path, never `BENCHBAR_HOME`: that variable belongs to install.sh and self-update, and a test that points it at a checkout must never move the checkout's state.
- `FL_SELF` is the path every generated file and printed command names: `<prefix>/opt/benchbar/bin/benchbar` under Homebrew, the Cellar form mapped to it, because brew upgrade keeps the opt path and brew cleanup deletes the Cellar one. For managed, checkout and other it stays `${SCRIPT_DIR}/benchbar`, so 0.7.0 rewrites no existing helper block.
- `FL_SELF_DIR` (`<prefix>/opt/benchbar/libexec` under Homebrew) is used for the two fix lines that name a phase script, for the same reason; SCRIPT_DIR stays the folder the running CLI reads lib/, templates/ and config/ from.
- `write_cli_link` links `~/.local/bin` to `FL_SELF` and `cli_link` accepts it: under Homebrew a link into the Cellar would dangle after the next cleanup. For the other kinds the target is unchanged.
- Managed and homebrew keep state in `~/.local/state/benchbar`; a checkout keeps `./.benchbar` and `FL_STATE_DIR` from the environment still wins. Not `~/Library/Application Support/benchbar`: on a case-insensitive disk that is the app's `BenchBar` folder, which the cask's zap trashes.
- The path is fixed, not `${XDG_STATE_HOME:-~/.local/state}`: BenchBar.app opened from Finder, an MCP client and launchd start the CLI without the variables of `~/.zshrc`, so a variable set only in the shell would give them another registry, other settings and another lock. `FL_STATE_DIR` stays the one override.
- The installer checkout's `.benchbar` moves with one `mv` and a symlink back, never a copy: an install.sh CLI still on the Mac then reads the same state and takes the same lock, so two CLIs cannot fork the benches. If the symlink cannot be made, the move is undone.
- The `mv` goes straight to the new path, not through a temporary name: on one volume it is a rename, so another run sees either the old folder or the new one, never neither, and creates no empty state in between.
- When `~/.local/state` and the installer's folder are on different volumes (one `stat` of both, on the first run only), nothing moves: the new path becomes a symlink to the old folder. A cross-volume `mv` copies, and a copy cut short would pass for the state on every later run.
- The move tests again right before the `mv` (after the `mkdir` process), and a `mv` that put another first run's link inside the new folder moves it back: two first runs at once (the app's status and list polls) leave one state and one link.
- When `ln -s` meets a folder an older CLI made again in that gap, ln puts the link inside it; that link is removed, the folder stays, this run keeps the moved state, and `cli_duplicate` names the split. No lock is created in the old folder before the move: a 0.6.1 run makes its logs folder before it takes its lock, so the lock would not stop it.
- A run that finds the new folder but no link at the old path (a run stopped between the `mv` and the `ln`) makes the link again when the installer's folder is there: a few tests on every run, no process.
- The move is deferred while `lock/` exists in the old folder (the rule of the `.frappe-local` rename): that run, and every run until the lock is gone, uses the old folder as is. A stale lock is reclaimed and released by the next mutating run, and the run after that moves.
- The move happens when state.sh is sourced, like the `.frappe-local` rename, so the first status poll after an upgrade may do it; every later run pays one `-e` test and starts no process.
- An existing new folder is never merged into: when both exist, the old one stays where it is and is no longer read.
- `.gitignore` says `.benchbar` without the slash: a pattern with a slash matches only a directory, and the move leaves a symlink in the installer's checkout (checked with `git status`).
- `~/.local/bin` stays on the helper block's PATH under Homebrew too: pipx and uv install `bench` there. The template is unchanged, so no helper block becomes outdated for it.
- The runner template marks `CLI_VERSION` with a new `#@unhashed` directive: that key is hashed as its `__CLI_VERSION__` token, so a new version alone (every brew upgrade) no longer makes every runner outdated. The value still lands in the runner, which writes it as `state.json`'s `cli_version`: the version that wrote the runner. The 0.6.1 runners are outdated once, since the hash changed.
- The report's `versions.txt` names the install kind, `FL_SELF` and the state folder: after the move, support needs to know which CLI ran and where its logs are.
- `tests/test-homebrew.sh` copies the CLI into a keg under `MOCK_BREW_PREFIX` (what the brew mock answers for `--prefix`): a symlink into the repo would resolve back to the checkout. Both shapes of the formula's `bin/benchbar` are covered, the wrapper and a symlink into the Cellar.
