- The sudo keepalive owns no stdio and sleeps in five second slices: with the caller's stdout inherited, every `$(...)` capture of a run waited for its `sleep 50`, and on macOS never returned. The tests capture output, so this showed up only in CI.
- The `git` mock's pass through to the real git picks the first `git` on PATH outside `tests/mocks` and refuses to exec itself: on macOS `/bin/bash` 3.2, `command -v -p git` still returned the mock, which then exec'd itself in a loop at full CPU. This was the hang behind three timed out macOS CLI jobs; the Linux job never saw it. The test runner now kills a test after ten minutes and prints its process tree, which is how this was found.

## Review findings on the pull request

- `fl_wkhtmltopdf_ensure` returns 2 for a deliberate skip (Rosetta or the package declined, no sudo) and 1 for an error; the repair action reports a skip as `skipped`, and the engine's verify pass ignores the optional actions `wkhtmltopdf_install` and `redis_stop`, so a bench install without PDFs exits 0.
- The report's assignment style masking takes a quoted value whole (`key="secret"`, `key='secret'`): the unquoted form stopped at the quote and left the secret in place.
- `install.sh --app-only` skips the Command Line Tools and Homebrew checks: the prebuilt app needs only `curl`, `shasum` and `ditto`; macOS and the architecture are still checked.
- The installer's PATH block is generated from the configured bin folder (`BENCHBAR_BIN_DIR`), written as `$HOME/...` when it lives under the home folder.
- A refused up-front `sudo -v` sets `FL_SUDO_REFUSED=1`, exported to the phase scripts, so an install asks for sudo exactly once whatever the answer and skips every sudo step with a message after a refusal.
- A hosts line that cannot be written because sudo was refused is a skipped step with the manual command printed, and `hosts_entry` joins the optional actions, so a run the user chose to keep sudo free still ends with exit 0 and warnings.
- The repair engine resets `FL_STEP_RESULT` before each action and reports it, so a skipped wkhtmltopdf or hosts step prints `skipped`, the documented step status, not `done`.
- The report's assignment style masking also treats `?` as a key boundary, so a credential in a URL query string (`?api_key=...&x=1`) is masked like any other.
- `release-local.sh` no longer re-signs the app: `macos-build.sh` already signed it ad hoc with the Hardened Runtime and the entitlements, and a plain `codesign --force -s -` dropped both. The script verifies the signature and the runtime flag instead.
- Phase 01 no longer requires `wkhtmltopdf`: a bench without it works, only PDF printing does not, and phase 00 already said so when the package was declined.
- The report's quoted value patterns accept backslash escaped characters inside the quotes, so `"api_key":"abc\"tail"` is masked whole.
- The utf8mb4 doctor check also requires the `!includedir` line in `my.cnf` (a missing `my.cnf` counts as missing). The live `character_set_server` query that 0.3.0 added was removed in 0.3.1: it read the root password from the Keychain, and doctor is read only and runs on a timer in the app, so it must never touch the Keychain. A test asserts that doctor makes no `security` call.
- `adopt --dry-run` never touched state (`fl_state_set` is a no-op under `FL_DRY_RUN`); a test now proves it.

- The report also masks Python repr mappings (`'password': 'x'`): worker logs print dicts that way.
- `adopt` runs the engine with `FL_ENGINE_SKIP_ACTIONS=honcho_install`: a missing honcho is reported with `pipx install honcho` or `benchbar repair` as the fix, and nothing is ever installed into the bench's `env/` by adopt.
- Phase 00 tells a failed wkhtmltopdf install (download, checksum, installer) apart from a deliberate skip: it prints FAILED and a manual step, but still exits 0, since PDFs are optional and the bench can be created.
- A generated MariaDB password is written to the Keychain before it is applied to the server; when the Keychain refuses (locked), MariaDB is left unchanged and the run stops with the fix, so no password ever exists only in a dying process.

- Phase 01 exits 2, the documented "root password unknown" code, when a fresh site needs the MariaDB password and no source has it; `benchbar install` reports it as a pending manual step like phase 00 does.
- wkhtmltopdf detection prefers the package binary at `/usr/local/bin/wkhtmltopdf` when it is the patched build, and warns with `brew uninstall wkhtmltopdf` when an unpatched build earlier on PATH would shadow it: Frappe finds the binary through PATH, and the launchd PATH puts Homebrew's bin before /usr/local/bin.
- A missing `my.cnf` counts as a missing `!includedir` in the utf8mb4 check: without it the drop-in folder is never read.

## Found on a real Mac (macOS 27, Apple Silicon)

- Verified read only on the real bench: `install.sh --dry-run` and `--dry-run --uninstall` write nothing; `doctor` and `report --print` run clean (no home path, username, hostname or site config value in the report); `adopt` on an already adopted bench is a no-op that only remembers the path. `release-local.sh` builds, passes the 114 Swift tests, and `shasum -a 256 -c dist/SHA256SUMS` passes; the DMG (UDZO, read-only, Applications symlink) mounts and the unpacked app carries a valid ad hoc signature. Every macOS flag the mocks assume was checked against the man pages: `security add-generic-password -U`, `arch -x86_64 prog`, `softwareupdate --install-rosetta --agree-to-license`, `installer -pkg X -target /`, `ditto -x -k` and `-c -k --keepParent`, `hdiutil create -format UDZO`, `sw_vers -productVersion` (the man page lists `--productVersion`; the single dash form still works and is what macOS 12 and older knew).
- `benchbar report` replaces every name the Mac goes by, not only `hostname`: on the test Mac `hostname` returned the router's name (`Mac.lan`) while the Bonjour name from `scutil --get LocalHostName` was the identifying one. The computer name (`scutil --get ComputerName`) is included too, and the sed patterns built from those names, `$HOME` and the username are escaped, since a computer name can hold brackets or a dot.
- `install.sh --uninstall --dry-run` ended with "BenchBar removed": the uninstall summary has its own dry-run branch now, and `quit_app` prints what it would do instead of hiding the line behind the redirected `osascript`.
- The Gatekeeper dialog for a quarantined ad hoc build reads "BenchBar" Not Opened, Apple could not verify it is free of malware, with Done and Move to Trash (Move to Bin in British English) where Move to Trash is the highlighted default. README says so. The System Settings steps were not clicked: Open Anyway changes a security setting, which the brief reserves for the user.
- `fl_password_generate` and the sudo keepalive were exercised on macOS `/bin/bash` 3.2: the generator returns 24 characters at once, also with SIGPIPE ignored, and 4 KB of urandom never yields fewer than about 900 usable characters; a captured `fl_sudo_begin` returns immediately and its keepalive exits within one five second slice of the parent leaving.

## CI speed

Measured on five PR runs (September 2026): 11 to 14 minutes wall clock, all of it the macOS CLI job (10.7 to 13.5 min; its test step 605 to 763s). Linux CLI took 7.5 to 8.8 min, the app job 1.4 to 1.7 min, the release bundle 1.2 to 1.8 min after it, v16 0.3 to 0.7 min, shellcheck 22 to 32s, `brew install` 2 to 4s (bottle cache hits), DerivedData restores from its restore key in about 5s.

- `tests/run-tests.sh` runs the test files in a bash 3.2 worker pool, `PARALLEL` at a time (default: the CPU count), output buffered per test and printed whole in list order, the ten minute deadline kept per test (polled every 0.2s instead of 1s), and every failure reported at the end instead of stopping at the first: the files were already independent (each builds its own HOME and mock state under mktemp; the real `ps` fallback only reads, the mocks only read from the checkout). Local, this Mac: 512s one at a time, 233s with 3, 113s with 10.
- The deadline's process dump lists only the stuck test's process tree: with tests side by side, the old `ps | grep tests/` would name every running test.
- The list is ordered longest first by measured time and a test file missing from it fails the run: the pool then never waits on a slow test started last, and a new file cannot be silently skipped.
- `SHARD=i/n` takes every nth test of that list: shards are balanced by construction (local, 3 workers each: 75s, 65s, 70s).
- The macOS CLI job is a matrix of shards, each running its share 3 at a time (macos-latest has 3 CPUs): the 11 minute test step should drop to about 2 minutes per shard. This line said three shards; it was four from the round 2 change on, and the matrix size now comes from the CI plan (see "CI tiering"). Four shards plus the app job are the five concurrent macOS jobs of the free plan.
- shellcheck runs as one more pool job in shard 1 (and on Linux), over the full file list the CI step had (`scripts/*.sh` and `install.sh` included): it overlaps the tests instead of adding 25s in front of them, and needs no extra runner.
- The separate v16 job is gone: `run-tests.sh` has run `test-profile-v16.sh` since 4d666ee, so it ran twice per PR.
- The release bundle is built in the app job (`release-local.sh --skip-tests` replaces the plain Release build step, then the Swift tests run on the warm DerivedData): its Release build is a superset of the old unsigned build (it also signs ad hoc and verifies), and it saves a second runner, Xcode selection, XcodeGen install, cache restore and a full Release build (about 1.5 min on the critical path), while still producing the bundle on every PR.
- The Linux job is gone (Akash, 0.5 pull request): BenchBar only runs on macOS, the macOS shards already run the suite on the `/bin/bash` 3.2 users have, and the Linux job was the slowest one left (4.5 minutes) while only ever finding GNU tool differences no user hits. The suite still aims to run on Linux for a Linux dev container, without a CI guarantee.
- `brew install` is skipped when the tool is already on the runner, and the Homebrew and DerivedData caches stay as they were: both already hit (restore key for DerivedData, which is expected when sources change).
- Estimate after: about 3 to 4 minutes wall clock per PR, bounded by the slowest shard. First run on the 0.5 pull request: shards 2m28s to 3m50s, the app job 1m50s.

## Batch port allocation

- Preserve usable allocations before choosing replacement blocks, so an early
  conflicting entry cannot displace another selected bench's valid address.
  Fixed claims take priority, then managed benches, then newcomers.
- Approval uses a fingerprint of the complete plan and relevant configuration
  and ownership state. Apply recomputes under the existing CLI lock, then checks
  listeners and running state again per entry. Foreign processes do not share
  that lock, so launch also checks; no unrelated process is killed for a port.
- Persist reservations in existing per-bench state after successful setup.
  Registration alone remains read-only with respect to bench configuration.
- Apply reuses the backed-up adoption engine. This is sequential execution, not
  an all-or-nothing transaction: report completed entries on a later failure.
- Ignore stale saved automatic reservations on read; fixed reservations remain pinned, and current configuration always participates without duplicate claims.
- CLI starts distinguish hard live-owner conflicts from stopped overlaps that can be confirmed for one-at-a-time operation; positively owned running benches keep idempotent `up`.
- Include the adoption dry-run service plan in the approval fingerprint; `ports setup` handles human preview and confirmation while token-based apply remains available for clients.
- Reject site/profile overrides for port planning and apply so the adopted service always matches the reviewed preview.

## 0.5.8: site backup and drop

- `site drop` wraps `bench drop-site` instead of dropping the database itself: bench's own backup with files comes first and stops the drop when it fails, and the folder is moved to `archived/sites/`, never deleted. benchbar only adds the refusals, the password on stdin and the hosts line.
- The typed confirmation is checked by the CLI (`--confirm-site` must equal the name), not only by the app, so no caller can drop a site by passing the name it already has; the app passes what the user typed.
- The default site cannot be dropped without `--new-default`, which runs `site default` first: dropping it would leave the runner pinging and `benchup` waiting for a site that is gone. The only site of a bench is never dropped.
- A backup "set" is the files sharing the timestamp prefix, read from the folder, not from bench's output: bench prints paths in formats that change between versions, and the folder also holds backups taken outside benchbar.
- The hosts line is removed only inside benchbar's marker block and only when no other known bench has a site with that name; a line outside the block is the user's and is only reported with the command to remove it.
- Without a terminal and a cached sudo credential the hosts step is not attempted and comes back as `manual_step`: the app never asks for a password, as with `site hosts`.
- `site backups` is read only and takes no lock, like `site list`, so the Sites tab can read it while another change runs.
- `uninstall-service` on a path that is not a bench falls back to the com.benchbar agents whose WorkingDirectory is that path, and touches nothing else there: the folder may be half deleted or reused, so only the agent is ours to remove.
- `dead_agents` looks at every com.benchbar agent, not only the current bench's: the bench whose runner is gone cannot run doctor any more, so another bench's doctor is the only place the warning can show.
- `benchbar console` and `db` exec bench (`bench --site S console|mariadb`) instead of running it as a child: the terminal, signals and exit code are bench's, and no benchbar lock or trap outlives the shell. `db` relies on bench reading the site's own credentials, so the root password is never involved.
- `doctor --fix-hints` lists failures before warnings and prints each fix once: several checks share `benchbar repair` as their fix, and an agent should run it once. Everything but the hints goes to stderr, so the output can be piped as it is.

## 0.6.0: update path

- `benchbar self-update` is the name, not `update`: `app update` and `profile update` exist and `bench update` is the command users fear, so the word alone would be ambiguous. It runs the one line installer instead of its own git pull, so the CLI, the app's Update Now and the release notes all run the same command; once a release is offered, the installer comes from that release's tag with `--version`, so the version confirmed is the one installed.
- `self-update` execs the installer pipeline: `install.sh` pulls the checkout the running script lives in, and nothing of benchbar should run after that.
- `install.sh --yes` on a Mac whose CLI checkout already exists is an update: it adopts or installs no bench and never starts the Homebrew installer (the one step that asks for a password). An interactive run still offers both.
- A CLI that is not `~/.local/share/benchbar` gets `--app-only`: a git checkout is the developer's own branch, and a pull the installer did not start could fail on local changes or move them. The same rule is in `lib/frappe-local/selfupdate.sh` and the app's `UpdatePlan`, tested on both sides.
- The release notes are built by `scripts/release-notes.sh` (tested) instead of inline workflow shell, with an Update section first: 0.5.x apps open the release page from Check for Updates, so the copy paste command has to be at the top of that page.

## 0.6.0: dependency freshness

- Doctor warns about the dependencies of focus apps, never about a focus app: the developer pulls the app they work on themselves, so a warning about it is noise, while the apps it needs go stale unnoticed.
- Focus is inferred from local reads only (a dirty tree, a branch other than the profile's or, without a policy, the remote's default, a commit whose author is `git config user.email` in the last 14 days, read from HEAD and the local branches), so doctor needs no network to decide it. A pin (`focus`, `ignore`) wins; `ignore` only means "not a focus app", the app is still checked when a focus app needs it.
- Pins live in one per bench key (`APP_FOCUS="a=focus b=ignore"`) in the bench's state file, not in the app's repo or the lockfile: focus is about one developer on one bench, not the team.
- The graph is `required_apps` from hooks.py (as `fl_app_required_apps` reads it), followed transitively, frappe left out: frappe moves under every app, and a warning for it on every bench would drown the ones that matter.
- "Behind" is counted against the remote tracking ref of the branch the app follows (`branch.B.merge` on its remote, else the same name), and the days are the age of the oldest commit HEAD lacks: that is how long the bench has been missing changes, not how old HEAD is.
- The count and that oldest commit are taken on the first parent line (`git rev-list --count --first-parent HEAD..REF`, `git log --first-parent HEAD..REF`): a merge of other history (india_compliance took in a repository with commits from 2011, and its version branch merges hotfix branches) put commits in `HEAD..REF` that are years older than the gap and thousands more than the branch moved, so the days read 5439 instead of 92 and the commits 5541 instead of 14. A merged release counts as its merge commit, not as the commits inside it.
- Doctor stays read only (Akash, 2026-09-29): no automatic fetch, not even once a day. Only `doctor --fetch` or `app focus --fetch` (one global `FL_FETCH`, parsed like `--fix-hints` and ignored by other commands) fetches the focus apps' dependencies, side by side, 20 seconds each, with no prompt (GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0, ssh BatchMode) and an explicit refspec into the tracking ref, so only `.git` changes. Without it the numbers are whatever git last fetched, the user's own `git fetch` included; FETCHED_AT is kept only to say how old benchbar's last fetch is. The other apps are never fetched.
- Offline is never a FAIL: a failed fetch keeps the last numbers, an app never fetched is unknown in the OK line, and `OFFLINE=1` or `--dry-run` never fetches. MCP's `benchbar_doctor` and the app's doctor runs never pass `--fetch`.
- `dependency_behind` is the one check id that can appear several times (one row per stale dependency, each with its own fix); the doctor runner takes extra rows from `CHK_MORE`, so the other checks are unchanged.
- The fix is `benchbar app update NAME`, which already fast forwards with a changelog, backups and migrate; a dependency with local changes gets `git status` first. Never `bench update`.
- `benchbar app focus` without a name lists, instead of a new top level command, and `app list --json` carries the same fields, so the app gets them from the read it already does.

## 0.6.0: profile sharing

- Export reads each default branch through the URL as it is on this Mac and writes the portable one: an SSH alias such as `github-exponent` exists because it carries another account's key, and `git@github.com` with the default key is refused for exactly those repos (found on Akash's Expo profile, 15 of 23 apps). Any app on the base's release branch keeps it, not only registry apps: india_compliance on `version-15` would otherwise be exported on `develop`.
- `profile list --json` keeps `source` as the kind (adding `imported` and `subscribed`) and puts an import's or subscription's URL in the new `source_url`: changing what `source` means would break readers under schema 1, adding a field does not.
- A reviewed plan is bound to the content it showed: the app add token hashes each planned repo's commit (and apply checks the clone is at it before build and install), and profile import, update and export plans carry a `digest` that `--expect` checks (the app also passes every export branch, not only edited ones), so a source that moves between review and apply is refused instead of applied unseen.
- `down` kills a port listener only when its folder is readably inside the bench (`fl_pid_is_bench_own_strict`); the lenient check stays for conflict detection, where an unknown folder should not block a start.
- Update Now and Copy Command fetch `install.sh` from the offered release's tag and pass `--version`, so the app installed is the one the prompt named; the CLI still follows `main` with `git pull --ff-only`, since a tag would leave the managed checkout detached.
- Freshness keeps one `FETCHED_AT` per bench and advances it only when every fetch of the run worked, rather than one time per app: the age note is a caution, and a partly failed run should keep it showing.
- The anonymous access probe runs git with `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null`, no system config and an empty credential helper: a global `url.insteadOf` that rewrites https to SSH would otherwise make every private repo look public.
- `personal` needs both a failed anonymous read and `api.github.com/users/OWNER` saying `User`: a public repo in a personal account is fine to share, so it stays `public`.
- Export keeps the release branch of a registry app (erpnext on `version-15` for a v15 base): the repo's default branch is `develop`, and following it would put a v15 bench on the development branch. Every other app follows its default branch, as agreed.
- Export drops a pinned `commit` when it changes the app's branch: the pin belongs to the old branch.
- Import adds `schema = 2` and `source` at the top of the stored file and removes the file's own lines for them, so what is stored always says where it came from and parses as schema 2.
- `profile check` exits 0 whatever it finds: the answer is in the output, and the app and MCP read the JSON. A missing or invalid profile still exits 1.
- Reachability runs `ls-remote --exit-code --heads --tags REPO BRANCH`: exit 2 (no such branch) is `false`, like a denied read, because install would fail on it too. A resolve or connect error and the 10 second limit are `null`, not `false`, so offline never removes apps.
- `install --profile` skips only `false` repos. With a repo that is `null` it goes on and fails at get-app as before, rather than silently build a smaller bench offline.
- A subscription's profiles are `profiles/*.toml` when that folder exists, else `*.toml` at the root: a config repo often keeps its own `benchbar.toml` lockfile or other TOML at the root.
- Clones and fetches run with `core.hooksPath=/dev/null` and `GIT_LFS_SKIP_SMUDGE=1`, and subscribe clones into a temporary folder first, so a repo that holds no valid profile leaves nothing behind.
- `profile_outdated` follows the read only doctor rule of dependency freshness: the subscription is fetched only with `doctor --fetch`; `.git/benchbar-fetched` in the clone dates the last good fetch for the age note.
- The `--plan` forms of export, import, subscribe and update take no CLI lock, like `list` and `check`, so the app can preview while an install runs.
- `profile remove` refuses a profile that is your own file or a `BENCHBAR_PROFILE_PATH` folder: it only undoes what import and subscribe did.

## 0.7.1: the app's CLI

- BenchBar.app carries the CLI (`Contents/Resources/cli`, install kind `app`) and Homebrew's and the installer's copy exec it through `~/.local/state/benchbar/bin/benchbar`: the app and its CLI are one version whichever channel updated what, without removing a channel or rewriting anyone's PATH. The app always wins over a standalone copy, even a newer one, so the CLI never drifts from the app.
- The hand off is decided in `install-kind.sh` from a fixed path and `-x`, with no process: status runs it on every poll. A dangling link (the app in the Trash) means no hand off. `BENCHBAR_HANDOFF_FROM` stops a second hand off and is unset at once, so no child sees it.
- The app's CLI records the link as `FL_SELF`, the same path whether the app or a handed off copy ran it, so the helper block does not flip between the two. A block naming Homebrew's or the installer's copy that hands off is kept as current: rewriting it would only churn `~/.zshrc`.
- An app copy that is not the registered one (its `FL_SELF` is its own path) never puts that path in the helper block: it keeps the recorded benchbar unless that is gone or an app's internal CLI, else names Homebrew's copy, the installer's or the link (`-x`, no version test: each is what `benchbar` in Terminal runs). Its own path is used only when nothing else is installed.
- Only an app in `/Applications` or `~/Applications` makes the link: an Xcode build, the DMG and a translocated copy would leave it leading somewhere that goes away.
- Only Homebrew and the installer hand off, not `other`: a copy of unknown origin (a test's temporary tree) keeps running itself.
- The CLI is embedded by `macos-build.sh` before signing, not by an Xcode build phase: the files are sealed with the bundle, user script sandboxing stays on, and Xcode's own Debug builds simply carry no CLI. Tracked files only, as in the tarball.

## Cloud sessions

- Cloud only agent notes live in .claude/cloud-context.md and reach Claude Code cloud sessions through a SessionStart hook gated on CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE; local sessions and AGENTS.md are unchanged.

## Linux and Windows

- The SessionStart hook prints linux-context.md in a local Linux session (WSL included) and windows-context.md when OS is Windows_NT; the Mac still prints nothing and cloud sessions still get cloud-context.md, checked first.
- "A recursive delete outside the repo, the benchbar logs and the temp folder" is a PreToolUse hook (.claude/guard-rm.sh), not a deny rule: permission rules match a command's prefix and cannot express "outside", so a deny rule would either block the repo too or miss `rm -fr`. A variable target is blocked because the hook cannot know where it points. It fails closed: without python3 a call mentioning rm is blocked, and a relative target resolves against the call's cwd and every directory a cd or pushd before it may have left the shell in (a cd that may fail keeps the old directory possible; one it cannot follow blocks relative targets).
- The `cli-linux` CI job is back (it was dropped in 0.5 when BenchBar was Mac only): the Linux platform layer is a supported target now, so a Linux regression is a user bug, not a GNU tool curiosity. One unsharded job on ubuntu-latest; the macOS shards are unchanged.
- CI speed with Linux back (PR runs had grown to 10 to 13 minutes, the unsharded Linux job the slowest at 9.5 to 13): the Linux tests run in four shards like macOS, the macOS tests in four instead of three (four plus the app job are the five concurrent macOS jobs of the free plan), and shellcheck is one job of its own instead of a pool job in both the Linux job and macOS shard 1 (85s each, the same 0.11.0). The macOS shards leave out the Linux platform tests (`SKIP_TESTS`): Linux users run that code on bash 5, which the Linux shards cover; locally on a Mac they still run. The test list is re-sorted by measured CI times. The required checks in the `main` ruleset name the macOS shards, so they change with the shard count.
- The real WSL test of the Windows shim runs on a GitHub hosted `windows-2025` runner, not on a personal machine (0.9.0, replaces the self hosted logon task runner): Vampire/setup-wsl (pinned to a commit SHA) installs Ubuntu 24.04 as WSL 2 (hosted runners support it, nothing is skipped), the checkout is copied into the distro and linked as `~/.local/bin/benchbar` like the installer does, and an empty `~/frappe-bench` makes `status --json` answer `not_adopted`. A self hosted runner on a public repo runs a pull request's own workflow edits as the owner's account (OPS-1, SHIM-16) and left runs queued for hours when the laptop was off (OPS-11, CI-1). The job still skips for Dependabot and fork pull requests; the repo also requires approval for all external contributors.
- The Windows only files of the shim (`host_windows.go`, `platform_windows.go`, `selfupdate_windows.go`) have unit tests that run in the `shim-windows` job (`windows-latest`) and the `shim-arm64` job (`windows-11-arm`, a GitHub hosted Arm runner, free for a public repo, which also builds the arm64 `benchbar.exe` and runs `--shim-version`), planned with the shim job (`shim_win` and `shim_arm` in `ci-plan.sh`). The per user registry paths are package variables, so the tests write only under a throwaway `HKCU\Software\BenchBarShimTest-PID-TIME` key they delete, and refuse to run if a test key equals a real one; HKLM is read only. ShellExecuteEx is never started with `runas` in a test: `shellExecute` takes the verb, and the test starts its own binary with `open` and the no UI flag to check the struct layout, the handle and the exit code. The test binary doubles as the child process (`BENCHBAR_SHIM_TEST_HELPER`) for the job object, `procControl` and wait tests, so no test kills a process it did not start. The real WSL test also sends newline, carriage return and tab in arguments, a command line of about 30000 units and 1 MB of binary stdin through `cat`; the same arguments go through the fake wsl.exe on every host. A Ctrl+C case is not part of it: `GenerateConsoleCtrlEvent` needs a console the hosted runner may not give the test, `CREATE_NEW_PROCESS_GROUP` switches Ctrl+C off for the children, and the fake based `TestInterruptReachesChildAndShimWaits` already covers the shim's side.
- `run_shellcheck` returns 1 when shellcheck fails: it runs inside an `if` in the job pool, where errexit is off, so a failing shellcheck still printed "shellcheck: ok" and the job passed. That is how SC2153 and SC2017 sat on main with a green macOS shard.
- Linux breakage list (2026-10-10, Ubuntu 24.04 in WSL): the mock suite fails only 3 of 47 tests on Linux (two need `zip`, one assumes an en_US.UTF-8 locale), because the mocks stand in for every Mac tool. The real CLI stops at its first step, `fl_platform_init`. Behind that gate the code read finds about 110 sites in six groups: Homebrew for every dependency and path (the largest), launchd for the whole service lifecycle and bench discovery (`~/Library/LaunchAgents` globs), lsof and BSD `pgrep -q`/`pgrep -l` for processes and ports (procps rejects `-q`, so "is it running" is always no), the Keychain for the MariaDB root password, `/etc/hosts` and Mac only doctor checks, and a handful of path and text spots. GNU versus BSD tool differences are nearly absent (earlier work already added GNU fallbacks). The fix is one Linux platform file with the same function names plus a systemd --user backend behind the existing `fl_agent_*` names; the full table is in the "docs: Linux breakage list" pull request.
- One CLI for both platforms: `fl_platform_load` picks macOS or Linux once at startup from bash's `$OSTYPE` (no `uname` process, status polls are cost tested), overridable with `FL_PLATFORM`, and on Linux sources `platform-linux.sh` and `systemd.sh` over the macOS functions with the same names. Callers never branch; `fl_is_linux` is only for skipping a whole Mac feature (a doctor check, the hosts step).
- The test harness exports `FL_PLATFORM=macos` by default, so a Linux machine running the suite still tests the Mac path; `use_linux` (or `BENCHBAR_TEST_PLATFORM=linux`) switches a test to the Linux layer and its mocks (ss, systemctl, loginctl, apt-get, dpkg-query, uv, fnm, wslview, xdg-open, getent).
- Ports and process folders on Linux come from one `ss -Hltnp` parser and `/proc/PID/cwd`, normalised to the lines lsof gave (`*:PORT` for any address), so the callers that parse them are unchanged. A socket whose owner ss cannot show (another user's) counts as a listener with pid 0, never as a bench process.
- The `stat -c ... || stat -f ...` fallbacks take each form's output only when it is a number. GNU `stat -f %m PATH` reads `%m` as a file name: it prints file system text for PATH and fails, so the joined output made a lock's age 0 on Linux and once reached the arithmetic of `fl_state_guard_stale`. A guard whose time cannot be read is not stale: the guard is never dropped on a guess.
- On Linux the MariaDB root password lives in `$FL_STATE_DIR/secrets/mariadb-root` (folder 0700, file 0600, written under umask 077 through a temp file and read back), behind the same `fl_keychain_*` names; `MARIADB_ROOT_PASSWORD` still wins.
- On Linux the state folder follows `$XDG_STATE_HOME` (absolute values only, per the spec), the helper block goes to `~/.bashrc` unless the login shell is zsh, and the next steps say `wslview` (WSL) or `xdg-open` instead of `open`.
- On Linux each bench is one `systemd --user` unit, `benchbar-<name>.service`, rendered from `templates/systemd-user.service.tmpl` with the same version hash header and driven through the same `fl_agent_*` names (systemd.sh overrides launchd.sh). `Restart=on-failure` with `RestartSec=20` mirrors KeepAlive SuccessfulExit=false and ThrottleInterval 20, and `StartLimitIntervalSec=0` leaves the decision to stop restarting to the runner's crash guard, as on the Mac.
- Autostart is a `# benchbar-autostart:` comment in the unit plus `systemctl --user enable` or `disable`, so toggling it changes the hash and repair rewrites the unit, as RunAtLoad does in the plist. `loginctl enable-linger` runs when a bench starts with autostart on; a refusal is a warning, not a failure.
- The runner stays one template: its three macOS-only lines (notify, a process's folder, a port's listeners) are `#@default` variables whose defaults are the old macOS text, so the macOS runner renders byte for byte as before (same hash, no "outdated runner" warning) and Linux passes `:`, `/proc` and `ss` instead.
- A bench's unit that is moved aside goes to `systemd/user-disabled` in the config folder, the counterpart of `LaunchAgents-disabled`; nothing in the bench is deleted.
- On Linux, sudo is used for apt, the wkhtmltopdf .deb, MariaDB (admin step, drop-in, restart, starting a stopped mariadb or redis-server) and the LAN guard (`lan-guard install` and `remove`: the `inet benchbar` nftables table, its systemd unit and `/etc/benchbar`), and for nothing else.
- Linux phase 00 runs its sudo sections (apt, MariaDB admin over `sudo mariadb`, the drop-in, the .deb) before the uv and fnm installers and drops the credential in between, so third party installer scripts never find a cached sudo; a fresh root is changed only when its hash is `invalid`, empty or NULL, and keeps unix_socket next to the new native password.
- Doctor on Linux leaves out the checks that only mean something on a Mac (formula dates, Python leaves, legacy agents, CleanMyMac, Mole, app copies, Full Disk Access, fork safety, a Redis on 6379, which is apt's own service there) instead of reporting them as OK: an `[OK]` line for something that cannot exist would read as a check that ran. The rest keep their ids, so `doctor --json` readers and `repair` actions need no Linux case.
- `wsl_time_sync` warns in WSL when NTP runs in the distro: the Hyper-V host sync cannot be turned off from the guest, so two clocks fight and each timesyncd poll steps the clock when Windows has drifted (a step ended a worker's Redis wait and stopped the bench). Its fix is `sudo timedatectl set-ntp false` with no repair action, because `sudo` on Linux is kept for apt, the wkhtmltopdf .deb and MariaDB; outside WSL the check is left out.
- `hosts` on Linux checks that the site name resolves to loopback (`getent hosts`), and the fix names a `*.localhost` site, which needs no `/etc/hosts` line; benchbar never edits `/etc/hosts` on Linux.
- `fl_sudo_begin` starts the session without `sudo -v` when `sudo -n -k true` succeeds, that is, when a NOPASSWD rule applies: with Ubuntu's `%sudo` line also matching, sudo's default `verifypw=all` makes `sudo -v` ask for a password that no command needs, and without a terminal the install stopped there. `-k` makes the probe ignore a cached credential, so a cached one still goes through `sudo -v` as before. Found on the first real install in WSL.
- `*.localhost` does not resolve through glibc on Ubuntu in WSL (nsswitch is `files dns`, and WSL's DNS proxy does not answer `.localhost`), although browsers on Windows resolve it themselves. The Linux install adds `libnss-myhostname`, whose own install script puts `myhostname` on the hosts line of `/etc/nsswitch.conf`, so wkhtmltopdf and Python inside the distro reach the site by name; benchbar edits neither nsswitch.conf nor `/etc/hosts` itself (WSL rewrites `/etc/hosts` on every start). Doctor's fix for a `*.localhost` name that does not resolve names the package (Akash chose this over a hosts line, 2026-10-10).
- v16-lts on Linux is refused for now: it needs MariaDB 11.8 from the vendor repository and Python 3.14, which this session did not set up or test. Later.
- `cli-linux-real` runs on pushes to main and on pull requests labelled `test-linux`, but ci.yml keeps the default pull_request event types, so the label takes effect on the next push (or a re-run), not the moment it is added: adding `labeled` to the types would rerun every CI job on any label change.
- `cli-linux-real` starts a systemd user manager on GitHub's runner itself (`loginctl enable-linger` plus `systemctl start user@UID`, then `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`): the hosted Ubuntu VMs boot systemd, but the runner's job is not a login session, so `systemctl --user` has no bus until linger starts one. It also purges the image's MySQL, which `mariadb-server` cannot install over.
- The unit uses `KillMode=mixed`: a stop sends SIGTERM to the runner alone, which forwards it to honcho as launchd's stop does on the Mac, and whatever is left in the unit's cgroup after `TimeoutStopSec=30` is killed. `control-group` would signal every bench process at once, before the runner could write its stopped state.
- fnm stays outside PATH (phase 00 installs it with `--skip-shell`, as uv's installer runs with `UV_NO_MODIFY_PATH=1`): benchbar edits only its own block of `~/.bashrc`. Repair and doctor's fix lines call fnm by its full path when it is not on PATH, and the helper block adds fnm's folder.
- `loginctl --no-ask-password enable-linger`: polkit allows a user to enable their own linger without a password only in an active session; elsewhere it would put an admin password prompt in the middle of `benchbar up`. Refused, it is a warning with the `sudo loginctl enable-linger` fix.
- The Linux helper block prepends fnm's folder and `~/.local/bin` first and the profile's Python and Node last, so an older `node` or `python3.11` in `~/.local/bin` cannot shadow the profile's (each `export PATH="X:$PATH"` line wins over the ones before it). Doctor reads the tools through the unit's own PATH (`fl_launchd_path_value`), the shared `fl_bench_which`, not a copy of the shell block.
- A start job systemd refuses (a masked unit, a start limit) fails `fl_agent_bootstrap` instead of counting as "agent loaded", so `service` and `repair` report it. A runner that starts and then fails is the crash guard's and `status`'s to show, as on the Mac.

## Windows shim (2026-10-10)

- `benchbar.exe` runs `wsl.exe -d D --cd ~ --exec /bin/sh -c WRAPPER benchbar ARGS`, not `wsl.exe -d D -- benchbar ARGS`: with `--`, wsl.exe joins the arguments into one line for the user's shell, which expands `$` and splits quoted spaces, and a non-login shell has no `~/.local/bin` on PATH, so `benchbar` was "command not found" on a fresh install. `--exec` hands the arguments straight to the program after wsl.exe splits the Windows command line, and the wrapper (`PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"; command -v "$0" || exit 127; exec "$0" "$@"`) adds the installer's folder and passes `"$@"` on unchanged. It sources no profile, so nothing a profile prints can corrupt JSON or MCP output.
- The shim hands its own stdin, stdout and stderr handles to wsl.exe instead of copying them, so there is no buffering and no line ending translation: JSON and MCP's stdio framing pass byte for byte.
- A missing distro is caught from the Lxss registry key before wsl.exe starts (no extra process on every call). wsl.exe's own error for it is UTF-16 text with exit code 127, which cannot be told apart from a missing command. Wrapper exit 127 means `benchbar` is not installed in the distro: one line with the fix, exit 1. Exit codes outside 0 to 255 (wsl.exe's own failures) become 1.
- Ctrl+C: the console sends it to every process attached to it, so wsl.exe gets it and passes SIGINT into Linux. The shim ignores it and waits for the CLI's exit code, and does not send it again. wsl.exe runs in a job object that kills it when the shim dies, so a client that kills `benchbar.exe mcp` does not leave a wsl.exe behind. The job allows silent breakaway: without it, every Windows program the CLI starts through interop (the browser `wslview` opens) joined the job and was killed when the command returned.
- One dependency, `golang.org/x/sys` (from the Go team): registry reads and writes, the job object, the WM_SETTINGCHANGE broadcast and the memory size would otherwise be hand written syscall tables.
- `doctor --json` and `doctor --fix-hints` are pure passthrough; the Windows checks print only in the text form, so a JSON reader sees the same output as on the Mac.
- `%LOCALAPPDATA%\BenchBar\config.json` keeps keys the shim does not know when it writes, so the tray can share the file. `cli_path` points at another `benchbar` inside the distro; the integration tests also use it to send exit codes and quoting through the real hop.
- The keepalive is a Task Scheduler logon task, the runner's pattern (interactive only, hidden through `conhost --headless`, restarted on failure), registered from XML with `schtasks /Create /XML` as the user, without an elevated prompt. Its action is `benchbar.exe keepalive run`, which boots the distro with `/bin/true` and keeps one `sleep infinity` alive, restarting it with a backoff. A `wsl --shutdown` therefore stops the benches for at most the backoff time. That is the purpose: an idle distro is what WSL shuts down.
- `schtasks /End` stops only the task's conhost, not `keepalive run`, so the run identifies itself instead. A named mutex (`Local\BenchBarKeepalive`) keeps it to one run. A pid file holds its pid and process creation time, and `remove` and `install` terminate that pid only when the image is benchbar.exe and the creation time matches. A reused pid (a `benchbar.exe mcp`, say) is never killed. `install` over a running keepalive stops the old run, so changing the distro takes effect at once.
- `wslconfig --apply` changes only `memory` (only when below 4 GB), `vmIdleTimeout` and `instanceIdleTimeout`, after a timestamped backup beside the file. It never runs `wsl --shutdown`, which would stop running benches; it says that the new values apply at the next start.
- Smart App Control in enforce mode (the default on a fresh Windows 11 install) blocks an unsigned `benchbar.exe` outright, as it blocked every build and test binary on the development laptop until Akash turned it off (2026-10-10). The CI artifact is for testing only. A release needs a trusted Authenticode signature, which this session leaves to the release work, together with the installer.
- The job object is attached right after `Start`, not with a suspended start: wsl.exe starts no child of its own before it talks to the WSL service, and a suspended start would need hand written `CreateProcess` code.
- `adopt-distro` changes nothing in the distro except linger, and only after a yes: turning on systemd needs root and a distro restart, and the install is interactive, so both stay fix lines.
- `.claude/guard-rm.sh` now lets a variable target through only when it resolves to a path inside a `/tmp/claude-*` session scratchpad. The value must come from the environment or a literal assignment earlier in the same command, and the name must not be set any other way in it (declare, read, for, eval). The resolved path must contain no whitespace and no symlink that leads out. The command must not mention `IFS`. A glob may appear only in the last component, never inside the variable's value: `rm -rf` on a symlink removes only the link, but a glob before a slash could pass through one. `find -exec rm` is blocked, and so is any `rm -rf` target with a brace expansion (`{a,../..}`, `{1..3}`), which bash expands before rm runs, for plain paths too. It reacts to `rm` as a word, including `rm''` and a tab after it. It blocks, instead of skipping, a piece it cannot parse, rm inside `sh -c` or `eval`, and `xargs rm -rf`, and it follows `then`, `do` and `if`. Both guards compare command names without case and without a `.exe` suffix: Git Bash runs `rm.exe` and `gh.exe`, and Windows finds `RM` as `rm`. It looks for `rm` anywhere in a command, so a wrapper with options (`nice -n 5`, `sudo -u x`, `timeout 5`) cannot hide it. It skips `cd` options before the folder, and counts an assignment only when it is sure to run (not after `&&`, `||`, a pipe or inside parentheses). It also blocks when python3 does not run or crashes: on Windows `python3` is the Store stub, which is on PATH but exits 9009, and the guard used to let every call through.
- GitHub API writes and `schtasks /Create` are checked in `.claude/guard-writes.sh` (pure bash, so it also runs in Git Bash without python3), not by deny rules. Besides the review thread resolve route it allows a thread reply on this repo and GraphQL that only reads or resolves review threads (Akash, 2026-10-10, so Autofix can answer and close Codex threads), each only as the whole command with the text from a file (`-F body=@FILE`, `-F query=@FILE`): free text on the command line could chain another command. A deny rule always wins over an allow rule, so it cannot express these exceptions, and a call with a field flag is a POST that the old `-X` deny rules missed. It reads only the command, never the description, and checks each `gh api` or `schtasks` call on its own text up to the next one. It handles combined short flags (`-iX`, `-fbody=`), and schtasks switches with `/` or `-` in any case. A command chained after a call is part of that call's text, so `gh api ... && tail -f log` is blocked: it errs toward blocking.
- The settings keep the command specific `~/.ssh` and `/mnt/c` deny rules and the read only allow list Akash chose over the old wildcard denies. That trades some cover for fewer prompts: `python3 *`, `find *` and `awk *` can run code that no rule sees. Narrow denies close the plain ways around: reading `~/.ssh` with grep, sed, awk, diff, python3 or find, and `find -delete`.
- The two hooks are a guard against mistakes, not a security boundary (the header of each says so): they read the command text, and a script file that deletes or pushes is not read. A recursive delete is checked wherever it hides in the text (`rm -r` without `-f`, after a backtick or `$(`, behind `unlink`, `shred`, `rimraf`, `find -delete`, `rsync --delete`, `git clean`, `git reset --hard`), and an interpreter call (python, node, perl, ruby, powershell) that mentions a delete is blocked, since its target cannot be followed. Force alone is not looked at: `rm -f` names files. Of the state folders only the logs are a valid target, because the backups beside them are what a repair is undone from; the repo's own `.git` is never one.
- Risky verbs are under `ask` in `.claude/settings.json`, not `deny`: `git push`, `git tag`, `gh pr merge`, `gh pr close`, `gh run rerun|cancel|delete`, `gh release` and `sudo` (the `sudo apt-get` and `systemctl` allows went, since ask wins over allow). The release run is a bypass mode session that must still merge and tag, and a deny rule would stop it; a person in a normal session is asked. `bench update`, `bench restore` and `mariadb -u root` are denied, as AGENTS.md says never to run them. The gh allow list is read only forms (`gh pr view|list|checks|diff|status`, `gh run view|list|watch|download`). `guard-writes.sh` also blocks the gh verbs that write (`pr`, `issue`, `release`, `workflow`, `run`, `secret`, `variable`, `label`, `cache`, `repo`) when `-R`, `--repo`, a github.com URL or the name after `gh repo VERB` names a repository other than askysh/benchbar and askysh/homebrew-tap; a call that names none works on the checkout's own repo and passes. Overturns the allow of `gh pr *`, `gh run *` and `sudo apt-get *` from the 0.8.x settings.
- `guard-writes.sh` looks for a `gh api` body flag (`-f`, `-F`, `--input`) only before the first `;`, `&`, `|` or newline: a `-F` after a pipe is awk's or cut's. The settings no longer name a Windows user: the `.ssh` denies use `Users/*/.ssh`. `tests/test-guards.sh` runs both hooks from files with a table of allowed and blocked lines, in the suite and in CI like every test.
- REST writes to askysh/benchbar and askysh/homebrew-tap pass `guard-writes.sh` (Akash's call: the release run edits rulesets, environments, issues and releases through `gh api`). The consequence: so do `.../pulls/N/merge`, `.../git/refs` (delete a branch or tag) and `DELETE repos/askysh/NAME`, which the hook does not stop. `settings.json` asks before them (`gh api * merge*`, `gh api * git/refs*`, `gh api -X DELETE repos/askysh/*`, and `gh repo delete|archive|rename|edit`), and before `git push` and `git tag` behind an option (`git -C X push`, `git -c X push`, `git --no-pager push`). Under a bypass mode `ask` does nothing, so the hooks are the only fence there, and they do not cover push, tag or merge by design: the release orchestrator needs all three. A person who wants that closed runs the release in a normal session. `mariadb` and `mysql` as root are denied in every spelling (`-u root`, `-uroot`, `--user root`, `--user=root`).
- `guard-writes.sh` reads a `-R`/`--repo` (and any other flag) before the verb (`gh -R other/repo pr merge`) and a `GH_REPO=` anywhere in the command, and blocks `gh secret` and `gh variable` `set|delete` for an organization or the user (`--org`, `--user`). In the JSON of a call a command's backslash is two, so `\rm -rf` is `\\rm`: `guard-rm.sh` drops those pairs before it reads `\t`, `\n` and `\r` as whitespace, which used to turn `\rm` into `\ m`. A command substitution or backtick that is the target of rm, unlink, shred, rimraf, find, git clean, git reset --hard or rsync --delete is blocked, since the piece before it has no operand to read; rm without a recursive flag still passes, as `rm -f` does. Text with a delete piped to `sh` or `bash`, node's `rmSync` and `.git` in any letter case are blocked. Not closed, and said in the header of `guard-rm.sh`: a name built in a variable, a glob for the name, a hidden file glob (`.g*`) and a delete word put together in code.

## Recovery and agent contracts (2026-10-29)

- `site restore` is built on pull's restore steps (`fl_pull_step_*`), not a copy: it fills pull's globals from a local backup set and runs the same backup, quiesce, new-site, restore, key, hook, migrate and cache steps. What it adds is the plan, the token, the local source and its own verify. A restored site is the same site, so it gets no `mute_emails` and no paused scheduler (pull's `dev_safety` step is left out).
- The plan's token hashes the plan body and the bench state it was made against: the site list, the default site, the hook's SHA-256, the backup set (size and time) and whether the bench is running. `--apply` recomputes the plan and refuses a different token, so a set rewritten, a site added or a hook edited since the review voids it.
- The quiesce step of site restore looks at honcho, socketio and the workers (`fl_pm_running`), not at listeners (pull keeps `fl_bench_is_running`): a Redis started by `up --services-only` would otherwise make the bench look "running", add a step and change the token between a plan and its apply.
- The backup an existing target gets first is checked against the set being restored: bench names a backup by the second, and one taken in the second of the set's stamp would overwrite it. A changed size or time of the set after that backup fails the run before the restore.
- The root password is judged in the plan without being tried (`fl_mariadb_root_password_known`: the environment or the store), so a plan says "unknown to benchbar" (exit 2 on apply) instead of passing with the dry run's placeholder; the message never names where the password lives. Apply tries it against the server before anything changes.
- A new site is created with `ADMIN_PASSWORD` or a generated password that is never shown or kept: the restore brings the Administrator password of the backup, so the one `new-site` needs lives only until then. Pull's own `admin_password` step still sets one on request.
- The `--before-migrate` hook must be an executable file (no `bash -c`, no shell text). It runs with the bench as its working folder and `BENCHBAR_SITE`, `BENCHBAR_BENCH` and `BENCHBAR_DB_NAME` set, stdin on `/dev/null` and fd 3 (the JSON stream) closed, and `env -u` for benchbar's own passwords, `MYSQL_PWD`, every variable named like a secret (PASSWORD, PASSWD, SECRET, TOKEN, PASSPHRASE, CREDENTIAL, APIKEY, or ending in `_KEY`, `_PASS`, `_PWD`, `_PAT`) and every variable whose value is a URL with a user name and password. Without `--no-migrate` it runs before migrate; with it, at the place migrate would be. The scrub is hygiene, not a security boundary: the hook is the user's own script, run as the user.
- The default site is recorded before the run and compared at the end. If a step moved it (a hook, a bench command), it is put back with `bench use` and a warning says so; benchbar never edits `sites/currentsite.txt` itself.
- `pull --dry-run` used to print a full plan for `--replace` on a folder that is not a site and then fail at `bench backup`, its first step. The check now refuses a target without a `site_config.json`, an unfinished `new-site` (its marker) and an unknown root password, in the dry run too. Pull creates a missing target with `bench new-site` as a plan step, because a restore needs the site to be there.
- `status --json` reads the Redis ports and asks the other sites for `served` with bash's `/dev/tcp`, not lsof or curl: the status path of a running bench is held to its program budget (`test-status-cost`), and a connect or one GET from bash starts no new program (a subshell for the probe). Each site probe waits one second and all share a 2 second budget, and none is made when the default site did not answer its ping; what is left is null. Only a 404 means "not served" (Frappe's answer for a Host with no site folder); no answer is `null`, never `false`. A stopped bench is not asked at all. `FL_LOCAL_PROBES=0` turns both probes off, and the test harness sets it so no test meets a service of the machine it runs on.
- `up --services-only` writes no stop flag and no `state.json` and needs no installed service: it is for a bench that is being repaired. The Redis stays up until `down`, which already stops the listeners that run in the bench folder.
- A pinned app is cloned with `--skip-assets`, checked for the commit (`git cat-file`, after `fetch --all --tags` and, for a shallow clone, `--unshallow`), checked out, and built once. `bench init` gets `--skip-assets` for a frappe pin the same way. So an unreachable commit is refused before the first build, not after it. The pin comes from the flag, else the environment (`FRAPPE_COMMIT`, as before), else the team profile's `frappe_commit` and `erpnext_commit`.
- `benchbar.toml` already carried `commit` for every app, frappe and erpnext among them, in `lock write`, `lock check` and `lock apply`; only its output changed (`lock check` names the pins). `lock check --json` gets no new key, because the app's fixture is compared key for key.
- `site restore` into an existing site restores from a copy of the set under the state backups (`<state>/backups/<stamp>/restore-set/`, hard links, else copies, sizes checked), made before the backup first: frappe's backup deletes backups older than `keep_backups_for_hours` in `sites/TARGET/private/backups`, which is where `--from STAMP` reads. The copy stays after the run and its path is printed and in the result (`set_copy`).
- The backup taken first must be a new stamp with its database and both file archives; otherwise the run stops before the restore, and there is no fallback to the newest older set.
- The plan runs pull's frappe version gate (`fl_pull_frappe_too_new`): a backup of a newer frappe than the code here is `can_apply: false`. A local set carries no app versions in its dump yet, so the gate only fires where versions are known; it is there for the day they are.
- A dry run (pull and the restore plan) does not exit 2 for an unknown MariaDB root password when a terminal run would ask for it: the plan lists `needs: ["MARIADB_ROOT_PASSWORD"]` and stays applicable. Runs that cannot be asked (`--json`, `--yes`, `--apply`, no terminal) keep exit 2 and the error.
- `down` with no agent loaded and no bench process (only a Redis from `up --services-only`) stops that Redis, waits for nothing and leaves an existing stop flag (crash, broken, port_conflict) and `state.json` alone; it writes `manual` only when there was no flag. `up --services-only` stops the Redis it started when the ports do not come up.
- `site restore` rewrites `encryption_key` in an existing site's `site_config.json` (through a temp file and a rename, as pull does) with the one from the set: the key must match the restored data. That is the one write to a file under `sites/` that benchbar makes outside bench's own commands, and it is the point of the command.
- A pin that is already checked out changes nothing and asks for no rebuild, so a second install is a no-op. A clone fetched with `--skip-assets` (bench init or get-app) leaves `logs/.benchbar/needs-build`; install builds when it finds it, so a run that stopped at an unreachable pin does not leave a bench that a later run without the pin never builds. A commit missing from a single branch or shallow clone is fetched by its hash from each remote (GitHub hands out reachable commits) before the pin is refused.
- The frappe version of a local set is read from the first row of its `tabInstalled Application` table, streamed (`gzip -dc`, `grep -m1`), so the version gate fires for `site restore` and `pull --from-dir`. The restore gets stdin on `/dev/null` in every mode, so frappe's downgrade question can never wait on a terminal.
- `down` decides by "no bench process and no running agent", not by "agent not loaded": an installed agent stays loaded (launchctl print succeeds, a systemd unit is `loaded` while its file exists), so loaded says nothing. The agent still gets a harmless SIGTERM.
- `site restore` copies the set only when it is in the target's own backup folder (the one bench's backup prunes); a set from anywhere else is restored from where it is. A copy that cannot be hard linked is checked against the free space first and removed when it fails. The three newest `restore-set` copies are kept, the older ones go.
- A command that already printed one JSON document with `--json` keeps printing exactly that one line (the Mac app decodes the whole stdout, and nothing under `macos/` changes); the `done` fields are added to it. `--jsonl` asks for the stream instead, so one flag never means two shapes.
- `up`, `down`, `restart`, `service`, `site add`, `site default`, `app update` (without `--dry-run`) and `ports apply` had no JSON, so plain `--json` streams for them, as for `install`, `adopt`, `repair` and `pull`; there is no second format, the stream is `plan`, `step`, `progress`, `done`.
- `--for-agent` picks per command from `mutating` in `lib/frappe-local/commands.sh`: the stream where a command changes things, one document where it reads. One table feeds `--help`, `commands --json` and this choice, and a test fails when a dispatcher label has no entry.
- The table is read into a variable with the `read` builtin: `status` and `list` are polled and `cat` would be a process on every poll. Those two commands do not even look at the table unless `--for-agent` is given.
- A dry run with `--json` is one `plan` (a stream: `plan`, then `done`). Commands that know their plan late (`service`, `site drop`, `site backup`, `app add`, `app update`) send their own; for the others the plan is the command and the dispatcher sends it before anything runs.
- `done.exit` is the process exit code (the EXIT trap sees it), so a pipe cannot hide a failure; `repair` keeps `exit_code` and gains `exit` with the same value.
- `state_after` is the `status --json` object computed in a subshell, without a per site ping; `null` in a dry run, without a bench, and for `ports apply`, which changes several benches. A stream reads it after the locks are released; a one document command (and `pull`) reads it while its lock is still held, which is only a read and keeps the document the command's last act.
- `changed[]` is filled by the code that changes something (`fl_changed`), `next_actions[]` from the `fix:` lines (the why is the last warning or failure before it), `warnings[]` from `[WARN]` lines; the engine records every action that ended `done`.
- The steps of `up`, `down` and `restart` go through `fl_step_begin` and `fl_step_end` with `FL_STEP_QUIET=1`: the stream gets the step lines, the terminal keeps its one line each.
- A refusal under `--json` is text on stderr and stdout stays empty (`fl_die` switches stdout to stderr), as `Rules for readers` always said; before, it was on stdout.
- `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` refuses before the lock, the log and any bench call, from a preflight in the dispatcher that names every missing input in one error (`fl_need`, `fl_need_check`). It is not the CLI's job to guess the answer to a question: `fl_confirm`, `fl_ask` and `fl_ask_secret` die naming the input instead of reading the missing terminal as no or taking a default.
- `MARIADB_ROOT_PASSWORD` is declared only by `site add` and `site drop`, and only when neither the environment nor benchbar's own store has one; `install` cannot know (a fresh MariaDB gets a generated one) and keeps its exit 2. The message never says where the password is stored.
- `site add` asks for `ADMIN_PASSWORD` first, before the apps and MariaDB checks. `uninstall-service` with no terminal and no `--yes` is refused in any mode (it used to end `Cancelled`, which is a "no" nobody typed); `--all` with `--bench-dir` keeps its own message.
- Steps and sudo under the mode: a plain `install` refuses a sudo that would ask; `--json` skips the root steps with their commands as it did. The hosts line of `site add` needs `--yes`.
- `not_adopted` is only decided when nothing runs and no stop flag exists, from file tests and reads in bash (the agent file, the primed default flag, the bench's state file, `registered-benches.txt`). Its `site` and `web_url` are `null`: the default bench's site is never named for another folder.
- `bench_bin` comes from the PATH baked into the agent file (plist or unit), read in bash and searched with file tests, so the status path starts no process. Without a service it is `null`; the PATH of the shell would be a guess about a different process.
- `status --ping` and the MCP status ask the sites only while the bench's processes run; a stopped bench is never pinged, and `list` never pings.
- `site add` records the default site before `bench new-site` and puts it back if bench moved it; only `site default` moves the default.
- `doctor --ack` is stored per bench in its state file as `ACK_<id>` with the message acknowledged, so a changed message (a different warning) lapses it and nothing else needs a clock. A FAIL cannot be acked; `repair` ignores acks (it fixes what is wrong, whoever has seen it).
- `doctor --quick` leaves out the checks that start brew, python, node, bench or git or ask the site (`FL_QUICK_SKIP`), and names them.
- The stale lock line names the command and subcommand and the start time, from an `info` file written when the lock is taken; never an argument, which could hold a token.
- `logs --json` keeps `lines` as strings and adds a parallel `ts`, so no reader breaks. Honcho lines carry only `HH:MM:SS`: the day is worked out backwards from the file's mtime (a time later than the line below it is the day before), and local time is converted with the zone offset of now. Only the newest 20000 lines are looked at, so a 50 MB log costs bounded memory. The calendar arithmetic is awk (BSD awk has no `strftime`).
- The MCP server sets `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` for every CLI it starts, runs the actions with `--json` and returns the `done` line as `result` next to the old `exit_code`, `output` and `status`; `benchbar_status` and the status after an action use `--ping`.
- The fixture checks in `tests/test-json.sh` and the install stream test now assert that every key of the app's fixtures is still printed, not that the sets are equal: fields may be added, never renamed or removed, and nothing under `macos/` changes.
- A key-value file is created with `: >>` (append nothing), never `: >`: two first writers both found no file, and the slower one's truncation emptied what the faster one had written under the lock (a lost key in test-run-lock on CI; with a 0.3 s pause before the create, the old code lost a key in 10 of 10 runs, the fix in none). Its lock (FILE.lck) is also broken only when it is 10 seconds old, not after 2 seconds of waiting, so a long queue of live writers is never broken into.
- A refusal of the non interactive mode is part of the stream: the stream (or the document) starts before the preflight, so `install --json` and `site add --for-agent` end with a `done` carrying `exit`, `error` and `missing`, and still take no lock, write no log and call no bench. It exits 2 when the MariaDB root password is among the missing inputs, as AGENTS.md promises for `site add`, `site drop` and `pull`.
- `changed[]` records only what changed: the shared library functions (`bench init`, `get-app`, `new-site`, `install-app`) call `fl_changed` themselves, so a second run is empty. Phase scripts are child processes: they append `kind`, `name`, `detail` lines to the file `FL_CHANGED_FILE` names and the install folds them in when it builds the `done`.
- `doctor --ack` writes under the CLI lock, answers with one small document under `--json`, and an ack-only state file does not make a bench `not_adopted` look adopted (`fl_bstate_file_substantive`).
- `site add` puts the default site back only when there was one; it re-reads `currentsite.txt` and the remembered site afterwards and fails the step if either is not the old default.
- `state: "not_adopted"` stays a new enum value: the Mac app decodes unknown states as unknown and shows such a bench as "State unknown" until it learns the value, which the unknown-enum rule allows.
- `repair --json` keeps the real stderr on fd 4: a refusal prints its `[FAIL]` reason there and ends with a `done` line (`exit` 1, `error`) even in a dry run, because the Mac app summarizes stderr and stdout when `repair --dry-run --json` fails and an empty one says nothing. A dry run that works is still the plan alone.
- A key-value file's lock is also broken when its mtime is more than 10 s in the future (the clock stepped back, a VM resumed), and after 600 waits (about 30 s) whatever its age: a state write must not wait forever on a lock nobody holds. An age that cannot be read is never a reason to break it: under contention that is mostly a lock released a moment ago, and breaking then removed the next writer's lock (with stat failing on purpose, 3 of 4 runs lost a key before the fix, none after).
- `site add` with neither the Administrator nor the MariaDB root password exits 2 in every mode, as `site add` always did when the MariaDB password was the unknown one; `ADMIN_PASSWORD` alone missing is exit 1.
- After a `site add` the default is compared with what `currentsite.txt` said before, not with the remembered site: a user who ran `bench use other` is not an error.
- A refusal's `done` has `state_after` null (no status is computed for a run that did nothing) and the reason is also one `[FAIL]` line on stderr, for a person who ran `--json` in a terminal.
- `tests/test-install-json.sh` is split in two files of about equal runtime (`test-install-json-2.sh` has the no gui mode, adopt, ports apply under the dialogs and profile list) so that each stays well under the 600 s a test may take; every assertion is kept and the cap is unchanged.
- An `app update` or planned `app add` that stops part way leaves a pending record (`<state>/benches/<bench>.pending/<app>.json`, one line of JSON with the steps not yet done), and the next run takes its steps from the record, not from Git: after the fast forward, `old == new` says nothing about whether migrate and build ran.
- The pending record is read with bash regexes, not a JSON parser: bash 3.2 has none and the CLI path must not need python3. That works because the writer only stores benchbar's own values (enum kinds, site and app names) and turns quotes and backslashes in a failure reason into `'` and `/`.
- The record lives in a `.pending` folder next to the bench's `.env` state file, one file per app, written through a temp file and `mv`. Status and doctor read it with a glob and `read`, so the status path starts no process (test-status-cost holds).
- `app update` with a record trusts the record's `to`, not the remote: the range the person reviewed is the one that finishes, and newer commits are counted (`newer_commits`) and wait for the next run. A fetch that fails is not fatal then, because finishing a migrate needs no network.
- An app whose HEAD is neither the record's `from` nor its `to` is refused rather than guessed at; `--discard-pending` is the explicit way out, and it logs the record it drops.
- `--skip-backup` on a retry removes backup steps still in the record: it is the person's explicit choice for this run.
- A planned `app add` keeps its record when a step fails, but a clone that failed and was rolled back leaves only the build of the apps that did get cloned (or no record): an absent app is simply planned again. Installs on different sites stay independent, and a failed install does not skip the restart.
- Dependency order is Kahn's algorithm with ties broken by the old list order (the walk reversed, the main app last), so a plan that was already in a valid order is byte for byte the same. The app being added is a node of the graph, which is how a cycle through it is found.
- One timeout, `fl_run_capture_timeout`, built on the existing process group helpers; the grace between TERM and KILL is `FL_KILL_GRACE` (default 1 second, polled every 50 ms: a group that is gone at once is not waited for) so tests need not wait a second per kill. The deadline is the clock's, not a count of polls: a command still running after the first 20 ms poll gets a `sleep` of the rest as its timer, because on a loaded macOS runner a counted 1 s deadline took 3.5 to 5 s (the job's own process group was fine there: `set -m` makes it without a terminal too, checked on the runner).
- The plain `app add` now goes through the plan and the shared apply loop. Behavior that changes: required apps are cloned before the app that needs them (apps.txt order), one question instead of two, and a plan that cannot be applied (an unknown required app) clones nothing, where the old flow cloned the app first and failed after. Its error hints (the remote's branches, the ssh and gh lines) are printed from the plan's errors.
- The mock tests check that every key of the app's fixtures (macos/BenchBarTests/Fixtures) is still printed, not that the CLI prints exactly those keys: the CLI adds fields this round (`pending_operations` and others) without a change under macos/, and the rule for readers is that fields may be added, never renamed or removed.
- The plain `app add` also changes how it reads the world: `hooks.py` comes from a filtered shallow clone (`--depth 1 --filter=blob:none --no-checkout`) before anything is cloned, so a server that rejects `--filter` or a repo whose `hooks.py` cannot be found now fails the plan where the old flow cloned first. An app already in the bench whose required apps are missing is planned (clone_required, build, restart) instead of refused, and Restart is always listed; it ends `skipped (not running)` when the bench is stopped.
- An update record accepts HEAD at `from` only while its `fast_forward` step is still listed; after it, `from` means someone went back (the `reset --hard` we print), and the run refuses and names `--discard-pending`. A record with no step left is checked once (HEAD at `to`) and cleared; `app update NAME --discard-pending` also drops an `app_add` record, and it runs before the dirty, detached and remote checks because it only removes benchbar's own file.
- An `app_add` record with no step left is verified and cleared when the app passes, otherwise the plain and the planned add fail and name `--discard-pending`.
- A get-app that cloned the app and then failed (its pip install) puts a `requirements_python` step for that app in the record, before the build, so the install-app that follows never runs against an env that lacks the package.
- Dependency order treats an app already in the bench as a pass-through: if A requires P (in the bench) and P requires B (to clone), B goes before A.
- `--discard-pending` drops the record and returns: it never falls through into an update, because the person asked to forget a half done run, not to start another (with `--yes` that would have run a backup, merge and migrate). With no record it says so and returns 0.
- A record that does not end in `}` (cut short) or is from another version is kept, not guessed at: `app update` and the plan refuse to run from it, doctor warns, and `--discard-pending` is the way out. A record that cannot be written stops the run before the first step (and counts as a failed step later), because a stop could not be finished without it.
- A failed `requirements_python` step skips build, install and restart like a failed build, and the record keeps them. The record path is built only from a plain app name, so a name with a slash can never reach a file outside the `.pending` folder.
- `app add` syntax for pins, batches and per app sites: the targets are the positional arguments, and `--branch`, `--name`, `--commit` and `--install-on` belong to the target before them (to the first one when they come before every target, so `app add --branch B URL` still works); `--site`, `--all-sites` and `--apply` are for the whole add. Chosen over `NAME#commit=SHA` or `NAME@SHA` because a URL can hold `#` and `@`, and one flag spelling serves one app and a batch alike. `--install-on` replaces `--site` and `--all-sites` for its app.
- `--commit` takes 7 to 40 hex characters. The plan clones the branch shallow and blobless, fetches a full hash by id (GitHub hands out reachable commits), and for a short one fetches the rest of the history until it resolves; `commit` in the plan is always the full hash, so the token pins one commit. The plan reads `hooks.py` at the pin, not at the branch tip. `get-app` still clones the branch (bench has no commit option); the checkout of the pin follows it (`fl_commit_reach`, then a checkout), before the build, and a clone that does not reach it fails like a moved branch (`planned X, cloned Y; nothing was built or installed`) and leaves the folder for `bench remove-app`. A pin on an app already in the bench must match its HEAD, else the plan has an error: benchbar never moves an app. The required apps are not pinnable; they are read and cloned at their branch tip, and checked against the commit the plan read.
- A batch is one graph: the required apps of all targets are walked once (an app two targets need is one node and one clone), a target another target requires is a node, not a missing app, and Kahn's sort orders all of them (ties by list order, targets in the order given). One build step covers every cloned app, one restart ends the run. Installs are one step per app and site, site by site and each site in dependency order, so a site's first failure shows early; an install is skipped when an app it requires failed to install on the same site, and the other sites carry on.
- The pending record of a batch is one record per target that has work (not one keyed by the first app): doctor's `benchbar app add NAME` fix and `app update NAME --discard-pending` work on each app, and a retry of one app plans only its own installs. Each record lists the shared steps (clones, build, restart) and only its own installs. A target with no work (in the bench, installed everywhere, no missing required app) gets no record. A failed clone writes the same short record as before (requirements, build, restart) for every target with work; the installs are planned again from the site lists.
- The apply result reports the install and its migrate with one status: `install-app` runs the app's patches and DocTypes sync, and there is no separate migrate step to report, so each `installs[]` entry carries `migrate` equal to its `status`. `apps[]` is always in the result, also for one app; in the plan it appears only when there are several, so the plan of one app keeps today's shape.
- The MCP site and service tools follow app add's plan, token, apply: `site add --dry-run --json` became a one document plan (like `app update --dry-run --json`), and `service`'s plan line gained a token computed in the plan hook from its actions, the bench, the port block and the scheduler, so `service --apply TOKEN` is refused before any action runs when the plan changed.
- `benchbar_site_add` takes the new site's Administrator password or generates one (20 letters and digits, never starting with `-`). A generated one is saved in `site-admin/SITE` of benchbar's state folder (0600, in a 0700 folder; the server asks `where --json` for the folder): written to a new temp file (`O_EXCL`, 0600) in that folder and renamed over the target, so an older file's mode or link never applies, with the temp file removed and the loss reported if any step fails; `site drop` removes the file and the result names the file (`admin_password_file`) and never holds the password: a result lives in the model's context and in any transcript. An agent that needs a login passes its own `admin_password` or asks the person. The MariaDB root password is never an argument or a result. The password was returned once before (review MCP-21).
- The MCP server withholds any line that names where the MariaDB root password is kept (`mariadb-password`, the `benchbar-mariadb` Keychain item, `secrets/mariadb-root`, `find-generic-password`), in one pass over every result and error just before the reply, text and structured content alike: agents that saw such a hint went to read the password themselves (agent review #2). A bare "Keychain" is not enough to withhold a line: a git credential helper error must reach the agent.
- `benchbar_site_restore` offers no `--before-migrate` hook: a script path from an agent would run arbitrary code as the user inside a benchbar run; the hook stays a terminal flag.
- MCP progress: with a `progressToken`, an action runs with `--jsonl` and each `step` or `progress` line becomes one `notifications/progress` (a counter and the step's name); the result is still built from the `done` line. Without a token nothing changes.
- `benchbar_site_add` returns a generated Administrator password only when its result says `created: true`: on a site that existed the password was never used, and returning it would hand the person a login that does not work.
