- `service --apply TOKEN` refuses `--with-schedule` and `--without-schedule`: the scheduler choice is stored before the plan is made, so with a token it would be written even when the token turns out stale. A reviewed plan is applied as it is.
- A streamed MCP call is complete when its `done` line has been read, not when stdout closes: `up --services-only` starts a Redis that may keep the pipe open, and waiting for the end of it would hold every such call to its timeout.
- The shim's `wsl_enable` is the only elevated action, and the UAC prompt elevates `<GetSystemDirectory>\wsl.exe --install --no-distribution` itself, never `benchbar.exe`: the default install is per user and user writable, so same user malware could swap the exe before the prompt is accepted, while System32 is writable by administrators only. The window is hidden, so there is no output to relay; the result is the exit code (3010 or 1641 means restart, other non zero fails with the code) plus `wsl.exe --status` afterwards (silent WSL means restart), a declined prompt (error 1223) is `cancelled`, and a named mutex `Local\BenchBarWslEnable` stops a second elevation while one runs. If the caller is cancelled while wsl.exe still runs, the install cannot be undone, so the result is not a failure: it says the install keeps running in Windows, asks for a restart and writes the restart marker.
- `setup check` never starts a stopped distro (running state comes from `wsl --list --running --quiet`, names only, so it does not depend on the Windows language) and never elevates; the steps inside the distro read "not checked" and `ready` stays false until `--probe`.
- `setup check` marks each step `blocking`. `linger`, `keepalive`, `wslconfig` and `shim_path` are advisory: they are `warn` at worst and never decide `ready`, because a user who cannot fix them (another `benchbar.exe` earlier on the machine PATH, a unreadable registry) would be stuck in the wizard; with no action the detail carries the exact fix command.
- `report-windows` is a shim command, not a CLI one, because the report has to work where the CLI cannot run (no WSL, no distro, stopped distro). It reads the running state with `--list --running --quiet` and never boots the distro, so a stopped distro simply has no `cli-report.zip` and the reason is listed under `skipped`; the CLI's zip is written to `/tmp` in the distro and copied out with `wsl.exe --exec cat`, not through `\\wsl.localhost`, so the shim never reads files under the distro from Windows. Redaction runs on the text parts only (the CLI redacts its own zip), and the profile folder becomes `C:\Users\<user>` so the log stays readable.
- A pending restart is the marker `setup\reboot-pending.json` written after `wsl_enable` said so (exit 3010 or 1641, the system text, or WSL still silent; `wsl_enable` and `distro_install`) and counts until Windows has started after its time; the generic Windows Update and CBS flags are used nowhere, not by check and not by `wsl_enable`, since an unrelated update would block onboarding.
- The distro is installed with `wsl --install -d NAME --no-launch` and started once as root with `--exec`: Microsoft runs the first-run user prompt only in an interactive shell, so nothing asks a question; `distro_user` then creates the user as root, feeds the password to `chpasswd` on stdin only, and sets `[user] default` in /etc/wsl.conf after a backup.
- `systemd` and `distro_user` stop only the one distro with `wsl.exe --terminate NAME`; the shim never runs `--shutdown` or `--unregister`, and `wsl_update` warns that WSL may restart. When the distro already runs (asked first with `--list --running --quiet`, because the action's own commands start it) and a stop is needed, they refuse before changing anything unless `--force` is passed: exit 1 with `error_id: "distro_running"`, since a stop ends the person's benches and MariaDB.
- The forwarder adds the five password and sudo variables that are set to the child's `WSLENV` copy and leaves the user's own `WSLENV` and every other variable alone.
- `BENCHBAR_SUDO=env`: `sudo` is a shell function that validates with `sudo -S` (password on stdin from a printf builtin in a process substitution, never a pipeline: bash 3.2 forks a child inside a pipeline element, so the ticket would belong to the wrong parent) in the same shell that then runs the real sudo (by its path, not "command sudo": bash 3.2 with `set -e` ends the shell when `command CMD` fails even under `if` or `||`), because without a terminal sudo ties its ticket to the parent pid; the command keeps its own stdin, and no keepalive runs.
- `BENCHBAR_SUDO_PASSWORD` is taken out of the environment before anything else runs (`sudo-env.sh`). It reaches only phase 00, as one line on fd 8; phases 01 and 02 and every other child never get it, and install forgets it right after phase 00.
- A wrong sudo password is checked up front with `sudo -k` first, so a cached ticket cannot make it pass. Its id is `sudo_password_wrong`, in `error_id` of every done line.
- Linux prerequisites read no root-only state: the sudo check uses group membership, not `sudo -n`, so the check never runs sudo.
- The Linux layer never calls `lsof`: listeners and the owner's working folder come from `fl_port_listener_pid`, `fl_port_listener_pids` and `fl_port_owner_cwd` in `platform-linux.sh` (ss and /proc), so a Ubuntu without the `lsof` package behaves the same.
- `tests/test-windows-commands.sh` runs the Windows tray's command list under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` with a failing `lsof` first on PATH, so a later direct `lsof` call fails the suite.
- `benchbar` starts itself again once when `BENCHBAR_SUDO_PASSWORD` is set (same pid, `exec`): the kernel keeps the first image's environment in `/proc/PID/environ` for the whole run, where the pip and npm scripts of the same user could read it. The second image gets the password on an inherited pipe (fd 9, `FL_SUDO_PW_FD`) and no variable. The variable being gone is the loop guard.
- On Linux, repair runs its sudo actions (`mariadb_bind`, `mariadb_utf8`, `wkhtmltopdf_install`) first in the `FL_ACTION_ORDER` of `doctor-linux.sh`; macOS keeps its order, where `hosts_entry` stays late and nothing is dropped, so the credential and the password can be dropped (`fl_sudo_phase_done`) before pip, npm and bench steps. They do not depend on the Python, Node or build actions.
- The Linux `sudo` prerequisite warns, not fails, outside the sudo and admin groups: a sudoers rule can still allow it, and the check never runs sudo to find out.
- `benchbar report` writes its zip with Python's `zipfile` when `zip` is missing (a distro with only the CLI, before the system dependencies).
- `install.sh` on Linux is the CLI half of the macOS flow with the same paths (`~/.local/share/benchbar`, `~/.local/bin`, the `benchbar-path` block), so `install-kind` says `managed` there too. It links only `benchbar` (no `frappe-mac`), skips Homebrew detection and the app, and never asks: `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` makes `has_tty` false, and a found bench is only named, not adopted.
- On Linux `--version vX.Y.Z` pins the CLI, not an app: a fresh clone uses `git clone --branch`, a rerun `fetch --tags` and `checkout`. This is the one case of a detached checkout the installer creates; the macOS rule that `--version` pins the app only is unchanged, and a run without `--version` goes back to `main` with the detached HEAD logic that was already there. The shim passes `--version v<its own version>` for a release build and nothing for a dev build, so the CLI in the distro matches `benchbar.exe`.
- `benchbar install` passes `BENCHBAR_PLAN_STEP1` (the phase 00 script) to phase 01 in a dry run only. With it, a tool that step 1 installs and is missing (uv, pipx, Python, Node, MariaDB, Redis) is a note "will install it", not a warning or a die; without it (01 run alone) the old warning stays, because nothing installs those first.
- The port blocker on Linux asks the distro's own systemd unit (`mariadb`, `redis-server`), not `ss -p`: a normal user sees no owner for mysql's process, and no owner at all for another WSL distro's, whose processes live in a PID space of their own. A listener with the unit inactive is foreign; with it active it is ours (a rerun). A dry run with a blocker still prints the plan, with `blockers[]`, and exits 1 like any other refusal; the done line carries `error_id`, `error` and `fix` (`FL_ERROR_FIX`).
- `open --as` lands on `/login?redirect-to=PATH&sid=SID&t=SECONDS`, not on `PATH?sid=` as `bench browse` does: for a signed in session frappe's login page answers 301 to PATH with the cookie, so the session id leaves the address bar. Browsers cache a 301, so `t=` makes every link new.
- `open --as` runs the steps of frappe's `LoginManager.post_login` except `set_user_info`. That one sets the response cookies (the browser's own `/login?sid=` request sets them) and finds the default page by importing every app: on a bench with Insights it was six of the seven seconds of a login. `on_login` hooks, IP and hour restrictions and the session run as in a real login.
- `open --as` needs `developer_mode` for Administrator too, where `bench browse --user Administrator` does not: one rule for every user, and a production copy brought in with `benchbar pull` never signs anyone in on its own.
- The session id of `open --as` is on the browser opener's command line (`open`, `xdg-open`, `wslview` take the URL as an argument; there is no other way to hand it over) and nowhere else: no run log, no file, not in `--json` unless `--print-url`. There is no MCP tool for it: no tool hands a credential to an agent.
- `site list` and `status` read `developer_mode` from the JSON files in bash, without frappe: status is polled, and clients need it to explain why Open as does not work; `open --as` asks frappe itself before it signs anyone in.
- `open --as` refuses a site other than the `default_site` of `common_site_config.json`: `bench serve` without `--site` serves that one site for every Host (`bench_helper.get_sites`), so a session made on another site is unknown where the browser lands. Plain `open` stays as it was.
- In WSL `open --as` opens the browser itself only for `localhost` and `*.localhost` names: `wslview` opens a Windows browser, which resolves names through Windows' hosts file, not the distro's that `getent` reads. `--print-url` and `--no-browser` are not refused for that reason, since whoever opens the link may have the name in Windows' hosts file. `open --as USER --print-url` is refused under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE` (so `--for-agent` and `benchbar mcp`) before any session is made, as `site open-as on` is (review MCP-2): a link in an agent's output is a login session in its transcript. Only the mode refuses, not a missing terminal, so the tray and a person's own script keep the link. `users` is not refused: it lists enabled user ids and holds no session.
- The bash read of `developer_mode` (one key per line, as frappe writes `site_config.json`) only decides what to offer; the Python check before the sign-in is the authority, so a minified file or a value like `2` can at worst refuse wrongly, never sign in wrongly.
- `open --as` and `users` need the person to turn Open as on per site (`site open-as on NAME`), kept as `OPEN_AS_SITES` (space separated names) in the bench's state file: never under `sites/`, and a site that `pull` or `site add` creates is never on by itself. `site drop` clears the name so a later site of that name does not inherit it. Plain `open` needs no opt-in.
- `open --as USER --print-url` is refused under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` and when stdin is not a terminal, unless the caller sets `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=0` itself (the Mac app's `openAs` does; the Windows tray never calls it). The first version refused only the mode, so an agent's plain shell still got the link (review MCP-2). The explicit 0 is a caller's statement that a person clicked, as for `site open-as on`; it is a guard against a mistake, and the docs say a process of the same user that fakes a terminal or sets the 0 is not stopped.
- `site open-as on` is refused when `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` (so under `--for-agent` and `benchbar mcp`), checked before the lock: password-less sign in is a person's decision, and an agent that could turn it on could then pull a session id with `--print-url`. `--yes` does not stand in for the person: `on` also needs a terminal on stdin, or `BENCHBAR_SUDO=gui` (the app, after its own alert), so an agent's plain shell with `--yes` is refused too. This is a guard against a mistake, not a boundary: whatever can run the CLI can also set the variable or edit benchbar's state. A byte of `--path` above 127 is masked before it is encoded: bash 3.2's `printf "'c"` sign extends it. `off` and `status` are always allowed. `on` is TTY yes (it asks, `--yes` skips) and MUTATING yes (it writes state, takes the lock and has a run log, which holds no session id).
- `fl_person_env_ok NAME VALUE` (ui.sh) is the one test for `BENCHBAR_SUDO=gui` and `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=0` where they lift "needs a terminal" (open-as on, open --as --print-url, lan-guard, share setup, start and the admin commands); `labs on` uses it too, with `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=0` (unset is not a claim), so a plain agent shell is refused with exit 2; `labs off` is never gated. On macOS it requires `BENCHBAR_APP_PID` to be a process that is above this CLI in the `ps -o ppid=,comm= -p` chain (at most 64 hops, `/bin/ps` by absolute path, no `pgrep`) and whose `comm` is `*/BenchBar.app/Contents/MacOS/*`. That is a name check on the parent chain: it stops an agent that only sets the variables, and the refusal names the reason (MAC-4 of #161; the app side is PR 204); it does not stop one that fakes a parent named like the app or sets the `FL_TESTING` seams. It runs only after the terminal test failed, so a person's terminal never pays for it. On Linux and WSL there is no check, because the Windows tray's process is not visible from inside WSL: there, and on a Mac for a process that already runs inside the app's process tree, the guard stays a safety rail and not a boundary, and AGENTS.md, docs/guides/agents.md and docs/app.md say so. Tests read `FL_PERSON_SELF_PID` and `FL_PS_CMD` under `FL_TESTING=1` only.
- `site list` and `status` give each site `open_as_enabled` (the opt-in) and `open_as` (opt-in, developer mode and the site `bench serve` answers as), so a client offers Open as only where the CLI will sign in.
- `open` stays non MUTATING although `--as` writes a session in the site's database: a MUTATING command has a run log, and a log could capture the session id. The sign in changes no bench file.
- `open --as` reruns the steps of Frappe's `LoginManager.post_login` except `set_user_info`, so it depends on Frappe internals. Tested on v15; v16 is untested so far. The side effects are those of a real login: `on_login` hooks run, `last_login` is updated, and Frappe may end the user's oldest session (`simultaneous_sessions`). `open --help`, the CLI docs and `site open-as on` say so.
- What a sign in does is listed in full where a person turns Open as on (the prompt, `open --help`, the docs, the app's alert), not only `on_login`: `make_session` also runs `on_session_creation`, so Frappe writes an Activity Log entry per switch, can mail the System Managers for an Administrator sign in, and ERPNext can create a Customer or Supplier for a Website User. Reported in review by Sumanth Udupi.
- `--path` refuses a backslash: browsers read `/\host` as `//host`, so `/\evil.example` passed the single slash check and would have sent the browser to another site after the sign in.

## Test cost

- The mocks find `_mocklib.sh` with `${0%/*}` and name themselves with `${0##*/}`, and run under `#!/bin/bash`, not `/usr/bin/env bash`: a mock call started env, bash, dirname and basename before it did anything, and now starts bash alone. `/bin/bash` is the 3.2 the CLI targets on macOS and exists on the Linux runners. What a mock logs and prints is unchanged, and test-status-cost no longer needs its dirname exception.
- `test-command-cost` keeps a program budget for `doctor`, `doctor --json`, `install --dry-run`, `adopt --dry-run` and `app list`, counted with test-status-cost's shims on two registered benches and one to adopt. Each budget is the larger of the macOS and Linux counts plus about 10 percent, so the test documents today's cost and fails when a change makes a command start more programs; a change that makes one cheaper lowers its budget.
- The cheap forks went from the paths these commands take: `$(basename X)` and `$(dirname X)` on a path from a glob or an agent file list (it has a slash) became `${X##*/}` and `${X%/*}`, `fl_bench_canonical` strips trailing slashes before it does the same for a folder not created yet, and exact line tests in `printf | grep -q` became `[[ ]]` patterns. `sed | head -n1` pairs and the case insensitive `grep -qi` tests stay: changing them needs a decision on what each pattern should match.

## CI speed, round 3

- Linux carries the suite: 8 Linux shards, 4 bash 3.2 shards and 4 macOS shards on a full run (this line said 2 macOS shards: two were tried and made the run longer, see below), not 4 and 4. Since "CI tiering" a pull request runs only the tests it affects, so the shard counts follow the weight of the selection. Linux runners start faster, cost less and run more jobs at once than macOS runners. The slowest shard is what a pull request waits for, and more Linux shards shorten it.
- A job runs the suite under bash 3.2.57 (the `bash:3.2.57` image) on Linux runners: the bash-ism risk, a 4.x feature that the macOS `/bin/bash` does not have, is a property of the interpreter, not of the OS, so a Linux runner covers it. The image is Alpine, where JavaScript actions such as checkout do not run in a job container, so the job checks out on the runner and runs the suite with `docker run`. It adds the GNU tools the suite expects and a `/bin/bash` link for the mocks, and runs as a user from a copy of the checkout.
- The macOS shards stay for what only a Mac has: the BSD versions of sed, awk, date, stat and find, and the `/bin/bash` users really run. They skip the Linux platform tests, as before (and, since "CI tiering", `test-wsl-preflight`, `test-windows-commands` and `test-sudo-env`, which call `use_linux`). 
- macOS stays at 4 shards, not 2: on the first run with 2 the whole run took 17.3 minutes instead of 14.1, because the slowest test files take 8 to 10 minutes each on a macOS runner and one shard held several of them (1,002 s against 552 s for the other). No shard count gets below the slowest single file; splitting those files is what would.
- The heaviest test files are split into parts that each build their own fixture and run side by side (test-app-recovery in 3, test-agent-contracts in 3, test-profiles in 3, test-site-restore by platform), the shared setup in tests/lib. A shard cannot finish before its longest file, and the old 250 to 320 s files set that floor. A part that needs state an earlier part builds repeats the few commands that make it, not the assertions.
- `SHARD=i/n` fills the shards greedily from a time table in tests/run-tests.sh (`TEST_SECS_MAC` and `TEST_SECS_LINUX` since "CI tiering"; first `TEST_SECS`: heaviest test first onto the shard with the least work so far), not by taking every nth test of a sorted list. With uneven tests the every nth split gave 361 to 634 s for four macOS shards; the table gives shards within 1% of each other. The cost is that a new or changed test needs its time in the table (an unlisted one counts as 10 s, and the shards stay disjoint and complete either way). `tests/affected.sh` picks every part of a test (`test-X-2`, ...) with `test-X`.

## CI tiering

Written for the macOS arm64 runner shortage of November 2026 (pull requests waiting hours for four macOS shards plus the app) and CI-2, CI-5, CI-7, CI-8, CI-11, CI-13, CI-15, CI-17, CI-18 of the review. The aim: a typical pull request under 6 minutes, with no job waiting on scarce runners it does not need.

- One planner decides the run: `scripts/ci-plan.sh`, run by the `changes` job, tested by `tests/test-ci-plan.sh`. Every job takes its `if`, its matrix and its test list from the plan, so a rule lives in one place and the workflow has no path filter (a workflow level `paths` would leave a required check pending, see "0.5.5: docs site").
- A pull request runs the tests `tests/affected.sh` picks, on Linux (bash 5) and in the bash 3.2 container: the same suite three times (CI-13) bought one thing on macOS, BSD userland, and nothing the container missed in the 15 failed runs of the review. macOS runs only when a path that needs it changed (`macos_paths` in the planner: platform, launchd, lan-guard, mariadb, sudo, wkhtmltopdf, the share files, the Mac phase, install.sh, packaging, the plist templates and the tests of those) or the label `test-macos` is on. The cost: BSD only breakage in other lib files is found at the push to `next-release` or the nightly, not on the pull request. The label exists for a change that touches `sed -i`, `stat`, `date` or `find` flags.
- A change to the harness, a mock, `run-tests.sh`, `affected.sh`, the entry point, `frappe-mac`, `templates/`, `config/` or a phase script runs every test on every platform, as does a change to `ci.yml` or the planner (those two also run the app and the shim). `affected.sh` used to run only `test-affected` for itself: the selector decides what runs, so a change to it must not skip tests.
- A push to `main` or `next-release` and the nightly (02:37 UTC) run everything everywhere, with the real install and the Windows jobs. GitHub runs a schedule from the default branch (`main`, the last release), so every checkout of `ci.yml` takes `next-release` on a scheduled run (the workflow file itself is main's, which is this file once a release carries it). A push that changes only docs, `site/` or `*.md` skips them. The pull request runs use a concurrency group per pull request number, so a pull request run and the push run after its merge never cancel each other, and a new push to a pull request cancels the run before it. A push to `main` is never cancelled. Merge queue (CI-12) is another issue.
- `affected.sh` (CI-8): each `lib/frappe-local` file has a family of tests (`lib_family`) that run it through a command (doctor, repair, install, adopt), because the function name match cannot see them; the LAN share's JavaScript and Python, the app fixtures, and the scripts have their own entries; `docs/` selects `test-docs` and `test-docs-command`. A lib file with no test of its own name and no family entry fails `test-affected` (`--lib-coverage`), and falls back to the end to end core set at run time. Without git (`--files`, used by the planner's tests) every function of a changed file counts as touched.
- Shards are sized, not fixed (CI-5): `ci-plan.sh` divides the weight of a leg's tests by 600 table seconds (Linux, bash 3.2) or 900 (macOS, 3 CPUs), so about 150 s of wall time each, up to 8, 8 and 4 shards. A one test change gets one shard, a full run keeps the old counts (the bash 3.2 container goes from 4 to 8 shards: Linux runners are cheap and plentiful). The table is now two tables, `TEST_SECS_MAC` and `TEST_SECS_LINUX` (the average of bash 5 and the container), regenerated by `scripts/ci-timings.sh RUN_ID` from the `--- ok: NAME (Ns)` lines of a green full run; run 44055044922 (2026-11-17) is the current source. 24 of 78 old entries were off by more than 1.6 times. Table drift costs shard balance only, never coverage.
- The test list and the seconds per test are not in `run-tests.sh` any more (November 2026, the 0.9.0 run). Every `tests/test-*.sh` is a test, so there is no list to add a new file to, and the seconds live in `tests/timings.tsv` (name, macOS, Linux; `scripts/ci-timings.sh --write RUN_ID` refreshes it; a test with no row counts as 10). Why: a new test had to edit `run-tests.sh` twice, `affected.sh` runs every test on every platform for a change to that file, so nearly every pull request that added a test became a full run with four macOS shards (the median pull request run of 20 November took 61 minutes of wall time), and every two open pull requests conflicted in the same two blocks. A change to `tests/timings.tsv` picks `test-ci-plan` and `test-suite-hygiene` only, and the file merges with `merge=union`. The list check ("is not in the test list") is gone with the list: a file cannot be forgotten now. `test-suite-hygiene` checks that each row names a test that exists.
- shellcheck (CI-15) runs 12 files per process and one process per CPU at a time (`xargs -P`), and on a pull request only on the changed shell files (`SHELLCHECK_FILES`); a full run checks everything. The binary download picks the x86_64 or aarch64 build with its checksum, so `ubuntu-latest` moving to ARM does not break it (CI-18f).
- `workflow_dispatch` has inputs `tests` and `legs` (CI-17): a flake hunt is `tests: test-run-lock`, `legs: macos`, one shard on one runner instead of sixteen. The test names are checked against the test list; names that are not test names are dropped, and a list with none is refused.
- `CI result` is the one aggregate job: `needs` every other job, `if: always()`, fails on any failed or cancelled need and passes on skipped. It must keep its name, because it is the required check of `next-release`. With it the shard names no longer matter (CI-18c), so the old "skipped inside the run" trick (a skipped matrix job reports one unexpanded name) is gone: a leg the plan leaves out is skipped as a whole.
- The bash 3.2 container job retries the image pull and `apk add` three times (CI-18b); a digest pin is left out because it cannot be checked offline.
- The label for a pull request is read when the run starts. Adding `test-macos` or `test-linux` later does not start a run (`labeled` is not a trigger: any label would cancel the run in progress), so push again or re-run the workflow.
- `homebrew-formula.yml` ran a sixth macOS job on every pull request that touched `lib/**` (CI-11). It now runs on pull requests only for the packaging files and the workflow itself, and on pushes to `next-release` and `main` for the rest, plus the nightly it always had.
- The plan checks that every selected test runs on at least one leg (`ci-plan.sh`). A test the Linux and bash 3.2 legs skip (the LAN share and guard tests) can be selected through a shared lib file (`state.sh`, `fl_same_path`) that `macos_paths` does not match; it then turns macOS on instead of leaving the test unrun under a green `CI result`. A test no leg can run, or a dispatch that names legs and leaves out the only one that can run it, fails the plan.
- A changed file that `affected.sh` cannot map to a test runs the whole suite. The files another job covers are listed (`macos/`, `windows/`, `examples/`, `site/`, `.github/`, `.claude/`, `install.ps1`, `scripts/app-*.py`, `*.md`), so a new kind of file is never skipped by default. `install.ps1` has no CLI test: `release-windows.yml` runs its installer tests on a pull request that changes it. `tests/share/*` selects `test-share-gateway`.
- `cancel-in-progress` is on for pull requests only. A push to `next-release` or `main` runs a full suite and must finish: the aim is a long run of green `next-release` pushes, and a merge that cancels the run of the one before it would hide a failure.
- A regression that only BSD tools show, in a lib file the pull request run leaves to Linux (`service.sh`, `freshness.sh`, `state.sh` and the like, which are not in `macos_paths`), surfaces on the push run after the merge, by design. That is the price of a pull request with no macOS job; the label `test-macos` runs macOS for a change that worries the author.

## Release branches

- Pull requests merge into `next-release`; `main` moves only when a release is cut, so it always equals the last release, and so do the one line installers and the docs site, which read from `main`. Work can land at any time without moving what users install.
- A release moves `main` to the head of `next-release` by a fast forward, pushed by the maintainer (a repository admin, who can bypass the `main` ruleset): no squash, no merge commit, no new commit. This overturns the first rule, "squash merge: the `main` ruleset requires a linear history and one commit per release reads well", and also the first plan of #132, a merge commit of `next-release` into `main` (the review of that plan found that it needs linear history off and merge commits on, settings the repository does not have, and that a merge commit is still a commit CI never ran). A squash made `main` carry one new commit that no review or CI run had seen, and `next-release` then needed a merge back. A fast forward makes `main` the very commit CI passed on `next-release`, and the two branches are equal afterwards, so nothing is merged back. The existing rulesets already allow it: `next-release` needs a pull request, squash only and a green "CI result" (admin bypass); `main` has linear history (admin bypass, which is how the maintainer pushes). Consequence: nothing but a release is pushed to `main`, because a commit only `main` has stops the fast forward; the release refuses and says so (docs/releasing.md).
- `scripts/release-promote.sh` is the one way a release moves `main`: it refuses unless `main` is an ancestor of `next-release` (it never forces; when `main` has only docs commits `next-release` lacks, a docs only pull request having targeted `main` by the owner's decision, it first makes one merge commit of `main` into `next-release` and pushes it as a fast forward of `next-release`, stopping on a conflict or when any of those commits changes more than docs, and never merges the other way), the version at that commit is final and agrees everywhere, its tag is new and the CI gate passes; it shows the plan, asks (or `--yes`), and runs `git push origin <sha>:refs/heads/main`. The checks run before the push because `auto-tag.yml` can only fail after it, with `main` already moved.
- `install.sh --version` pins the CLI checkout on a Mac for a release candidate and with `--no-app`, besides Linux (an earlier decision pinned the app only, because a tag leaves the managed checkout detached). A candidate is installed only when named, so there is no `git pull` to keep working; `--no-app` is the CLI only install, which Homebrew and `benchbar self-update` cannot pin; a run without `--version` switches back to main as before. An app update to a final release (Update Now passes `--version`) still leaves the CLI on main. `benchbar self-update` got no `--version`: the notes and docs/releasing.md say so.
- `auto-tag.yml` tags `vX.Y.Z` when a push to `main` brings a version that has no tag yet and dispatches `release.yml` and `release-windows.yml` with the tag as their ref: a tag pushed with GITHUB_TOKEN starts no workflow by itself. A push that keeps the version tags nothing. Before it tags it runs `scripts/check-version.sh --folded`, the one check of every place the version lives (benchbar, `macos/project.yml`, the bug report forms, the CHANGELOG section, the README and ROADMAP paragraphs, no changelog fragment left); `release.yml` and `tests/test-version-sources.sh` run the same script, so the two greps that once disagreed (`## X.Y.Z ` with a space in `release.yml`, `## X.Y.Z` with or without a date in `auto-tag.yml`) are one.
- `auto-tag.yml` pushes no branch. After the tag it only warns when `main` has commits that `next-release` has not. When the tag of the version exists already, a push to `main` that changes only docs (`scripts/docs-only.sh`, the same rule as `docs_paths` in `ci-plan.sh`, kept equal by `test-release-workflows`) is a notice; one that changes anything else fails with "release PR merged without a version bump?", because a notice there hid a release that built nothing.
- A release candidate is a tag with a suffix, `vX.Y.Z-rc.N`, tagged by hand on a `next-release` commit whose sources say `X.Y.Z-rc.N` (`scripts/bump-version.sh`). It is built, signed and drafted by the same workflows as a pre-release (`--prerelease --latest=false`), so every reader of GitHub's "latest" (Sparkle's feed, `install.sh`, `benchbar self-update`, the app's update check) ignores it without a change, and `homebrew-tap.yml` skips it before its job asks for the `release` environment. The sources carry the candidate's own version, not the base one, because `app-embed-cli.sh` and `cli-tarball.sh` require the app, the CLI and the tarball to say the same version. `main` never carries one: `auto-tag.yml` refuses it.
- The version grammar is strict and written once, `^(0|[1-9][0-9]*)\.(0|[1-9][0-9]*)\.(0|[1-9][0-9]*)(-rc\.[1-9][0-9]*)?$` in `scripts/check-version.sh`; `release.yml`, `homebrew-tap.yml` and `release-windows.yml` carry the same text (`tests/test-version-sources.sh` compares them). The old loose `[0-9]*.[0-9]*.[0-9]*` glob let `1.2.3 --draft` through. Only an explicit `--version` or `-Version` installs a candidate; the Windows installers accept the suffix there and `benchbar.exe` built from a candidate behaves as a development build (it never offers an update), to keep the shim's `X.Y.Z` assumptions as they are.
- The signed release job does not run the CLI suite or the Swift tests again: it checks that "CI result" passed for the tagged commit (`scripts/release-ci-gate.sh`, the check runs of the commit through `gh api`, with `checks: read`). A release is a fast forward, so the tagged commit is the one CI ran on `next-release`; the push to `main` starts a second run on it, which may still be going when the tag is made, so the last finished run decides and a run in progress does not undo it. That saves the 45 minutes the suite took in run 44096767885 (job total 58 minutes). An ad hoc test build from a branch has no verdict to lean on and still runs the CLI tests. A push that changes only docs, site or `*.md` (the rule of `ci-plan.sh`, `scripts/docs-only.sh`) skips every test job and a skipped job passes, so "CI result" on such a tip is green without tests: the gate walks back the first parent line over such commits and also needs a green result on the nearest commit that changed code (the review of #228 and #229 found a red code push followed by a docs push would have passed). An earlier version of this line said the earlier pushes ran the code; nothing checked it.
- The tag a signed build runs on must be on `main`, or on `next-release` for a candidate: the keys sign what the tag points at, and a commit that only lives on a pull request branch is refused (`release.yml`, "Check that the tagged commit is on main or next-release").
- `chk_fork_safety` reads each plist key and the line after it into a variable and tests it with `[[ ]]`, not `grep -A1 | grep -q`: under `pipefail` that pipeline failed when `grep -q` quit before the first grep finished writing, a false "Fork safety env" warning that failed adopt and repair at random (it showed up on the bash 3.2 shards). The other `producer | grep -q` checks have the same exposure and are listed for a follow up.

## Pipelines and locks

- No `producer | grep -q` (also `-qx`, `-qxF`, `-qi`, `-qiE`) in the shell sources: under `pipefail` it fails when grep quits at the first match and the producer then dies of SIGPIPE (141). The output is captured first, with `|| true` so a failing producer still reads as no match, and tested without a pipe: `fl_has_line TEXT LINE` for a whole line (`grep -qxF`), `[[ ]]` or `case` for the rest. `test-pipelines` fails on any that comes back.
- The case insensitive tests (`wkhtmltopdf --version`, the offline messages of a profile fetch) use `case` with both case patterns, or the text lowercased by `tr`, because bash 3.2 has no `${var,,}` and a here-string writes a temp file.
- A key value lock is never stale on age alone: a lock whose token PID is alive is not broken by a 10 second old (or future) mtime, because the mtime lies when the clock steps and a live holder can be stopped for seconds on a loaded runner. The waiter broke it, took the next lock, and the holder then renamed its older copy of the file over the new one, losing a key. The last resort break after 600 rounds waits 6000 when the holder is alive, and a holder renames only while its token is still in the lock, else it locks again and rebuilds.
- `test-port-management` sources `sudo.sh` where it sources `port-management.sh` alone, and asserts the output of the apply path has no "command not found": `fl_cmd_ports apply` asks `fl_sudo_gui`, which `benchbar` loads from `sudo.sh`, and the stray error was printed and ignored (a missing function reads as false).
- `producer | head -n N` becomes `| awk 'NR==1'` (or `NR<=N`) where the producer can write more than head reads (find, sort, ls, sed, git, a function printing a list): awk reads to the end, so nothing is left to die of SIGPIPE, and the call keeps one process. A first line of a captured variable is taken with `${v%%$'\n'*}`. The one `head -n 40` over a database dump in pull.sh stays, because reading the whole dump is the cost it avoids and its status is ignored.
- `auto-tag.yml` starts `release-windows.yml` with `wait_for_draft`, so it waits up to 130 minutes for `release.yml`'s draft as a tag push does. Without it the first auto tagged release (v0.8.1) failed: the Windows packages finished before the Mac draft existed, and a dispatched run only attaches to a draft that is already there.
- Share setup keeps the bench wide scheduler pause, with a marker. A pause of the copy alone is too late: frappe's restore writes a fresh site_config.json, so jobs could run on the copy while the data goes in. The marker (`share/scheduler-paused` in the state folder) is written before the pause and removed when it is lifted; the `share_scheduler` doctor check (action `share_resume`) flags a marker whose pause is still there, and the next `share setup` or `share start` resumes it first. A pause without the marker is the user's and is never touched.
- `lan-guard install` keeps the token `pfctl -E` prints, and whether pf was already enabled (`pfctl -s info`) before the first install, in `lan-guard/pf.state` in the state folder, never the bench. The root script prints both on stdout and the user side writes the file, so root never writes into a folder the user owns. `lan-guard remove` runs `pfctl -X TOKEN` for each recorded token, except when pf was already on: then pf stays on and nothing is released.
- lan-guard stays a global, unconditional pf rule: the bench ports and 3306 are refused from the LAN address even with no room open, which also refuses Docker and VM traffic that dials the LAN IP. The rule is not tied to a room, so it holds before `share start` and when a crash leaves no one to remove it. The cost is documented in the share reference ("What lan-guard blocks"); `lan-guard remove` is the way out, and `allow-node` is not one (it only changes the macOS application firewall).
- The CPace session id is empty. The channel identifier CI carries the per room salt (the one the invite carries), the join request id sits in ADb, and a recorded exchange does not replay: every attempt draws fresh scalars and the confirmation tags bind the TLS exporter of the connection. A non empty sid would only repeat what the salt and the request id already bind.
- Ten wrong codes lock the room for everyone until `share code --new`, not per guest: a per guest count would need a guest identity before the code is proven, which is what the code is for. The cost is a denial of service by anyone who can reach the gateway; the host sees the locked room, and a new code ends it. Documented in the share reference.
- Labs: experiments live behind one global `LABS` key the CLI owns; the app and MCP only read it; an agent can never turn one on (`labs on` exits 2 under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE`). One gate in the dispatcher hides and refuses the commands of a lab that is off, so a feature needs no check of its own.
- The lan-rooms lab gates `share` and `lan-guard` in the dispatcher (the lab row names both), so no share code checks the lab. `labs off lan-rooms` runs `fl_lab_off_lan_rooms`: the share stop path in a subshell with no prompt, and a warning (never sudo) naming `benchbar lan-guard remove` when the pf files are there. The `share_scheduler` doctor check runs only while the lab is on or `share/` exists in the state folder, so a Mac that never used sharing sees nothing of it.
- The lab gate runs after the JSON stream starts and before the non interactive preflight, so `share start --json` with the lab off is a done line with exit 2, not the preflight's "needs a person at a terminal" exit 1.
- The key-value lock (`fl__kv_lock`) does not trust `mkdir` alone: after its mkdir succeeds a writer puts its token in the folder and checks that it is the only one there, and backs off when it is not. On GitHub's Linux runners two writers have both seen `mkdir` succeed within milliseconds of the previous holder's `rmdir`, with both tokens ending up in one folder (test-run-lock, traced on 2026-11-04); one key was lost per collision. A token that cannot be written is no lock, and unlock without a token releases nothing.
- The share gateway lets an app front end through only when the copy itself declares it (`add_to_apps_screen` routes, and `website_route_rules` of the single page app form `/x/<path:app_path>` served by `x`, read by `signin.py routes` at share start; portal rules such as `/orders` or `/rfq/<path:name>` stay out) and the route is a plain slug that is not reserved (`app`, `api`, `assets`, `files`, `private`, `room`, `pair`, `console` and the single-page web routes). Frappe still checks every page and API call; a helper that fails gives no extra routes, never a wider allowlist.
- `copy_present` is a critical check (the job runs it every minute), not only part of the static `copy_ready`: a site drop or cleanup can remove the copy while sharing is on, and the web process keeps answering, so `web_up` alone never ends the share.
- A removal blocks the device only until the next code (`Room.rotate` clears the blocked list), not for the whole room: the new code reaches a removed person only if the host sends it, so a mistaken removal is undone with a new invite instead of stopping the room for everyone. `share remove --new-code` and an unknown intruder still end up with a code they do not have.
- Shim: a CLI newer than `benchbar.exe` is a warning with no action, because `cli_install` would take the CLI back to an older release. An older CLI makes the `cli` step `missing` with the same `cli_install` action and a `cli_version` field, so the tray can label it Update.
- Shim: a Docker Desktop, Rancher Desktop or Podman distro is never picked automatically, default or not; a configured distro is always honoured. The tray uses the same rule (`DistroName.Pick`).
- Shim: `distro_os` blocks every distro that is not Ubuntu 24.04, other Ubuntu releases included, because the Linux install supports 24.04 only.
- Shim: the firmware virtualization flag only warns, because `IsProcessorFeaturePresent` can be false while a hypervisor runs; the hard failure comes from `0x80370102` at install or boot.
- Shim: its own `wsl.exe` calls have a 60 s deadline (90 s when a distro may boot) and the user's forwarded command never does; a timeout is never read as "WSL is not installed", and the advice is to wait and retry before `wsl.exe --shutdown`.
- Shim: only `setup run` listens for Ctrl+C and console close, so forwarded commands keep the console's own Ctrl+C behaviour.
- Shim: Windows drive paths and same distro `\\wsl.localhost` paths in arguments become Linux paths, also after `--`, because `--` precedes bench paths and a drive path is never a valid Linux argument.
- Shim: `config.json` writes take the named mutex `Local\BenchBarConfig` (existence based, never waited on), and the tray takes the same one; `adopt-distro NAME` replaces an invalid file and keeps the old text as `config.json.invalid`.
- Shim: the installer line carries `set -o pipefail`, so a 404 from curl is the exit code; curl hints apply only when curl printed its own `curl: (N)` line.
- install.ps1 reads Smart App Control and refuses to install while it is on, because an unsigned program is blocked with no per app exception and an install that cannot start helps nobody.
- install.ps1 removes the Zone.Identifier mark from the installed files after the SHA256SUMS check, because the copy keeps the mark and the checksum is the trust anchor.
- install.ps1 `-Uninstall` edits the user PATH itself and does not call `benchbar.exe path remove`, because an older installed exe would forward that to the distro. It removes a shortcut only when it is a `.lnk` in the Start menu, Startup or install folder whose target is the installed tray, and with no terminal it does nothing without `-Yes`.
- install.ps1 takes the newest published release that has the Windows zip for this CPU, not `releases/latest`, because Windows zips can be attached later than the release.
- A non release Windows build gets the shim version `0.0.0-dev-SHA`, so it never pins the distro CLI install to a tag that does not exist.
- SignPath Foundation declined the signing application on 2026-11-13 (the project needs more public adoption first). The dormant signing steps were removed from `release-windows.yml` (issue 135): the Windows zips ship unsigned, the Microsoft Store submission is dropped from 0.9.0 (the MSIX package code still lands and CI builds it), and the user docs say so plainly, with the command line inside WSL as the route when Smart App Control is on. User docs promise no date.
- `.gitattributes` keeps shell scripts, templates and `install.ps1` at LF, because `install.ps1` is served raw from GitHub and works that way today.
- Linux install preflight (`preflight-linux.sh`): blockers stop before sudo, warnings only inform. github.com blocks; the apt mirror, pypi, npm, nodejs.org and astral.sh warn, because curl does not know apt's own proxy or a file mirror.
- A Windows listener is seen with `netstat.exe -ano` (all TCP, language independent) and a 127.0.0.1 connect; a port that also listens in the distro is never a Windows holder (wslrelay).
- A half built bench is moved aside by install only when `apps/` holds nothing but `frappe` and no site exists; anything else keeps the stop with the repair hint.
- Lingering is enabled before phase 00, while the sudo credential still exists.
- An apt proxy travels in a 0600 `APT_CONFIG` file, never on a command line, because proxy URLs can hold credentials.
- The wkhtmltopdf arm64 row is trusted from the release asset (the pinned sha256 was computed from the download, the same way the amd64 one reproduces); not tested on real arm64 hardware.
- Shim: `keepalive remove` leaves the task alone when it runs another existing copy of `benchbar.exe`, because the task has one fixed name and an uninstall of a second install must not remove the first one's keepalive.
- The Windows port check starts `netstat.exe` from `/`, because on some WSL builds a Windows program fails with "Invalid argument" when the working directory is a folder under the Linux home (seen on Windows build 26320 with WSL 3.0.5).
- Doctor's "is MariaDB running" test is whether 3306 listens, not whether the listener's pid is visible: `ss` hides the owner of the apt `mariadbd` (user `mysql`) from a regular user (found by the first full install on a real WSL distro).
- Windows self-update runs the one line installer instead of a downloader in Go, because the installer already verifies the release zip against its SHA256 sums, writes files without the internet mark (no SmartScreen dialog), moves files in use aside and restarts the tray.
- Windows self-update fetches main's `install.ps1`, not the release tag's, and pins `-Version`: the installed copy and an old tag lack later installer fixes, and main's installer can install any release, also an older one.
- Windows self-update starts the installer as `[scriptblock]::Create((irm URL))` with its parameters on the command line, so a Restricted execution policy does not stop it and no variable is set in the user's shell; the `install.ps1` variables a shell may still hold (`BENCHBAR_UNINSTALL` and the rest) are removed from the child's environment.
- `self-update --check` finds the latest release with one HTTPS request from Go (env proxy, then the Internet Options proxy), not by starting PowerShell, and mirrors install.ps1's choice (published, not a prerelease, has the zip for this CPU); a proxy auto config script or a proxy that wants the Windows sign in is not supported there, and `--version X.Y.Z` still works through such a proxy because only the installer downloads.
- `self-update --check` asks the CLI in WSL only when the distro already runs, so a poll from the tray never boots WSL.
- After a Windows update the CLI part runs through the new `benchbar.exe` (`setup run cli_install`), not the old process, so the CLI is pinned to the release that was just installed.
- After a Windows update, a keepalive task of this install is moved to the new program with the new `benchbar.exe keepalive install`; the keepalive run check accepts `benchbar.exe.old`, the name the installer gives a running exe it replaced.
- The Microsoft Store case of self-update is recognised by the exe path (`\WindowsApps\`), because the package detection lives on the MSIX branch; switch to it when that merges.
- `self-update` starts Windows PowerShell 5.1 without `PSModulePath` (any case of the name): PowerShell 7 sets it to its own module folders, and 5.1 started with that value cannot load its own modules (`Get-FileHash` is not recognized); without it 5.1 builds its default. Every start of powershell.exe in the shim goes through `powerShellEnv`.
- install.ps1 replaces the installed files all or nothing: phase 1 renames every file it replaces or removes to NAME.old (NAME.N.old when taken) inside the install folder, phase 2 copies the new files in; a failure in either phase deletes the new files and renames every backup back before anything else runs, and only a rollback that itself fails reports a mixed install, with the one line installer as the repair. A failed run tells `self-update` in the file named by `BENCHBAR_INSTALL_RESULT` (unchanged, mixed, changed or unknown, then the error line), so the shim keeps streaming the installer's output to the console as it is.
- The shim hands its Windows folder to the distro in `BENCHBAR_SHIM_CWD` and the `sh` wrapper converts it with `wslpath -a -u`, instead of converting it in Go: only the distro knows `[automount] root`, and the wrapper falls back to the home folder when the folder is not there (a folder of another distro, a drive WSL does not mount). Every fallback prints one line on stderr (`benchbar.exe: <folder> has no path in WSL, running in ~`), never on stdout: a silent fallback made `adopt .` and `ports plan -- .` act on $HOME. Drive paths in arguments are converted in Go with the mount that `wslpath` reported (one extra call, only when an argument holds a drive path), `/mnt/x` when it fails.
- `benchbar.exe doctor` with `--json`, `--jsonl`, `--for-agent` or `--fix-hints` adds the Windows checks to the CLI's output (a `windows_checks` array at the end of the document, or the fix lines after the CLI's) and never prints the human WINDOWS block, so stdout is one document for a program. The array is spliced into the CLI's bytes, not parsed and written again, so the CLI's key order is kept; output that is not one JSON object passes through unchanged. With `--jsonl` (several lines) the array goes into the `done` line, which holds the document's keys.
- Speed of `app list` and `profile check` (#146): the facts of an app (branch, remote, upstream, dirty, commit, shallow) are read once per app, in at most 8 side by side jobs (`FL_AF_JOBS`), and focus, behind and the JSON reuse them; the author search for the focus apps is limited to `FL_FOCUS_DAYS`. `profile check` asks each repo and branch once, 8 at a time (`FL_PROFILE_JOBS`), and keeps a yes for `FL_PROFILE_REACH_TTL` seconds (300) in the state folder (0600, repo URLs without user info) so `check` and `install --profile` do not ask the same repo twice; a no is never kept, and `install` still asks every repo again (`fl_validate_app_refs_online`) before its plan, so a branch deleted inside the window stops it with a message. The pool refills a slot when any job ends (a poll of the job pids: bash 3.2 has no `wait -n`) and runs in the shell that traps INT and TERM, not in a `$( )`, so `fl_on_signal` can stop the jobs (`FL_BG_PIDS`) and each job's own git group (`fl_bg_job_stop`). The per app files (apps.tsv policy, sites/apps.json version, hooks.py `required_apps`, the site lists) are read once per bench by `fl_app_prefetch` when the facts load: a subshell of a big bash costs more per app than the awk it ran. Every read of a remote goes through `fl_git_remote_*` (`run.sh`): ssh connect limit `FL_GIT_CONNECT_SECS` (5), and a timeout ends git with its whole process group.
- The tray's recorded CLI output (`windows/tray/fixtures`) is checked by `tests/test-tray-fixtures.sh` through `tests/record-tray-fixtures.sh --check`: four files are generated from the CLI under the mocks (as the Linux CLI of WSL, paths rewritten to `/home/akash`) and compared byte for byte; the hand shaped scenario files are only checked for fields the CLI no longer prints, since the mocks cannot make an older CLI, a refusal or a future check level. A CLI field added is therefore never a failure there, a removal or rename is, and a version bump needs a regeneration (docs/releasing.md).
- A `memory` value in `.wslconfig` that the user set is never changed by `wslconfig --apply` or the setup step, only reported by the idle check: it is their limit for a small PC. Only an unset value on a PC with less than 8 GB gets the 4GB suggestion.
- The shim does not print a "starting WSL" notice after a delay: its stdout and stderr are the child's own handles (no copy loop, a terminal stays a terminal for the CLI), so it cannot tell whether the CLI has printed. The version skew line after an exit 2 needs no such knowledge.

- The gateway tests register every server and child they start (`track` in `tests/share/helpers.js`) and close them in an `after` hook, and `writeDevices` renames a temp file into place like the device store does: the gateway reads `devices.json` on every tick, and a plain write let a tick see an empty file and die with `fatal schema`, which left the upstream server open and hung the file until the 600 s limit.

- The Linux install supports Ubuntu 24.04 only. Debian 12 ships MariaDB 10.11 but nothing tests it, so it is stopped like Ubuntu 22.04 (10.6) and Debian 13 (11.8), before sudo and any package; `--allow-unsupported-distro` is the documented way to try.
- `install` stops, before any change, when the systemd user manager cannot be reached after lingering was tried, and a dry run only warns: a bench with no loaded unit is not a finished install, and the failure used to come after the bench and the site were built. `status --json` keeps `agent_loaded` a bool (false when unknown); only the text says `unknown (no user bus)`.
- A step that ended `failed` makes its install phase `failed`; `warning` is only for a phase whose actions worked and whose verify pass still has findings. Both exit 1.
- The tools `sudo` runs on macOS are named by absolute path (`fl_root_tool`), and `sudo` itself is a function (`lib/frappe-local/sudo.sh`) that runs `/usr/bin/sudo`, so a `sudo` planted in a folder of the user's `PATH` is never found and no call site changes. `/etc/hosts`, the wkhtmltopdf paths and `FL_SUDO_BIN` (the program the function runs) come from the environment only when `FL_TESTING=1`, the one switch for test seams. Linux keeps the `PATH` lookup: Ubuntu's sudo has a `secure_path`, and the `BENCHBAR_SUDO=env` wrapper names its own binary.

## Backups and state limits (0.9)

- Keeping a site's older backups: frappe's `delete_temp_backups` reads only `keep_backups_for_hours` from the site or common config (no environment override, and `--backup-path` does not move its target; 0 falls back to 23). Setting it would change the user's `site_config.json` for good, so every benchbar call that runs bench's backup (`site backup`, the backup first of `site restore`, `site drop`, `pull --replace`, `app update`) instead hard links the folder's files under `<state>/backups/<stamp>/kept-sets/<site>/` first and links back what bench deleted (a copy after a free space check when links are impossible; the run stops before bench starts when neither works). `site drop` puts them back in `archived/sites/NAME/private/backups`. benchbar prunes no site backup. Not overturned: the `restore-set` copy of `site restore` stays as a second guard.
- While bench runs the kept copy can be the only copy, so it is protected on every path: each kept folder holds `.from` (where its files came from); benchbar's EXIT trap puts them back on a signal (INT, TERM and HUP end the shell through `exit`); a kill -9, a failed put back and a site drop that found no archive leave the folder, which `fl_state_prune` never removes (it counts toward the 50 but is skipped), which the next `fl_backups_keep_begin` puts back first (into `.from`, else the newest archived folder of that site), and which doctor (`kept_backups`) warns about until `repair` or a later backup has put it back. A file whose name is taken in the target by a different file is left in both places. The copy fallback reads `df` once before copying and compares it with the whole size; adding the copied bytes to a free space that already counts them was a second count.
- A hard link shares its inode, so linking a file changes its `st_ctime`, which frappe's `is_file_old` reads (not the mtime): an old set looks recent for another `keep_backups_for_hours`, and frappe's own scheduled non forced backup may skip one run. Accepted: the alternative (a copy every time) costs the disk space the links save, and the files are only ever kept, never lost.
- A backup set is read before the old data is replaced: `gzip -t` on `*.sql.gz` and `*.tgz`, `tar -tf` on archives (only the archives that are restored), size above zero for encrypted files (no key to read them). The dump is not searched for mysqldump's trailer: frappe's dump options do not promise it. `site restore` checks in the plan and again at apply; `pull` checks the local files before its first step (after decrypt for an encrypted set). `site backups` marks `complete` from sizes only, so a listing never reads every dump.
- State folder limits are counts, not ages, and apply at the start of a run that makes a log: 100 run logs, 50 backup stamp folders (one that holds a `restore-set` stays), 14 update checks, `state.env.XXXXXX` files older than a day. A run that removed something says so in `changed[]` (kind `pruned`).
- `bench.previous.log` stays the newest copy (the app and `logs --previous` read it); the three copies before it are `bench.previous.1.log` to `.3.log`.
- The runner's heartbeat loop cuts `bench.log`, `worker.log` and `worker.error.log` in place when one passes 20 MB (`BENCHBAR_LOG_MAX_KB`), checked every 10th beat (about 5 minutes, one `wc` per log, so the heartbeat stays cheap). It keeps the last 2000 lines (and at most a quarter of the cap) and puts one line first that says so. In place, because launchd and systemd append to the same file: a rename would leave the live log empty. A line written between the copy and the cut can be lost. The `.old` copies of `repair` stay for a log that is already large when the runner starts, and the runner template is now version 9.
- A pull request adds a changelog fragment (`changelog.d/<issue>-<slug>.md`) and does not edit `CHANGELOG.md`; `scripts/changelog-fold.sh` folds them into `## Unreleased` at release time, because every merge into `next-release` otherwise conflicted on that one block and reran CI. Sections are a fixed list in the script and in `changelog.d/README.md`, so a typo in a heading fails `--check` instead of making a new section; entries already in Unreleased stay where they are.


- Test seams (`FL_KILL_CMD`, `FL_CONFIG_DIR`, `FL_TEMPLATE_DIR`, `FL_OSASCRIPT`, `FL_NOW`, `FL_LOCAL_PROBES`, `FL_EFFECTIVE_UID`, `FL_LOCK_GRACE_SECS`, and the runner's `BENCHBAR_KILL_CMD`) take effect only when `FL_TESTING=1`; the test harness exports it and a production run ignores them, so a variable in the environment cannot change what benchbar stops, renders, runs or believes (CORE-25). The runner's timing variables (`BENCHBAR_PING_*`, `BENCHBAR_HEARTBEAT_EVERY`, `BENCHBAR_STATE_LOG`, `BENCHBAR_SHARE_*`) and the Linux path seams (`FL_PROC_DIR` and its kin) are not behind it yet.
- The legacy agent finder lists a LaunchAgent that is not `com.benchbar.*` or `com.frappe-mac.*` only when its plist names this bench's folder and runs honcho, an old or new runner, socketio, or `bench` with serve, worker, schedule, socketio or start (`fl_plist_runs_this_bench`). The old rule, any plist that says "frappe" or "honcho" anywhere, moved a person's own backup or VPN agent aside on `repair` (SVC-13).
- Lean start (0.9.0): `path`, `commands`, `where`, `list`, `status`, `--version` and `--help` (only as the first argument) skip the libraries of the heavy commands (doctor, repair, share, pull, profiles, app plans, templates and others, listed in `benchbar`). A command that needs a skipped function fails with "command not found", so `tests/test-lean-load.sh` compares each lean command with `BENCHBAR_LOAD_ALL=1`; moving a call into a lean command means moving its function into a lean library (as `fl_site_ping_code` and `fl_lock_file_resolve` moved to benchstate.sh and `fl_cli_standalone_script` to selfupdate.sh). A lean command needs a library guard (`declare -F`) never: the test runs each one under several environments. `--profile` anywhere in the arguments turns the lean start off, so the profile name is still checked. The exit trap gets no-op stubs of the functions it calls from skipped libraries. A late `source` was rejected: it would put checks.sh over the Linux layer's functions.
- Deferred render (0.9.0): `fl_context_init` no longer renders the service files. `fl_render_ensure` renders them at the first read, and every function that reads `FL_R_RUNNER`, `FL_R_PLIST`, `FL_R_PROCFILE`, `FL_R_LISTEN_PY`, `FL_R_LISTEN_JS` or `FL_R_HELPERS` calls it first (the template checks, `fl_listen_files_stale` and the four `act_write_*`). Until then the six variables are unset, not empty: a reader that forgets the call stops the run under `set -u` and can never compare with, or write, an empty file. The render was about 230 ms of every call that is not lean (`down --dry-run` 362 ms with it, 130 ms without), and only doctor, repair, service, adopt, install and `up` read it. An explicit `fl_render_all` after a change of ports, site, scheduler or listen mode stays as it was.
- The wait of `fl_run_long` and `fl_run_with_timeout` pauses 20 ms five times, then 100 ms until three seconds have passed, then 500 ms (`fl_run_poll_pause`). The look at the command's state (`ps`, for a command stopped at a terminal read) happens at most once a second and not in the first second. A fixed `sleep 1` made every command cost a full second: 40 percent of the wall time of `test-pull` and `test-apps`, and up to a second per bench step for a person. `tests/test-core-reliability.sh` pins the first pauses.
- `fl_template_render` keeps the lines in an array and joins them once, and puts the header into the lines that hold `__HEADER__`. Appending to one string and substituting the header over the whole body cost 94 ms for the runner template under bash 3.2 in a UTF-8 locale (the substitution alone 20 to 260 ms for 11 KB). The output must stay byte equal, or every installed file would read as outdated.
- The state folder migration (`state.sh`, CORE-20) stays on the start path: it is globs and `[[ -d ]]` tests, no process, so it costs microseconds; removing it needs a release policy for old state folders, which is the owner's call.
- Linux crash notification (#176): the systemd runner calls `notify-send` over the session bus (`$DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS`, else the socket `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/bus`, which a systemd user unit has) and only when that socket exists. A headless server can have a bus too (Ubuntu Server ships dbus-user-session, and a lingering user has `/run/user/UID/bus`): what keeps it quiet is that notify-send is usually not installed there, its output goes to /dev/null and nothing is written to the log, and `timeout 5` bounds the call. In WSL the body stays `:`: the tray polls `status` and toasts a `crashed` or `paused` snapshot (windows/tray/DECISIONS.md), so a toast from the distro as well would show every crash twice. The body is chosen when the runner is rendered (`fl_is_wsl`), so a runner written before this change is outdated for doctor and `repair` rewrites it. The pause is recorded in `state.json` and the log before the notification, so a headless server and the tray see it whatever notify-send does.
- Linux profiles (#176): the Linux layer no longer refuses everything but v15-lts. Python and Node come from uv and fnm for any series (Python 3.10 and newer, Node 18 and newer), so only MariaDB can rule a profile out: it is the version apt gives (10.11 on Ubuntu 24.04, `FL_LINUX_APT_MARIADB`). A profile whose own MariaDB differs (v16-lts names 11.8) takes apt's when it is inside the profile's range (v16: 10.6 to 11.8), as the Mac takes a running server in range, and `FL_MARIADB_SOURCE` says `distro`. A bench pin still wins. A running server does not: on Linux `fl_mariadb_running_formula` recognises only Homebrew paths, so an apt or vendor `mariadbd` is not detected and a vendor MariaDB 11.x that v16 accepts still gets phase 00's version message (nothing breaks; apt aware detection is a later change). A profile whose range excludes apt's version is refused before any step, with the three numbers in the message. A vendor repository for MariaDB 11.8 is not added: it would change the user's apt sources. Evidence for v16 on 10.11: the v16 profile's range starts at 10.6, a v16 bench ran on the mariadb@10.11 server on the Mac (DECISIONS, 0.4 run), and uv and fnm ship Python 3.14 and Node 24 for linux x86_64 and aarch64. This part of the change was run only against mocks; an Ubuntu 24.04 run of `benchbar install --profile v16-lts` is still to be seen.
- Labs (#176): `fl_lab_on` is false for a lab the platform does not list, so the `BENCHBAR_LABS` override and a state file copied from a Mac can no longer turn on `lan-rooms` on Linux (its commands run pf and launchctl code with no platform check of their own). A refused command says "NAME is not available on PLATFORM yet" instead of the hint to run `labs on`, which refuses too. `scripts/docs-reference.py` pins `FL_PLATFORM=macos` so the generated tables list every lab's commands on any host.


## Release keys
- The ten release secrets live in the `release` environment (v* tags only, the maintainer as required reviewer), and only the jobs that read one declare `environment: release`: `build-signed` in release.yml and `tap` in homebrew-tap.yml. A pull request run never names the environment. `tests/test-release-workflows.sh` enforces both directions.
- release.yml is split into `check` (no secret), `build-signed` (environment) and `build-adhoc` (no environment, no secret): an environment cannot be switched off per run, and an ad hoc test build from a branch must not need approval or touch the keys. The steps before and after the build are two composite actions, `release-prepare` and `release-draft`, which take no secret.
- A v* tag always builds signed and fails when a secret is missing; ad hoc runs only for a dispatch with `adhoc` on. Overturns the 0.6.1 rule that missing secrets mean an ad hoc release.
- The certificate is imported with `-T /usr/bin/codesign`, not `-A`, in the step that builds, and deleted by an `always()` step before the draft step. `macos-release.sh` takes the notary and Sparkle keys as content (`NOTARY_KEY_BASE64`, `SPARKLE_ED_KEY`), unsets them at once and writes each to a 0600 file only around `notarytool` and `generate_appcast`. The checkout uses `persist-credentials: false`. The path variables stay for local runs.
- Nothing in a workflow edits a published release: `release-draft` and `release-windows.yml` fail on one, and on a draft they `gh release delete-asset` a file of the same name before `gh release upload`, with no `--clobber`. The "Immutable releases" setting is on, so the platform refuses the same edit too; the workflow says why before it gets there. Overturns the Stage 4 rule that uploads use `--clobber`.
- The tap pull request is opened by homebrew-tap.yml and merged by a person after reading it; the workflow no longer runs `gh pr merge --auto`. The tap token still acts as the owner's account and could merge a tap PR: closing that needs a second identity (a GitHub App), a residual risk. (Replaces the 0.7.0 rule that the tap pull request merges itself.)
- An `adhoc` run is refused on a `refs/tags/v*` ref, so an ad hoc build cannot draft a release for a release tag.
- The keychain keeps `-lut 3600`: the build signs, waits for notarization (up to 50 minutes) and signs again, so a shorter lock would fail a slow notarization. Key files from content go into one folder (`BENCHBAR_KEY_DIR`) that the `always()` step removes, whatever the script left behind.
- homebrew-tap.yml clones the tap without the token and gives it to `git push` on one command line, so no credential is in the clone's config while brew runs formula code.
- The Windows package job runs for pull requests too and has only `contents: read`. No signing secret is read anywhere in the workflow.

## Verified downloads

- Downloads are verified by where they were built, not only by a checksum from the same release (#134): `release.yml` has an `attest` job after `build-signed` that attests the DMG, the app zip, the CLI tarball and `SHA256SUMS`, and it alone has `id-token` and `attestations` write. It runs on the signed (tag) path only: an ad hoc run is refused on a tag, so nothing ad hoc is attested as a release. `homebrew-tap.yml` runs `gh attestation verify` on the files and `SHA256SUMS` before it renders anything, except for a run by hand for a version below 0.9.0, which has no attestation: it says so and checks the sums only.
- `install.sh` refuses when `gh attestation verify` fails, and says only the checksum was checked when `gh` is missing or signed out: most Macs have no `gh`, so a hard requirement would make the one line install unusable. A release before 0.9.0 has no attestation (`gh` answers HTTP 404 or "no attestations found"): for those the installer says only the checksum was checked and goes on, so `install.sh --version v0.8.3` and an upgrade from it still work with `gh` signed in. For 0.9.0 and later, and for any other `gh` error, it still refuses. Each `gh` call has a 30 s deadline (a `with_deadline` helper, since macOS has no `timeout`) and then the same fallback; the deadline is shortened by `BENCHBAR_ATTEST_TIMEOUT` only under `FL_TESTING=1`. The signer is pinned (`--signer-workflow askysh/benchbar/.github/workflows/release.yml` for the Mac files, `release-windows.yml` for the zips) so a file attested by another workflow of the repository does not pass.
- `install.sh` runs `codesign --verify --deep --strict` and `spctl --assess --type execute` on the unpacked app and refuses on either: `curl` sets no quarantine flag, so Gatekeeper never assessed the app. The running app is quit only after both checks passed, so a refused app leaves it running. The app is copied to `BenchBar.app.new`, the old one renamed to `.old`, the new one renamed in and the old one removed, so a failed copy leaves the old app. On Linux it downloads no file (git is the transport), so there is nothing to attest there.
- `install.ps1` keeps the internet mark unless `gh attestation verify` accepts the zip (a run from an unpacked folder has no zip: mark kept). Only a verified provenance proves more than the same-origin checksum, which was the reason the mark used to go. A failed or unavailable check does not stop the install, as the checksum did pass; it only keeps the mark and says so.
- `install.ps1` always names a newer release it skipped for lack of Windows files; the test seam `BENCHBAR_TEST_RELEASES` (a JSON file for the release list) lets CI prove it without the network.
- The sums files carry `install.sh` and `install.ps1` of the tag (`macos-release.sh` and `release-local.sh` append them, `release-windows.yml` adds `install.ps1` to the Windows sums), so `self-update` can check an installer against the release it belongs to.
- `self-update` (CLI and `benchbar.exe`) runs the installer of the release it installs, not `main`'s: the file is downloaded to a temporary one, its sha256 must equal the `install.sh` (`install.ps1`) line of that release's sums, and only then it runs. A release cut before the sums listed the installers has no such line and is refused, with the way to update by hand. The same sums are same origin as the installer, so this finds a swapped or changed file in transit and a `main` that moved, not a hijacked release; the attestations cover that for a person who checks.
- `BENCHBAR_API` and `BENCHBAR_INSTALLER_URL` are read by `self-update` only under `FL_TESTING=1` (the test seam rule): a left over variable in a shell cannot point an update at another server. `install.sh` itself still reads `BENCHBAR_API` and `BENCHBAR_REPO` (it is the installer, run by hand), so `self-update` starts it with `env -u BENCHBAR_API -u BENCHBAR_REPO` unless `FL_TESTING=1`: a variable left in the shell reaches neither the release lookup nor the clone of an update.
- On a Mac `install.sh --version` pins only the app, so `self-update` checks the CLI out at the tag itself after the installer succeeded (`git fetch --tags`, `git checkout vX.Y.Z`, a detached HEAD that the installer's next run leaves for `main`). A failed checkout (local changes) says so, names the command and exits 1 with the app already updated. A developer checkout and any other CLI stay `--app-only` and are never touched.
- The line that installs the CLI inside WSL (`installerPipe`: the wizard's `cli_install` step and the fix lines of `doctor` and `adopt`) is the same download, check, run for a release build: install.sh of the shim's own tag goes to `~/.cache/benchbar-install`, its sha256 must be the `install.sh` line of that release's `SHA256SUMS` (`sha256sum -c`, always there on Ubuntu), then `bash install.sh --version vX.Y.Z`. The line has no double quote, dollar sign or backtick, because it is pasted inside `bash -c "..."` into cmd and PowerShell. The first download heads an `&&` chain so a 404 is still curl's exit 22 for the hints. A dev build has no release to check against and still pipes main's installer. The tray's copy of the line (`WizardModel.LinuxCliInstallLine`, shown to copy by hand) is unchanged.
- `benchbar.exe self-update --version 0.8.x` is refused before anything runs, because releases before 0.9.0 do not list `install.ps1` in their checksums: the message says that plainly and prints the installer line to run by hand (unchecked, said so). It is not a retry case.
- The shim downloads the installer and runs it as `Get-Content -Raw -LiteralPath FILE` inside a script block, as before for `--installer FILE`: still no execution policy problem, and nothing is piped from the network into PowerShell. The temporary folder is removed when the run ends; the retry command names `--installer` only when the user gave it.
- The Mac app's own installer strings (`WizardViews.installerLine`, the update command) keep the short `curl -fsSL` form: the wizard snapshots hold it, and they cannot be regenerated without a Mac.

## 0.9.0: the bench listens on loopback by default

- The dev web server and socketio listen on 127.0.0.1 by default on macOS and Linux (WSL included) and `benchbar listen all` is the switch back (a per bench `LISTEN` key, only `all` is stored). Frappe v15 and v16 hardcode `run_simple("0.0.0.0", ...)` in `frappe.app.serve` and call `server.listen(port)` with no host in `realtime/index.js`, so there is no setting to use; a firewall was the other option (issue 117), but pf and nftables need root and cover only what the person installs, while a loopback bind is the default for everyone and needs no sudo.
- The bind is made by two small files rendered from templates into `config/benchbar-listen`, started through the Procfile line (`PYTHONPATH="$PWD/config/benchbar-listen..."` for the web line, `NODE_OPTIONS="--require \"$PWD/config/benchbar-listen/socketio.js\" ..."` for socketio) and not through a wrapper command: the process stays `python -m frappe.utils.bench_helper frappe serve` and `node apps/frappe/socketio.js`, so the runner's cleanup, `orphans` and the stop patterns keep matching, and werkzeug's reloader (which starts `sys.executable -m ...` again with the same environment) keeps working. The paths are absolute (`$PWD` is the bench folder, where honcho starts the line) because `bench serve` changes into `sites/` before it starts the frappe child (bench/cli.py), and a relative `PYTHONPATH` entry would then name `sites/config/benchbar-listen`: Python finds no `sitecustomize` there and the dev server stays on 0.0.0.0. `$PWD` is expanded by the shell of the Procfile line, so a bench path with spaces works and a moved bench needs no rewrite. `bench` itself starts as `python -E`, which ignores PYTHONPATH, but that is the bench entry point only: the frappe child it starts inherits the environment and so sees the file.
- `Procfile.lean` in `all` mode renders the same two lines as before 0.9.0 and writes no listen files; the version is 2 either way, so every existing bench shows `procfile` outdated once after the update and `repair` (or `service`) rewrites it. A running bench keeps listening as before until its next restart, and `bench_listen` warns about that (fix: restart). A stopped bench whose Procfile is the old one is not reported as fine either: its next start binds every interface, so `bench_listen` warns with `repair` as the fix.
- `listen all` is refused without a person (`--for-agent`, `mcp`, `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE`) and asks first: it puts the debugger on the network. `listen local` is always allowed.
- `bench_listen` warns when the setting says `all` (even while the bench is stopped) and when a listener on the web or socketio port is not on loopback with the setting at `local`. It looks at the two ports only: Redis and MariaDB have their own bind and checks. The Python realtime server (`socketio_backend: python`, uvicorn with host `0.0.0.0` in `frappe/realtime/server.py`) is not patched: that would mean importing uvicorn before gevent has patched the standard library, which no bench of this machine could test. Doctor reports it as a backend benchbar does not cover yet, and names the switch to the node server as the fix, not `restart`: a restart starts the same uvicorn on the same host.
- WSL: a Windows browser reaches a port that listens on 127.0.0.1 in the distro through WSL's localhost forwarding (the `localhostForwarding` setting of `[wsl2]` forwards ports bound to the wildcard or to localhost, and mirrored networking shares the loopback), and docs/windows.md already tells people to use `*.localhost` names for that path. Nothing in the shim, the tray or doctor dials the distro's own address (checked by search), so nothing else needed a change.
