- The share gateway listens on this Mac's address on the default route's interface (`SP_LAN_IP`, the address `share preflight` already probes and the invite already names), not on 0.0.0.0 (SHARE-7). It does not rebind: an address change makes `gateway_reachable` fail in the watchdog's next tick, with a message that names the old and the new address, and the room ends. Rebinding while guests are connected would drop their sessions, so ending the room and starting it again is the honest behavior. IPv6 was never served. The gateway reaches the share's web process on 127.0.0.1, as before. The room's site and host labels in `/room/hello` and Bonjour are left for a next change.
- Microsoft Store package (MSIX, shim part, issue 135): the data folder of a packaged `benchbar.exe` is the physical `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Packages\<family>\LocalCache\Local\BenchBar`, opened directly, so every printed path is true and nothing diverges; the old `%LOCALAPPDATA%\BenchBar\config.json` is read once and never written from a package.
- A package never puts its versioned `WindowsApps\<full name>` folder on PATH or in the keepalive task: both use the app execution alias, which survives updates. Alias existence is tested with `Lstat`, because `Stat` on an alias is refused.
- A keepalive task that points into any versioned folder of this package family counts as ours and stale; `keepalive install` refuses when the alias is turned off, because the task would fail at logon.
- A package needs no self update code path: `self-update` already reports a Store copy as `store` and changes nothing, and `doctor` names the Store as the way to update. The CLI installer inside WSL is unchanged.
- `doctor` in a package skips the .NET Desktop Runtime and Startup shortcut checks: the packaged tray carries its runtime and starts through the package's startup task.
- The "two installs" fix line runs the installer as a script block with `-Uninstall`, not with `BENCHBAR_UNINSTALL` set, because the variable would stay in that PowerShell window and turn the next install into an uninstall.
- `benchbar.exe keepalive start` starts an installed keepalive task whose run has ended and never creates one: a package update stops the run cleanly, so the task's restart on failure never fires, and the tray calls it when Windows restarts the tray after the update.
- A process is packaged only when it has package identity and its executable path contains `\WindowsApps\` (any case), the same test `installKind` uses for "store". Identity alone is inherited by children: a zip `benchbar.exe` started by a packaged launcher (seen with a Raycast child) would otherwise use the package data folder and skip `path install`.
- `app add NAME` for a name no profile lists takes the folder in `apps/` (its remote), else the `url` and branch of its unfinished add record, before it asks the profile; only then is the name unknown. A record URL with a user name or token is never used. `profile list --json` runs the Linux profile gate per profile in a subshell with the MariaDB source reset to `profile`, so it answers for a fresh machine, like `install`.
- `windows/msix/build-msix.ps1` holds BenchBar's Store identity (Name, Publisher) as defaults for Store mode: they are public in every installed package, and one source keeps the workflow and a local build equal. `msix.yml` builds the package and uploads it as an artifact; it never signs with a real certificate and never publishes. The Store submission is dropped from 0.9.0 (issue 135), so nothing runs it on a release.
- Uninstall leftovers and platforms (#155): `uninstall-service --all` offers the `/etc/hosts` block only to a person at a terminal (default no, sudo, backup first); `--yes`, a mode that cannot ask, and the app's `BENCHBAR_SUDO=gui` print the `sed` command instead (a dry run only says it would ask), so a script never edits the file (AGENTS.md keeps sudo for a person's decision). It does not remove the lines with the services: a bench kept and started by hand still needs them. An Intel Mac check on real hardware is dropped from 0.9.0 and said so in the docs and the roadmap; the code side (`fl_brew_default_prefix`, `/usr/local` on x86_64, the installer's macOS 14 check that skips only the app) is covered by the mocks. The installer's own `PATH` block stays in `~/.zshrc` on macOS for bash users too, because `--uninstall` looks only in that file; a bash or fish user is told the line to add instead.
- `pull --apply TOKEN --yes` runs the plan `pull --dry-run --json` printed. The token is a hash of the plan line without `dry_run`, `bytes` and `age_hours` (the clock and a retry move those) plus what the line does not show but the run does (`--no-files`, `--keep-scheduler`, the quiet scheduler of a running bench, the sha256 of the `--before-migrate` script). It is made again after the check phase and before the new backup gate and the apply question, so a newer backup on the server, another set of apps or a changed script is a stale plan: exit 1, nothing changed. `--new-backup` still needs the typed or `--confirm-site` name, token or not. `pull.sh` uses `fl_plan_token_v` and `fl_plan_token_check` when `plan-token.sh` is loaded and a plain sha256 (as `site restore` made it) when it is not, so it needs no change when that file lands.
- The ssh command of every network git (`fl_git_net`) is the user's own (`GIT_SSH_COMMAND`, else `core.sshCommand` as git sees it in the clone) plus `-o BatchMode=yes -o ConnectTimeout=N`, never a replacement: ssh keeps the first value of an option, so a user's own setting wins and a work key or `-F` file keeps working (APP-4, #152). Freshness fetches use the same helper (connect timeout 5 s, not 10).
- Fetches in a clone (`app update`, `lock apply`, `fl_commit_reach`) go through `fl_git_in`, the same helper as ls-remote and clone, with the clone deadline (`FL_GIT_CLONE_TIMEOUT`, 120 s) and the process group kill; a timeout reads "timed out after Ns" (APP-5).
- `app add` reads the branch tip again with ls-remote right before `bench get-app` and refuses when it moved (nothing is cloned). A pin is exempt, and after its checkout the app gets `bench setup requirements --python` and `--node` again, because get-app installed the tip's. The late checks that stay say what happened: get-app cloned and pip installed, nothing built or installed on a site (APP-6). `lock apply` puts a clone that was moved to its pin among the changed apps for the same reason (APP-7).
- A team profile's `frappe_branch` moves erpnext with it (`version-N` and `develop` give the same erpnext branch); any other frappe branch with a bundle that has erpnext needs `erpnext_branch`, else the profile is invalid. `erpnext_branch` is a schema 1 key. A profile with `[[apps]]` names its own branches, so nothing is paired there (APP-8).
- `config/apps.tsv` uses `version-15` and `version-16` for crm, helpdesk and lms (they have both); wiki, builder and drive keep the one production branch they have (`master`, `master`, `main`); insights keeps `version-3`, which spans Frappe 15 to 17. `scripts/check-apps-branches.sh` checks every repo and branch with ls-remote, weekly in CI (APP-9).
- `app add` refuses an app whose `[tool.bench.frappe-dependencies]` frappe range excludes the bench's Frappe (read from `apps/frappe/frappe/__init__.py`), as a plan error, not a warning: get-app and install-app would fail or half work. A clause the checker cannot read is skipped, and no pyproject or no range means no check (APP-10).
- ls-remote checks name the exact ref: `refs/heads/NAME` (and `refs/tags/NAME` where a tag is allowed), because a bare name is a tail match and `feature/main` answers for `main` (APP-17).
- Flaky tests (#138): a stop signal that arrives while doctor has forked a background job but not yet kept its pid is held (`FL_PAR_STARTING`, `FL_SIG_DEFERRED` in facts.sh, run.sh) and sent again once the pid is kept, so `fl_par_stop` always knows the job and waits for it before `fl_facts_end` deletes the folder (the old `jobs -p` fallback sent TERM without waiting). `run-tests.sh` reruns a failed test once only when `CI` or `RERUN_FAILED=1` is set and always prints `FLAKY: NAME` with the first failure: a rerun that hides nothing, not a retry that makes red green.
- Flaky tests, visibility (#138): a test that fails and then passes on the rerun is never quiet. `run-tests.sh` prints `FLAKY: NAME` with the first failure in the test's block, `flaky: N (names)` at the end, and under GitHub Actions a `::warning title=FLAKY::` annotation and a "Flaky tests" table in the job summary. `STRICT_FLAKY=1` (ci.yml sets it on a push to next-release and on the nightly) keeps the rerun but fails the run at its end for any flake, so a streak of green runs holds none; pull requests keep the forgiving rerun.
- `NO_SKIP` (#138): a test that prints `NAME: ok (SKIPPED ...)` is listed as `skipped:` at the end of a run, and fails when it is named in `NO_SKIP`. ci.yml names `test-share-gateway` on the macOS and Linux legs, which have node; the bash 3.2 container has none, so a missing node there stays a listed skip and anywhere else is a failure.
- Test ports (#138): `test-services-served` takes its three ports at random from 20000 to 30000 (the first that binds), because port 0 hands out the system's ephemeral range, where the client end of another test's connection can hold the number before the listener starts. A stopped doctor run also owns the second folder that a load of the apps' facts makes (`FL_AF_DIR`, stopped and removed by `fl_facts_end`, and made inside the run's folder when there is one).
- Unknown options are errors (0.9.0, CORE-3, #149): after the main loop, `fl_args_validate` checks the options after the command against that command's `flag` records in commands.sh (the table is the one list; `tests/test-unknown-flags.sh` walks every flag and fails when a case pattern in `lib/` or `benchbar` names a flag the table lacks). An unknown option exits 64 (EX_USAGE, `error_id` `unknown_flag`) with the option and the usage line, before the stream, the log or the lock, and a value flag with no value (or whose value is the next option) exits 64 too (`missing_value`). Not 2: exit 2 means "the MariaDB root password is unknown" and the Windows tray (WizardModel) reads a 2 without a done line as that case and asks for the password; not 1: the Mac app's calls that accept [0,1] would parse an empty output. Under `--json`, `--jsonl` and `--for-agent` the same refusal is JSON on stdout (`fl_usage_refuse`: a `done` line for a command that changes things, one document for a read), built before the stream starts; the shim's version skew hint fires on 64 as well as 2. `BENCHBAR_LENIENT_FLAGS=1` is the switch back for an unknown option only (a warning on stderr, the old behaviour), never for a missing value. It was kept only for scripts; `--dry-run` typed as `--dry-rn` next to `--yes` ran for real before this.
- The usage check ends in `exit`, never in `set -e`: bash switches errexit off inside a function that an `if`, `||` or `&&` calls, and the dispatcher calls `fl_args_validate` as `[[ ... ]] || fl_args_validate`, so a plain failing command in it (or in `fl_usage_refuse`, `fl_opt_value`) would not stop the run. Every refusal there is an explicit `exit 64` (0.9.0, #149).
- Core reliability (0.9.0, #149): the entrypoint runs with errtrace (`set -E`) and `trap 'fl_on_error "$BASH_COMMAND"' ERR`, so a command that fails inside a function names itself with its file and function (bash 3.2 reports the line of the function's first line, so the text says "near"). The trap does nothing in a subshell or command substitution (`$BASH_SUBSHELL` above 0: `x="$(grep a b)" || true` must stay silent) nothing while the exit trap runs (`FL_EXITING`) and nothing while errexit is off (`$-` has no `e`: a `set +e; ( ... ); rc=$?; set -e` section collects its failures itself, and bash fires the trap there all the same); a command that is a function (a deliberate `return 1` after its own message) keeps the old rule, the last `fl_run` command or nothing. The phase scripts keep their top level only trap.
- A state file's folder must be writable before its lock is taken (`fl__kv_dir_ok`): a failing `mkdir` of the lock used to look like a busy lock and looped for ever. A lock that cannot be created for a minute dies too. The wait for a lock that is really held stays as it was.
- A command that changes things on a bench it detected on its own (`FL_BENCH_SOURCE=detected`) prints `acting on PATH (auto-detected ...)` on stderr; under BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE with more than one known bench it refuses with exit 1 and lists them (`fl_bench_guess_gate`). install, adopt, register, scan, profile, labs, ports, lan-guard, report, self-update and the read commands are left out: they name their target or change no bench.
- benchbar refuses to run as root (`fl_refuse_root`, exit 64 always, JSON under `--json`, `--jsonl` and `--for-agent` with `error_id` `root_refused`, via `fl_refuse_json`, the body of `fl_usage_refuse`; before any state is touched; only `--version`, `--help` and `help` pass). The only override is the tests' `FL_EFFECTIVE_UID` under `FL_TESTING=1`. Doctor does not look for root owned files yet.
- The run lock waits `FL_LOCK_WAIT_SECS` (8) for a live holder, and a holder is the recorded pid with its recorded start time (`pstart`, from `ps -o lstart=`): after a crash a reused pid is a stale lock. A lock of an older benchbar has no `pstart` and is judged by its pid, as before. The harness sets the wait to 0, so a test of a busy lock does not sit for 8 seconds.
- Ctrl-C and timeouts: TERM, then up to `FL_KILL_GRACE` (10) seconds, polled every tenth of a second and cut short when the group is gone, then KILL. The temp logs of `fl_run_long` and `fl_run_with_timeout` are registered (`fl_tmp_new_v`) and removed by the exit trap.
- The refusal of root exits 64, not 1 or 2 (0.9.0, #149): the WSL CI distro and some imported distros run as root, so `status --json` printed the text refusal and the Windows shim test could not parse it. A refusal of the run is a command line the CLI cannot act on, like a usage error: exit 2 would make the tray ask for the MariaDB root password, and 1 would make the Mac app's calls that accept [0,1] parse an empty output; 64 is already read as a plain failure with the `[FAIL]` text by the app, the tray and the shim.

## 0.9.0: one time notices for new defaults (issue 188)

- Every new default of a release has a switch back, an upgrade note and a notice after the update. The notices are one table in `lib/frappe-local/notices.sh` (id, SINCE, title, text, switch back, docs path), so the terminal, `status --json` `notices[]`, `benchbar notices` and the app read the same texts. The unknown flags notice names `BENCHBAR_LENIENT_FLAGS` from #224.
- A run whose version differs from `LAST_VERSION` (state file) activates the notices whose SINCE is above the version it came from, on every command, so the app's first `status --json` counts. Only a human command prints them: not `--json`, `--jsonl`, `--for-agent`, `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE` (so not `mcp`), a dry run, `path`, `where`, `commands`, `help`, `docs`, `notices` or the command that prints the root password. What the terminal printed is `NOTICES_SHOWN`, what is active is `NOTICES`, and a dismissal removes the id from `NOTICES` only, so a terminal that printed them does not hide the app's banner.
- 0.8.3 and older never wrote `LAST_VERSION`: a state folder with content (state file, registered benches, a bench's file) and no `LAST_VERSION` is an update from below 0.9.0. A start with no state at all is a fresh install: it writes nothing then (a read or a refusal leaves no state behind), and the exit trap records the version when that run made state of its own (`install`, `register`, `adopt`), whatever its exit code: `install` writes `PIPX_BIN_DIR`, `BENCH_BIN` and `RC_FILE` before the step that can fail, and a failed first run recorded only on exit 0 would leave state with content and no `LAST_VERSION`, which the next command takes for an update from 0.8.3 (the other choice, recording before the first state write, would need a hook in every writer, including `registered-benches.txt`, which does not go through `fl_state_set`). A dev tree (0.8.3) still activates the notices, since the test is "the recorded version is below SINCE", not "the current one is above".
- The common path (version unchanged) reads the state file once with the read builtin and starts no process. A write (the update, the first print, a dismissal) goes through the state file's own lock, not the run lock, so the app's Dismiss never waits for a running command.
- The terminal prints the notices only when stderr is a terminal (`[[ -t 2 ]]`). A script's `2>log` or cron must not use up the one print, so a pipe prints nothing and records nothing as shown, and the next run in a terminal prints it.
- SINCE is compared without the prerelease part of either version (`0.9.0-rc.1` counts as `0.9.0`): an update from 0.8.3 to an rc activates the notices, and the rc to the final release activates nothing, so a notice the person dismissed on the rc does not come back. `fl_version_lt` of `selfupdate.sh` is the one that is loaded in every start (`version-policy.sh`'s numbers only one is sourced first and replaced) and orders a prerelease below its release, which is right for "is there an update" and wrong here, hence `fl_notices_since_lt`.
- When the app's CLI and a Homebrew CLI of different versions share the state folder, the older one leaves a `LAST_VERSION` that is newer than its own version as it is: no write, no notice activated. Without this the two would rewrite `LAST_VERSION` on alternate runs, each a locked write.
- An update's notices and its LAST_VERSION are written when the run ends with exit 0, not when it starts: a refused or failed command writes nothing to the state (test-input-hygiene), and the next command that ends well records them. Until then they live in memory, so the run's own `status --json`, `notices` and the one print still see them.
- The Linux LAN guard (`lan-guard-linux.sh`) is one nftables table, `inet benchbar`, with an input chain at priority -10 and a policy of accept, loaded at boot by `benchbar-lan-guard.service`; `remove` deletes that table, the unit and the rules file and nothing else in nftables. The rule is `ip saddr != 127.0.0.0/8` (and `ip6 saddr != ::1`) on the bench ports, not an `iifname lo` accept, so this machine's own LAN address is refused and the probe from it proves the rule, as on macOS. The table is global and always on like the pf anchor, not tied to a room.
- ufw and firewalld stay untouched: BenchBar adds no ufw rule for a room's port 8443. An active ufw already refuses what it was not told to allow, so the guard stacks with it and the one thing a person must do is `ufw allow 8443/tcp`, which `lan-guard install` and `status` print. Writing into a firewall a person manages is a decision for the person, and the guard is correct without it.
- Every `lan-guard` subcommand first checks the platform and stops before any prompt or root step on one that has no guard (macOS and Linux have one). `allow-node` is macOS only and says so on Linux. The `lan-rooms` lab keeps `macos` as its only platform until `share start` works on Linux; `FL_LABS_ANY_PLATFORM=1` (tests only, read under `FL_TESTING=1`) lets a test reach the Linux guard through the dispatcher before then.
- Readiness (#179) is one object, `ready`, in `status --json`: the parts agent, processes, web (2xx or 3xx), socketio, redis, workers and stale. The fast path adds no program: workers (pgrep, lsof) and stale (find) are `null` unless `--deep`, `wait` or the MCP tools ask, and a `null` part never makes `ready` false; `ready.deep` says whether they were asked. The status cost budget stays at its old count. `wait --for stale` is refused (exit 1) where `stale` is `null` (bench not running, no `started_at`), because a null part counts as holding and the wait would pass at once.
- `stale` compares `*.py` files under `apps/` with `started_at` of the runner's `state.json` (UTC) using one `find -newermt`, not the web process's own start time: it needs no `ps` and no date conversion that differs between macOS and Linux, and the runner starts the web server within seconds, so an edit in that window is a false positive that errs towards a restart. So does any `*.py` under `apps/`, tests and docs too: it is a known false positive, kept because telling code from tests needs each app's layout. The search runs `find -H` once per app folder, so an app that is a symlink to a checkout elsewhere is searched (links inside an app are not followed, so no loop). Without a `state.json` it is `null`.
- `benchbar wait` never exits 2 (that stays the MariaDB password): 0 ready, 1 not ready. It returns at once for a bench that cannot become ready by itself (`paused`, `not_adopted`) and waits the whole timeout for a stopped one, since another command may be starting it. It takes no lock and starts nothing.
- `up` and `restart` wait for the web part of the contract only (`fl_ready_web_ok`), as they always did, now with 2xx or 3xx instead of exactly 200 (the runner's ping loop, template v9, marks `running` on the same rule and writes the code it got); the full contract is `wait`, and their `state_after` carries `ready`. Waiting for socketio and Redis inside `up` would change what existing callers see as a failed start.

## 0.9.0: operation handles (op_id, benchbar op, --background)

- Every run that makes a log (the dispatcher's `fl_log_init`, so exactly the runs that change things) is an operation: `fl_op_begin` makes `op-<date>-<time>-<pid>` (a `-N` suffix on a clash, the file taken with noclobber) and `ops/<op_id>.json` in the state folder. A dry run, a read and a refusal before the log have none; `fg` and `self-update` never do, because they end by `exec` and no exit trap would finish the record. The text form prints no handle, so no existing output changes; `op list` shows it.
- The record is one line, written whole through a temp file and a rename at the start, at each step (`fl_step_json`, `fl_section`), at each progress line and by `fl_cleanup` right after the done line goes out, so a reader that sees the record end finds that line in the run's output (the other order lets `op wait` return before it is written). A run killed between the two shows as `interrupted` with its done line in `result`. A write failure never ends the run. Only the process that began the op ends it (`FL_OP_OWNER`); the phase scripts inherit `FL_OP_ID` and update the step. `benchbar` resets the variables at its start, so a nested `benchbar` is its own op.
- The op_id sits right after `event` in every line of every stream emitter (`fl_jsonl`, pull's, repair's: first) and next to the done fields of a one document `--json` (`fl_doc_emit`), not inside `fl_done_fields_v`, which a stream's done line also uses and would carry it twice.
- `interrupted` is computed by the reader (`kill -0` on `pid`, and the process start time kept in `pid_start`, so a pid that an unrelated process reuses is not the run: field 22 of `/proc/PID/stat` on Linux, no process; `ps -o lstart=` on macOS. Only `op` and the prune read it, never the lean status path; a record without `pid_start`, or a start time that cannot be read, falls back to `kill -0` alone) and never written back, so `op status` stays a read that takes no lock. A `starting` record older than 60 seconds is interrupted too. Records are pruned with the logs, in `fl_state_prune`: the newest 100 (`FL_KEEP_OPS`), never a run that still runs, with their `.jsonl` and `.err`. Count, not days: a bench left alone for a week keeps its last runs. `op wait` looks once a second; the test seam `FL_OP_WAIT_POLL` shortens that under `FL_TESTING=1`.
- `--background` is the CLI starting itself again, detached (`setsid`, else `nohup` with job control), with `--jsonl` added and its stream in `ops/<op_id>.jsonl`; the launcher writes the `starting` record and the child takes it over (`FL_OP_PRESET`, accepted only for a record that says `starting`). It sets `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` before the preflight, so a missing `--yes`, `ADMIN_PASSWORD`, `MARIADB_ROOT_PASSWORD` or `sudo` is the launcher's refusal, not a done line nobody reads. `ADMIN_PASSWORD` and `MARIADB_ROOT_PASSWORD` reach the child through the environment of its subshell only (never argv). `BENCHBAR_SUDO_PASSWORD` is refused: it travels on a pipe the child cannot read, and a second copy in the environment is what that design avoids.
- Which commands take `--background`: `install`, `repair`, `pull`, `app add`, `app update`, `site add`, `site backup` and `site restore`, the ones that take minutes and end in a done line. `up`, `down`, `restart`, `service` and `adopt` take seconds and their callers want the answer. A person only command (`share`, `lan-guard`, `labs on`, `site open-as on`, `listen all`, `mariadb-password`, `uninstall-service`) is not in the list, so it is refused under `--background` for the same reason it is refused under an agent: nobody can answer it. `benchbar run --site X -- migrate` has no command to attach to yet; it takes the flag when it lands.
- MCP: `benchbar_op_status` (read only, `op_id` checked against the id pattern before it becomes an argument) and `op_id` at the top of every action's result. `background: true` is on `benchbar_app_add` and `benchbar_site_restore` only. `benchbar_site_add` generates the new site's Administrator password and keeps it only for a site that run created, which a launch cannot know; and `up`, `down`, `restart`, `service_install` end in seconds. A background call skips the follow up list or status (nothing has changed yet) and is not turned into a stream, so no progress notifications: the progress is in the op. There is no tool that waits for an operation: it would hold a call for minutes, which is what `background` is for.
- The MCP tasks primitive (2025-11-25) is later. The server negotiates 2025-06-18, 2025-03-26 and 2024-11-05; tasks are experimental in that revision and a client has to ask (`task` in the request) and poll `tasks/get`. The op record is the state such a task needs, and the task id will be the op_id, so adding tasks changes the MCP layer only. The apps (Mac and tray) read `op list --json`; they are not part of this change.
- A detached run is `starting` in its record until it holds the lock (`fl_op_running`, called by the dispatcher after the lock block), and the launcher polls that record for up to 5 seconds (`FL_OP_LAUNCH_WAIT` under `FL_TESTING=1`). A run the lock refuses (or that fails before it) is the launcher's refusal: the run's own `error` and hint, its exit code, nothing printed on stdout, and its record says `failed`. No queue: a second run that waits for the lock is the caller's decision, and `op wait` on the first one is how.
- A record's `site` is the NAME of `site add|backup|restore|drop NAME`, then `--site`, then the default site; `bench` is the context's, else `--bench-dir`, else the bench found the way every command finds it (one read, before the run's own context exists), so a run the lock refused still names both. A step still `running` when the run ends is closed as `done` or `failed` by the exit code, and the progress line is cleared.

## 0.9.0: docs structure (part 2)

- The contributor part of `docs/testing.md` (choosing and timing the tests) moved into CONTRIBUTING.md, so the testing page is for testers. `docs/DECISIONS.md` and `docs/releasing.md` stay under `docs/` because AGENTS.md and the release workflows name those paths.

## Docs content (0.9, DOC-4 to DOC-22)

- `docs/whats-new.md` is written by hand, one section per release, for people who use BenchBar (platform tags, links, upgrade notes). CHANGELOG stays the full log for contributors. The section of the next release is built from the `changelog.d` fragments and merged work, and the release checks it. `tests/test-docs.sh` reads every version heading of `CHANGELOG.md` from 0.8.0 on and fails when `whats-new.md` has no section for one; `docs/releasing.md` has the step that writes the section before the tag.
- First run bugs have an entry on `docs/first-run-problems.md` with the symptom, the cause, what the release changed and the workaround for the old release. A bug fix that has a workaround adds or updates its entry in the same pull request.
- The docs site honors Do Not Track (`data-do-not-track` on the Umami script), and `data-exclude-search`, so a query string is never sent with a page view. `docs/privacy.md` lists what a page view sends (path, referrer, language, screen size, browser, OS) and what the three custom events send, and names a content blocker first because Safari ignores Do Not Track. Before this the page did not exist and the setting was off.
- The "What's new" links of the Mac app (the About pane and the menu), the tray's About window and the release notes point at `https://benchbar.akashmishra.com/whats-new/`, the page for users; the full `CHANGELOG.md` stays the contributor log and the body of a GitHub release.
- Every image in `docs/images` has a row in `docs/images/manifest.tsv` (page, maker, date, status). The Mac screens come from `DocsSnapshotTests`, which open real windows and run only with the `on-screen` marker, never in CI, so a person runs them after a release that changed a screen.
- Titles, descriptions and the og image say "macOS, Linux and Windows (WSL)". `benchbar --help` keeps "macOS and Linux": WSL is Linux, and the shim has its own help.
- SECURITY.md lists the sharing gateway, Labs, Open as, the MCP tools that change things and the loopback default as things a report can break. The sudo list lives in AGENTS.md and SECURITY.md carries a copy that `tests/test-docs.sh` checks only for the old counts.

## 0.9.0: action history (benchbar history, BENCHBAR_CALLER)

- Retention (#185): the op records keep the newest 100 runs with their steps and progress, which is the wrong shape for "what happened here last month". So a run also appends one compact line to `history.jsonl` when it ends (`fl_hist_record`, called by `fl_op_end` before the record is finished, so a reader that sees the run end finds its line). The file is rotated at 1 MB with three old files kept, the rule of `mcp-calls.jsonl`, by size and not by count so a line cannot grow the folder without limit; at about 700 bytes a line that is thousands of runs. Not written for a killed run (no exit trap), which `op list` already shows as `interrupted`: a second writer at the start would double every line for a case that is rare and visible elsewhere.
- A run an agent started names the MCP tool and the client that made the call. The history copies both when the line is written, and does not look them up in `mcp-calls.jsonl` when it is read: `benchbar mcp` passes the tool name and the client to the CLI it starts (`BENCHBAR_MCP_TOOL`, `BENCHBAR_MCP_CLIENT`) and `BENCHBAR_CALLER=mcp`, and `history.jsonl` keeps them in `mcp`. Bash cannot match two JSON Lines files by `op_id` without a slow loop per line, and a reader would depend on an audit log that rotates on its own. The audit log gets the `op_id` as well, so a person can still go from an audit line to the run. Both values are checked against a short pattern in the CLI (`benchbar_` and lower case for the tool, letters, digits and `._ +/@-` for the client) and dropped otherwise, because the client name is text from outside.
- `BENCHBAR_CALLER` is one of `terminal`, `app`, `tray`, `mcp`, `agent`, `script`; any other value is ignored, so no text of a caller reaches the history or the log through it. Without it: `--for-agent` is `agent`, a terminal on stdin is `terminal`, anything else `script`. An explicit value wins over `--for-agent` (the app asks for JSON the same way). It is exported once the first run resolves it, so the phase scripts and a `--background` child (which has no terminal) say what the launcher said. It is a label any process of the user can set, the same as `BENCHBAR_APP_PID` for the person guard: it answers "who did this" for support and for an agent that reads the history, not "who may do this".
- The command line is kept as `argv` (an array), redacted word by word with the rules of the MCP audit log (`SECRET_KEY`, `SECRET_PARAM`, URL credentials): the value after a flag whose name looks like a secret, and `--apply`, since a plan token lets the same plan run again. It over redacts on purpose (`--keep-secrets` hides the next word). Words are cut at 200 characters and the list at 40; `--background` shows the command as typed. The same redacted words are the first line of the run's log (`run: benchbar ... (caller: ..., op: ...)`), which is the "run log header" MCP-5 left for this issue.
- `changed[]` keeps `kind` and `name` of the first 20 entries, no `detail` (the pruning line alone is hundreds of characters): the done line and the log have the rest, and `changed_more` says how many were left out.
- `benchbar history` is a lean command (no library beyond ui.sh), reads the files newest first with `tac` (`tail -r` on a Mac) and stops at the limit, so the cost is the limit and not the file. `--since` is compared as text against `started_at` (ISO UTC of one length), so it never forks `date` per line.
- The Mac app and the tray only set `BENCHBAR_CALLER` here (`CLIClient.environment()`; `SystemProcessRunner` and `SystemStreamRunner`, and the shim forwards it). A page that shows the history comes later.
- The caller is checked by equality with one word of the list, and `FL_CALLER` is reset when `history.sh` is loaded: only `fl_caller_resolve` sets it, from `BENCHBAR_CALLER` after that check, `--for-agent` or the terminal test. A variable of the environment must not choose what the history and the log say; `BENCHBAR_CALLER` stays a label any process can set (see above).
- The history file is appended and rotated under its own lock (`history.jsonl.lck`, the key-value lock of `state.sh`), in a subshell so a lock that cannot be taken ends only the history write, never the run. Two runs that end together do not rotate at once and do not lose a line to a rotation. The word after a secret looking flag is hidden only when it is not a flag itself (`--keep-secrets --yes` keeps `--yes`).
- The tray's Update Now window sets `$env:BENCHBAR_CALLER = 'tray'` in its script: a ShellExecute process cannot take an environment from `ProcessStartInfo`.

- A dead launchd agent (its runner file is gone) is a FAIL with the action `dead_agents`, not a stub in the plist: repair boots out and moves aside the agent of a folder that is no bench, from any bench, because the bench whose runner is gone cannot run repair any more. A bench that still exists gets its runner written again by the normal `write_runner` step. Only agents that run `benchbar-run.sh` or `frappe-mac-run.sh` count, so a `com.benchbar` label pointed at a person's own script is never touched.
- Redaction has one routine, `fl_redact_stream`, extended rather than a second one: a Cookie or Set-Cookie header is masked to the end of its line, `--password VALUE` style flags and `-pVALUE` (only on a line that runs a mysql or mariadb program, so `-p 8000` and `bench serve -p` are safe) are masked, and the key list gained credential, cookie, session, sid, pass (only whole or between separators, so passed and bypass stay) and names ending in _pwd. A bare 40 hex string is not masked: git commit hashes are everywhere in a report and are what a maintainer needs. The Windows shim got the same names and the same two flag shapes. `curl -u` and `--user` (a `--user` elsewhere only with a colon in the value, so `systemctl --user restart` is untouched; `docker run --user 1000:1000` is masked, which costs nothing), `-b` and `--cookie`, and a header whose name ends in `-Token` or `-Key` also inside quotes.
- wkhtmltopdf on Apple Silicon: no arm64 package exists to offer (the packaging repository published one macOS package, 0.12.6-2 of June 2020, Intel only, and nothing for macOS since; the project is archived; Homebrew's build is not patched). benchbar says so before it installs anything, asks for Rosetta 2 as before, and points at Chromium PDFs of Frappe v16. The download is `https` only, TLS 1.2 or newer.
- Plan tokens stay 64 hex characters and stateless: H(key, window, H(key, plan)), with the key in `plan-key` in the state folder (0600, made by the first plan; an existing file that is empty or readable by group or others is replaced with a new key and logged, because a key others can read proves nothing) and a ten minute window. Apply accepts the current window and the six before it (60 to 70 minutes) and tells an expired token from a changed plan by trying older windows. A token store with exact expiry would need a write from a plan, which is read only. The first plan writes the key once, so a dry run writes nothing but that file, and the docs say so; the test harness makes one so "a dry run writes nothing" stays true in tests.
- A `--before-migrate` hook is hashed again right before it runs (`FL_PULL_HOOK_SHA`), because a restore can take minutes between the plan and that step; a changed script is not run.
- Service limits: the open file limit is always set (10240 on a Mac; `65536:524288` soft and hard on Linux, because a bare 65536 would set the hard limit to 65536 and lower systemd's default of 524288). Nice, ProcessType Background and MemoryHigh are rendered at their no-change values (0, Standard, infinity) and come from the bench's state keys `SERVICE_NICE`, `SERVICE_BACKGROUND` and `SERVICE_MEMORY_HIGH` (zero, `0` or `0%`, is ignored: it would starve the unit), so a normal bench behaves as before. `NoNewPrivileges` and `TasksMax` are left out: a bench command that calls sudo, or a build that forks a lot, would break for a laptop that did not ask for a cap.
- The macOS MariaDB drop-in keeps the name `frappe-mac-local-only.cnf` and its "Written by frappe-mac" line: renaming it would orphan the file on every existing install and change the template hash for nothing a person sees. Linux keeps its own name.
- `fl_process_running NAME` matches the process name (`pgrep -x`) when NAME is a bare word, so `vim mariadbd.cnf` or `tail -f mariadbd.log` is no MariaDB; a pattern with anything else still matches the command line. `fl_port_listening` on macOS asks netstat when lsof sees nothing, because lsof run by a normal user does not list a socket owned by root.

## Supply chain (0.9.0, issue 133)

- Every `uses:` of every workflow and composite action is a full commit sha with the version in a comment (`# v7.0.1`), the actions of GitHub included. A tag can be moved to other code, and every job holds a token, the release job next to the signing keys. Dependabot moves the sha and the comment together, a week after a release (`cooldown`). `tests/test-supply-chain.sh` fails on an action that is not pinned.
- The build tools that are downloaded come from one script, `scripts/ci-install-tool.sh`, with a version and a sha256 per archive (shellcheck, XcodeGen, actionlint, zizmor). They no longer come from Homebrew (`brew install xcodegen shellcheck` followed whatever the formula was that day), and no Homebrew download cache that a CI run of another branch could have written is restored. The sum is the vendor's where one exists (actionlint publishes a checksum file); the others come from the digest GitHub shows on the release asset and were checked again against the downloaded file. A tool moves by changing its version, URL and sum together, in a pull request.
- Xcode is `26.6` in `ci.yml`, `snapshots.yml` and the `release-prepare` action, not `latest-stable`: a signed release must build with the same compiler a week later, and the snapshot references depend on it. It moves by hand, with the references.
- The Swift packages are pinned by `macos/Package.resolved`. XcodeGen makes the Xcode project and the project is not committed, so Xcode has no pin file of its own: `scripts/macos-packages.sh` copies the committed file into the generated project, fetches exactly those revisions (`-onlyUsePackageVersionsFromResolvedFile`), the builds pass `-disableAutomaticPackageResolution`, and `--check` fails the build if Xcode moved a pin. The first versions in the file are what a resolution gave the day it was added (Sparkle 2.10.1, swift-subprocess 1.0.2, swift-system 1.9.0).
- `macos/Package.swift` is not built. It names the two packages and their ranges for Dependabot, which cannot read an XcodeGen spec (the `swift` entry failed with "No files found in /macos" before). It repeats `project.yml` and `sparkle.yml` on purpose: the test compares all four files, so a range that differs fails in CI and not in a release.
- The shim's Go is `1.27.4` (go.mod), a supported release; Go 1.23 no longer got security fixes. `x/sys` moved to a version without GO-2026-5024. The `shim` job runs `govulncheck` (pinned by version, its modules checked by the Go checksum database) against the Windows build and fails on a vulnerability the code calls.
- The Go module cache is off in the `shim` and `wsl` jobs: they upload what they build, and the one module is a quick download that `go.sum` verifies. The Homebrew caches of the app job and the release job are gone. The DerivedData cache of the app job stays, accepted in `.github/zizmor.yml` with its reason: CI runs only, and the signed release builds from a clean checkout.
- The CI tests of bash 3.2 run the image by digest (`bash:3.2.57@sha256:...`) and not only by tag. `apk add` in that container stays unpinned: the packages are the current Alpine tools the suite is meant to run against, and the Alpine index is signed.
- `actions/setup-go`, `setup-dotnet` and `setup-bun` are pinned by sha, but their toolchain downloads have no checksum we can pin. `go install ...goversioninfo@v1.7.0` and `govulncheck@v1.10.0` are version pinned and checked by the Go checksum database. The tray's `global.json` pins the SDK and `dotnet-sdk` in Dependabot moves it.
- A job `workflows` in `ci.yml` runs actionlint and zizmor on `.github/` and fails on any finding. What stays is listed with its reason in `.github/actionlint.yaml` and `.github/zizmor.yml`. The entries for `release.yml`, `release-windows.yml` and `auto-tag.yml` exist because issue 132 reworks those files at the same time; that change removes them.
- The Linux install's third party installers are pinned: uv 0.13.9 (the GitHub release's `uv-installer.sh`, which checks the uv archive against sums inside itself) and fnm 1.39.0 (the script of the tag, run with `--release v1.39.0`), each checked against a sha256 before it runs, over `--proto =https`. A mirror sets the URL and the sum together. The fnm release zip has no checksum that the vendor publishes: it stays unchecked, and `docs/install.md` does not claim it. The Homebrew installer cannot be pinned (Homebrew publishes only `HEAD`, and it installs the current Homebrew): `install.sh` still offers it and `docs/install.md` says so.
- Not done here: `frappe-bench` and `yarn` are still unpinned (INST-9 asks for them in `config/release-profiles.tsv`), and the NuGet lock file with `--locked-mode` (OPS-7) is not added.
- The result envelope (#178, `docs/json-schema.md`) is guaranteed under `--for-agent` and `--jsonl`, not under plain `--json`: the polled readers (`status`, `list`, the app's `doctor`) keep their 0.8 documents byte for byte and start no process for it, and a read that fails under `--json` is still text on stderr, which the app and the tray read by exit code. A read under the guaranteed modes is kept back in a file in the state folder (fd 8 is the real stdout; fd 9 belongs to the facts wake pipe of `doctor`) so that `exit` can be added to its one line, or a failure document printed when it died before it had one. The alternative, adding `exit` where each of the 40 reads prints its document, lets the 41st skip it.
- `ok` is not an envelope field: `exit == 0` says it. `app add` and `site restore --apply` keep their `ok` (always `exit == 0`) because dropping a field is a schema break and nothing was gained; `share preflight`'s `ok` means the critical checks passed and stays. Adding `ok` to every command was rejected: two fields for one fact drift apart.
- The twelve changing commands that had no JSON mode (`register`, `autostart`, `listen`, `uninstall-service`, `app install`, `app unfocus`, `lock write`, `lock apply`, `profile create`, `ports setup`, `ports mode`, `site hosts`) are streams in the catalogue now, so `--for-agent` gives them a done line. The commands that stay `none` are terminals or text helpers (`fg`, `watch`, `console`, `db`, `docs`, `mcp`, `help`, `mariadb-password`, `path`) and the usage pages of the command groups; `tests/test-envelope.sh` lists them, and a new command with neither a row nor an entry there fails it.
- A usage error and a refusal that is not about a password both exit 1 with `error` in the envelope. A separate usage code (64) is left to the change that adds it; the test checks that `exit` equals the process exit code, whatever it is.
- The MCP server puts `exit` in every CLI result and, for an action, `changed`, `warnings`, `next_actions` and `error` at the top of `structuredContent` beside `result`. `exit_code` stays: the first release's readers use it. A result the server makes itself (invalid argument, unknown bench, plan refused or declined, timeout, benchbar not startable) has `exit` and `error` too: 1, 1, 1, 1, 124, 127.
- `benchbar run` (#181): the allow list is a table in `lib/frappe-local/bench-run.sh`, not a file in `config/`: a list that decides what an agent may run is code that ships with the CLI and is reviewed with it, and a file next to it is one more thing to edit by mistake. The MCP enum and the reference page are checked against it (`tests/test-bench-run.sh`).
- `benchbar run` allows `migrate`, `clear-cache`, `clear-website-cache`, `build`, `run-tests` and the reads `doctor`, `list-apps`, `version`. Refused, with a reason where people ask: `console`, `shell`, `mariadb` (interactive or a shell), `execute` (it calls any Python function with any arguments; a per function opt in needs its own design), `update`, `drop-site`, `restore`, `reinstall`, `set-admin-password`, `install-app`, `get-app`, `setup`, `config`. `run-ui-tests` is left out: it needs a browser and a runner a CLI cannot supervise. `show-config` is left out: it prints the site's secrets.
- `benchbar run` never takes a positional word, `--force` or a path: a command takes only the flags its table row names, each value one plain word (letters, digits and `. _ : -` and spaces, not starting with `-`), so a value cannot become an option or a file name. The MCP tools take those flags as fields for the same reason (an MCP string that starts with `-` is refused).
- `benchbar run -- migrate` always takes a database backup of the site first (no opt out): migrate can leave a site half migrated, and the failed run names the restore of that backup as a next action. The `--timeout` does not count the backup. `clear-cache`, `build` and `run-tests` take none; `run-tests` warns that it writes test records into the site and to use a throwaway site.
- `benchbar run` exits 64 (EX_USAGE) for a refusal of its arguments, and otherwise with the bench command's own code (124 at the deadline, 125 when it waits for input). 2 and 64 are benchbar's own (the root password, a refusal), so a bench command that exits with them is reported as 1; `command_exit` in the result has the real code.
- `benchbar run` changing commands take the CLI lock and the plan token (the same rule as `site restore`): `--json` and `--for-agent` without `--dry-run` or `--apply` exit 64, a bare `--yes` is a person's shortcut at a terminal and is refused under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` (an agent goes through the plan), and the token covers the plan body, the sites, the default site, `apps.txt` and the hash of the site's `site_config.json`. A read runs at once without the lock; one that needs Redis (`doctor`) says so when the bench is stopped and does not start it, while a change starts the setup Redis and stops it after, as `site backup` does.
- `--timeout` is capped at 7200 seconds in the CLI and 3000 in MCP, whose call limit is an hour (a backup, a deadline and the follow up status must fit in it). The tail of the output in the result is the last 60 lines, at most 8000 characters, with colors and URL credentials removed; the whole output is in the run log. In MCP it comes back labeled as data from the bench.
- MCP: `benchbar_run_read` is a read tool that runs a command in a bench folder, so it gets the known bench guard of the changing tools (MCP-19) although it is read only: an unknown folder could hold code the bench would load.
- The Linux LAN guard (`lan-guard-linux.sh`) is one nftables table, `inet benchbar`, with an input chain at priority -10 and a policy of accept, loaded at boot by `benchbar-lan-guard.service`; `remove` deletes that table, the unit and the rules file and nothing else in nftables. The rule is `ip saddr != 127.0.0.0/8` (and `ip6 saddr != ::1`) on the bench ports, not an `iifname lo` accept, so this machine's own LAN address is refused and the probe from it proves the rule, as on macOS. The table is global and always on like the pf anchor, not tied to a room.
- ufw and firewalld stay untouched: BenchBar adds no ufw rule for a room's port 8443. An active ufw already refuses what it was not told to allow, so the guard stacks with it and the one thing a person must do is `ufw allow 8443/tcp`, which `lan-guard install` and `status` print. Writing into a firewall a person manages is a decision for the person, and the guard is correct without it.
- Every `lan-guard` subcommand first checks the platform and stops before any prompt or root step on one that has no guard (macOS and Linux have one). `allow-node` is macOS only and says so on Linux. The `lan-rooms` lab lists `macos,linux` (`share start` works on Linux, and so in WSL), so a test reaches the Linux guard through the dispatcher with no test seam.
- The Linux sharing job is a `systemd --user` unit written into `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/systemd/user`, not `~/.config/systemd/user`: the bench unit's globs (doctor, uninstall-service) never see it, nothing is enabled, and a logout or reboot forgets it as launchd forgets the bootstrapped job. The unit deletes its own file (`ExecStopPost`) and `CollectMode=inactive-or-failed` drops a failed one. The job file keeps the name `job.plist` in the share folder on both platforms, because the runner removes that name when it ends.
- On Linux the runner is the same file as on the Mac, with its tools passed in: `DNS_SD` is `avahi-publish` or empty (no advertising, said once in the log and by `share start`, never degraded), `CAFFEINATE` is empty (no sleep inhibitor yet), `LSOF` is `ss`. One template and one set of timings keep the two platforms from drifting.
- `share status` reads the guard through `fl_lg_state_v`, the one place that knows which platform's files are the guard, so the `lan_guard` field means the same on both.
- `fl_mariadb_running_formula` dispatches on the platform (`version-policy.sh`): the Homebrew path parser on a Mac, the server's own reported version on Linux (`fl_mariadb_running_formula_linux` in `doctor-linux.sh`). The Linux function already existed but never ran, because `version-policy.sh` is sourced after `platform-linux.sh` and replaced it; a dispatcher is robust to the order, a same-name override is not.
- `labs off NAME` drops a saved entry the platform cannot turn on, and only that: `fl_lab_on` is false for it, so "already off" left a stale `LABS=` entry for ever on a copied state file. An entry the platform lists is still turned off through the lab's cleanup as before.
- On WSL a room needs mirrored networking (`fl_sp_wsl_net_v`, `wslinfo --networking-mode`; no `wslinfo` counts as NAT, since a WSL without it has no mirrored mode). On NAT `share start`, `share invite` and the `wsl_networking` static check refuse, because the VM's address is private to the PC and every probe runs inside the VM, so the checks would pass for a room nobody can reach. On mirrored they warn with the `New-NetFirewallHyperVRule` line for port 8443 (Microsoft's own example, narrower than `-DefaultInboundAction Allow`) and go on; BenchBar does not run it, since it needs administrator rights on Windows and BenchBar never edits Windows firewall or `.wslconfig`.
- The `lan-rooms` lab stays available in WSL whatever its networking mode, and `labs list --json` is the same everywhere: a lab's platforms are a fixed list, not a probe of this PC's network. Whether a room can be reached is the checks' job: on NAT `share preflight` and `share start` refuse with the fix, so a person who turned the lab on is told at the first step, and in the tray's Sharing tab, with the reason and the switch to mirrored networking, instead of finding a switch that will not turn on.
- Retiring a bench (#183): `bench forget` is a plan with a token an agent may apply: it only moves benchbar's own service files aside (each backed up first) and edits benchbar's state, and `benchbar adopt PATH` undoes it. `bench remove` and `bench gc --apply` drop databases and move a folder, so, like `lan-guard`, a person runs them: refused under `--for-agent`, `mcp` and `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE`, and without a terminal (the app's claim counts as in `fl_person_env_ok`). A token only proves the plan is current, not that someone saw it, so `remove` also takes `--confirm-bench NAME` (as `site drop --confirm-site`). The plan of both stays open to agents.
- `bench remove` reuses `bench drop-site` for every site (a backup with files, the database, the folder to `archived/sites`) and checks a backup set is in the archive before it goes on, then renames the bench folder into `<parent>/.benchbar-trash/NAME-DATE`: the same volume, so a rename and never a copy, and benchbar never deletes it. The default bench is refused. `forget` leaves the hosts lines (another bench may share a name); `site hosts --prune` removes only single name lines of benchbar's block whose site no known bench has.
- A scratch bench is a bench whose state file has `SCRATCH_UNTIL` (epoch) and `SCRATCH_DAYS`; `install --scratch [--ttl DAYS]` and `bench scratch PATH` write them (default 7 days, 1 to 90, never with `--make-default`). `bench gc` lists expired ones from the benches benchbar knows, skips the default bench, and applies the plan with the token of its text (`--apply TOKEN --yes`), one bench after the other, stopping at the first failure. `FL_NOW` is the tests' clock (`FL_TESTING=1`).
- `db orphans` lists only names of frappe's shape (an underscore and 16 hex digits) that no site_config.json of a known bench names, and says when an archived site folder names one. It is read only and there is no command that drops a database: dropping stays a person's `mariadb-dump` and `DROP DATABASE`, because a database benchbar does not know may be another program's.
- `uninstall-service` removes the shell helper block only when no other agent points at a bench (the block is one for every bench): the first of several benches keeps it, `--all` and the last remove it.
- `bench remove` and `bench gc` do what `site drop` does for each site: keep the older backups around bench's own backup (frappe deletes the files older than 23 hours), take a backup with files and read every part to its end before anything is dropped, then `bench drop-site`, check the archive folder is new and `sites/NAME` is gone, and put the kept backups into the archive. Any failure stops with nothing moved. A bench folder that is a mount point is refused: the rename would copy and delete.
- `bench forget` writes the folder to `<state>/forgotten/benches.txt`. `db orphans` and `site hosts --prune` count the sites of those folders (while they are bench folders) as known, so a forgotten bench outside the usual folders does not show its live databases as leftovers or lose its hosts lines. Even so the orphan list says a database may belong to a bench or program benchbar does not know.
- `bench scratch PATH` asks (an agent passes `--yes`) and records who marked the bench (`install`, `person`, `agent`) and when; `bench gc` shows both in its plan. Marking is what lets a person's gc remove a bench, so an agent that marks one is visible at the plan, which stays the gate; `gc` only sees benches benchbar knows, and `bench scratch` says when it will not. The manual `site hosts --prune` command keeps a `.bak` copy (`sed -i .bak`).
- `benchbar stats [--json]` is its own read command, not a `--resources` flag of `status`: status is polled for every bench and `tests/test-status-cost.sh`, `test-lean-load` and `test-command-cost` pin its process count, so a flag would put a second code path (and a temptation to poll it) in that command. `stats` is not on the lean start (it needs the process matcher, which a status poll loads anyway, but it is asked for, not polled). It measures the set `benchbar down` stops (`fl_bench_process_pids`, the one matcher) plus every descendant by parent id, so node watch and anything a worker starts count. `resources` is null unless `fl_bench_is_serving`, so a Redis alone is not a running bench. CPU on macOS is ps's own `%cpu` from one `ps -axo pid=,ppid=,%cpu=,rss=` call (a decaying average, no second sample); on Linux it is two reads of `/proc/PID/stat` taken `FL_STATS_WINDOW` (0.5 s) apart, measured against `/proc/uptime`, the one extra process being that `sleep`. Memory is RSS (`VmRSS` on Linux) and not the Mac app's physical footprint, which `ps` cannot give; `cpu_source` says which of the two a client got. The speed figure is returned as `speed.raw` with the Mac app's formula and smoothing alpha so the tray computes it the same way. The Mac app and the tray keep their own readers in this change; the tray adopts `benchbar stats`.
- A pool job (`fl_bg_job_stop`, the profile check) ignores later TERM, INT and HUP once it has begun to stop its git group (#152): benchbar sends it TERM twice (the job table in `fl_on_signal`, then `fl_bg_stop_all`) and a terminal INT comes on top, and a second signal under the default action ended the job before its group was stopped, so one git of six ran on (`test-run` holds it with a command that ignores TERM).
- The Windows installer test job sets `SEE_MASK_NOZONECHECKS=1` (#134): `install.ps1` now keeps the internet mark when no build provenance verified, so starting the marked stub tray made Windows ask "Open File - Security Warning" and wait for a click on the runner until the 20 minute limit (checked on a Windows PC: without the variable `Start-Process` of a marked exe blocks, with it the program starts). The variable is in the test job only, never in the installer: a person is asked once, as the installer says.

## 0.9.0: MCP parity, part 1 (issue 186)

- The rule: every feature is on every surface, and the only allowed gaps are safety decisions. A command with no tool is either a safety line (named below, in the agents guide and in the pull request) or covered by the tools that give an agent the same result.
- Safety, `register`: registering a bench makes it one the changing tools may touch (`benchbar_list` adopted true, the check every action tool makes), so an agent that could register would widen its own access. `benchbar_scan` finds benches (read only) and says `adopted`; the person registers in a terminal or names the folder with `benchbar mcp --allow-bench`.
- Safety, `site hosts` and the hosts line of `site drop`: they need `sudo` on a Mac. The read is `hosts_entry` in `benchbar_site_list`; a drop that leaves the line behind says so in `manual_step`.
- Safety, `console` and `db`: a Python shell in a site and the site's SQL shell run any code or query with the site's own access. No plan or token can describe that, so the reads are `benchbar_site_list`, `benchbar_site_backups`, `benchbar_app_list` and the plan tools.
- Covered, `fg`: it stops the service to run honcho in front. `benchbar_up`, `benchbar_wait` and `benchbar_logs_tail` give an agent a running bench and its output without a terminal.
- Interactive by decision, `watch` (`benchbar watch`, rebuild assets on every change): a watcher that never ends is not a tool call, so it is a person's terminal command. What an agent needs from it, one asset rebuild after it changed JS or CSS, is `benchbar_run` with `build` (#257); only the continuous rebuild has no tool.
- Still no tool, as before: `repair`, `install`, `labs on`, `share`, `lan-guard`, `listen all`, a password in any form, `self-update`.
- `doctor --ack` and `--unack` are one tool, `benchbar_doctor_ack`: `check` (the id) and `unack: true` to show the warning again. Two optional string arguments `ack` and `unack`, of which exactly one is wanted, cannot be told in a schema, and a call with neither would be a tool error with no CLI run. It changes what doctor shows for a bench, not the bench, so it is a plain action tool without a plan. It runs the text form: under `--json` a refusal (an unknown check id, a FAIL) printed nothing at all. The fresh doctor report that follows carries the acknowledged ids.
- `service --apply TOKEN` now takes `--with-schedule` or `--without-schedule`. The token already held the scheduler choice, so a plan made with one could never be applied. The choice is kept in memory while the token is checked and stored only after the run succeeded; a stale token stores nothing. The service tools take `schedule` (true or false) in the plan and in the apply, which must agree.
- `status --json` has `autostart` (a bool), the read side of `autostart on|off`. `benchbar_autostart` is a plain action tool: the change is one flag in the bench's state file and the agent file, and a running bench keeps running.
- `ports apply` is a tool (`benchbar_ports_apply`) because it already is a reviewed plan with a token: the plan and its token come from `ports plan`. It refuses a path benchbar does not know: the apply registers each bench it sets up, so an unknown path would be a way to register. `ports setup` is the interactive form of plan then apply. `ports mode` is a plain action tool (it changes no port).
- `report` is one read tool, `benchbar_report`, that returns `report --print`: redacted text in one field. The zip is not offered: it is written to the person's Desktop on a Mac, outside the state folder, to be attached to an issue.
- `benchbar_site_drop_plan` and `benchbar_site_drop` refuse the bench's default site and take no `new_default` argument: every agent site tool promises that the default site never moves, and a drop with `--new-default` runs `bench use`. The refusal is an error result that gives the person the command (`benchbar site drop SITE --confirm-site SITE --new-default OTHER`); the CLI keeps that path for a person. The check runs before the person is asked, and the CLI would refuse it too, since no tool passes `--new-default`.
- The `site drop` token also covers the database name from the site's own `site_config.json` (the plan shows it as `database`): `bench drop-site` drops the database that file names, so a changed `db_name` between plan and apply is a stale token, not another database dropped.
- `site drop` has a plan token (`--dry-run --json`, then `--apply TOKEN --yes`): a SHA-256 of the plan and of what it was made from (the site list, the default site, the hosts line). The MCP tools never compute it. The apply asks no password: benchbar reads the MariaDB root password itself, and without one the run exits 2 before it changes anything.

## Apps and profiles input (issue 153)

- Who wrote a file decides how strict its `repo` check is (`FL_TOML_REPOS` for `toml.sh`). A file from someone else, which can name any repo and run its code on this machine, takes `https://`, `ssh://` and `user@host:path` only: `profile import`, `profile subscribe` and its updates, a profile in a `BENCHBAR_PROFILE_PATH` folder or with a `source` key (it was imported), and a lock inside an app repo of the bench (`--lock apps/...`, which changes when that app updates). The person's own files (a profile in `~/.config/benchbar/profiles` with no `source`, a lock outside `apps/`) also take a local path, `file://`, `http://` and `git://`, because `lock write` and `profile create` write the remotes the bench really has and read the file back; they warn per app whose remote teammates cannot clone, and still write. A `transport::address` form (`ext::`, `fd::`) is refused in every file and on the command line. `BENCHBAR_ALLOW_LOCAL_REPOS=1` is the person's own opt-in for a foreign file with a local repo; the test suites that import from `file://` folders set it, the harness does not.
- The `ext::` transport is turned off with `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT`, `GIT_CONFIG_KEY_n` and `GIT_CONFIG_VALUE_n` (`fl_git_ext_off`, set in `fl_git_net` and for `bench get-app`), after any the caller set, not with `git -c`: the mocks log the arguments, and `-c` would change every logged line. `bench get-app` runs its own git, so the variables are the only way to reach it.
- The notice of whose code an install runs lists host and owner (`github.com/acme`), not each repo. A "new" owner is one that no app in the bench and no repo of `config/apps.tsv` uses; `github.com/frappe` is always known. It is a warning, not a question: the existing confirmations stay the one gate.
- A lock `commit` needs 12 hex characters or more (a profile `commit` stays 7 to 40): `lock apply` matches a pin by prefix, and 7 characters can collide, but a profile pin is resolved by `get-app` against the repo it names.
- `app install` and `app add --site` take a backup by default (a `backup` step per site before its installs), because `install-app` syncs DocTypes and runs patches; `--skip-backup` is part of the plan, so the token changes with it. A failed backup skips the installs on that site.
- `changed[]` says `partial:` in the detail instead of adding a field: the entry shape stays `{kind, name, detail}`. It is written for a clone that was not built, an `install-app` or `migrate` that failed, and a fast forward that moved HEAD and then failed.
- The TSV readers split on the unit separator (`tr '\t' '\037'`), because a tab is white space for `read` and two tabs collapse into one; the rows of `release-profiles`, `app-bundles` and `wkhtmltopdf` are tested to have no empty cell.
- APP-13 (the HEAD of each app already in the bench in the plan token) is not in this change: it belongs on `plan-token.sh` of #225, which this branch does not have.
- History and leases share BENCHBAR_CALLER (0.9.0, #185 with #184): a lease uses its full value as the holder name, the history only its kind. The history keeps the kind in FL_CALLER and passes it to child runs as BENCHBAR_CALLER_KIND, and never rewrites BENCHBAR_CALLER; a value NAME:REST counts as the kind NAME when NAME is in the list (the MCP server holds leases as mcp:CLIENT:PID and its runs are mcp), and benchbar mcp sets BENCHBAR_CALLER_KIND=mcp so a holder name of the person's own still reads as mcp in the history.
- `benchbar run` is gated by a lease when its bench command changes the site (migrate, clear-cache, clear-website-cache, build, run-tests): a site lease when --site names one, else the bench; the reads of its allow list (doctor, list-apps, version) pass. The MCP tools that change things and came with or after leases (site drop, app install, app update, run, ports apply) take a `lease` argument like the others, so a lease you hold never refuses your own change.


## JSON correctness (issue 150)

- `fl_json_escape_v` runs under `local LC_ALL=C` (bytes, as under launchd) and tests for a byte above 127 with `[![:print:][:cntrl:]]`, a class test that needs no range; a plain ASCII value costs that one test and no process. Invalid UTF-8 is repaired in bash, byte by byte, not with `iconv`: a non-ASCII bench path is escaped many times per status poll, and one fork each would show in `tests/test-status-cost.sh`.
- An invalid byte becomes U+FFFD (one per byte) and not `?`: a reader can tell it from text, and it round trips through Swift and C# decoders. Overlong forms, surrogates and values above U+10FFFF are invalid, as in RFC 3629.
- Messages are cut with `fl_json_cut_v` at a byte count (500, 1000) and never inside a character: the cut backs up to the character before. Under a UTF-8 locale the old slice counted characters, so a CJK warning now keeps fewer of them; the limit is a size limit and bytes is what a reader pays for.
- Newline, tab and carriage return are `\n`, `\t`, `\r` and the other control characters `\u00XX`; they were deleted before, which joined the lines of a git error. A terminal color code is still dropped whole, since its text is not a word anyone wrote.
- `fl_json_str` still writes `null` for an empty string: every field documented as `string or null` (site, stop_reason, message, command) stays as recorded. `fl_json_text` is the helper for a field that is a plain string and may be empty; none of today's documents needs it, so no document changes. The rule is in the schema document for readers.
- `fl_json_num` writes `null` for a value that is not `-?(0|[1-9][0-9]*)`, not a repaired number: `007` could be a pid read wrong, and a guess would hide that.
- `doctor --json` names no site for a bench benchbar does not know (`fl_bench_adopted` false), the rule `status --json` has: the site doctor printed there was the default's guess.
- `fl_bench_candidates` no longer guesses `${SCRIPT_DIR}/frappe-bench`: it came in with the first commit, when the checkout sat beside the bench; no test, document or install form uses it, and from a Homebrew or app copy it pointed into the formula or the app bundle.
- `fl_table` reads `\|` in a row as a literal `|`, and `fl_cell_v` writes one; `benchbar list` uses it for the name, site and path, the only table whose cells are paths. A literal backslash then pipe in a cell is not a case worth a second escape.
- The internal marks `\034` (`FL_FIXQ`, the quoted fix mark), `\036` and `\037` (the doctor jobs and check rows separators) are still dropped by the escape: a fix command starts with the mark, and the control character rule would have written it as `\u001c` into every `fix_command` (found by test-doctor).
- The internal marks `\034` (`FL_FIXQ`, the quoted fix mark), `\036` and `\037` (the doctor jobs' and the check rows' separators) are still dropped by the escape: a fix command starts with the mark, and the control character rule would have written `\u001c` at the start of every `fix_command` (test-doctor found it).
- bash 3.2 on a terminal gets a `printf` function (#138): bash before 4.2 takes SIGCHLD without SA_RESTART, so a write to the terminal fails with EINTR ("printf: write error: Interrupted system call") when a background child ends at that moment, and `set -e` then ends the command in the middle of its output (seen in CI on a pty: `lan-guard install`, `pull`). `ui.sh` defines `printf` as a function only when bash is older than 4 and the run has a terminal; it tries a failed write to a terminal once more and leaves a write to a pipe or a file to the builtin. The cost is that a line can show twice after such a failure, which is better than a run that stops. A retry in each output helper was not chosen: most output lines are plain `printf` calls outside the helpers. The retry is for a failed write only: `printf` also returns 1 for a bad argument (`printf "%d" abc`) after it wrote its output, and the status does not tell the two apart, so the failed call first runs again with its output sent to /dev/null (a write there cannot be interrupted). When that fails too the arguments are at fault: its message and status are the answer and nothing is printed twice.
- A stop signal held while a doctor job starts is read after the hold ends (#138): `fl_par_group_off` clears `FL_PAR_STARTING` first and reads `FL_SIG_DEFERRED` second. A trap runs between two commands, so the other order dropped a TERM that came between the two lines, and the run went on to exit 0.
- `up` and `restart` write `Procfile.lean` and its two listen files again when they are not current (#188, follow up of SVC-2 and SHARE-1), instead of only warning. The choice was between that and a warning line on every start: the write is the one `repair` and `benchbar listen` already do (`act_write_procfile`: a backup, no sudo, no question, files with benchbar's own header), and a warning leaves the dev server and its debugger on the network of everyone who does not read it. Only these three files: the runner and the agent need a reload, which stays with `repair`. Not written: a bench set to `listen all` (the person's switch back, and the lines would be those of before 0.9.0 anyway), a `Procfile.lean` without benchbar's header, and `up` on a bench that already runs (a documented no-op; it warns and names `benchbar restart`). A write that fails warns and does not stop the start: the bench then listens as it did before the update. The rewrite of a file from before 0.9.0 is an `fl_warn`, so it reaches `warnings[]` of the done line, and the write is in `changed[]`.
- `uv` is required without looking at the version of frappe-bench (#188, follow up of SVC-5 and INST-1): the doctor check `toolchain_uv` warns, and the env rebuild, `app add` and `app update` stop before they change anything when it is missing, as `install` already did. A bench command older than 5.31 does not call `uv`, but telling the two apart costs a `bench --version` on every app command, and `brew install uv` is harmless there. The check looks where the CLI looks (PATH, Homebrew's `bin`, `~/.local/bin`), not on the bench's launchd PATH: the CLI runs the bench commands that need it. The `uv` that pipx puts into frappe-bench's own venv is not used: its folder also holds a `python` and a `honcho` that must not reach PATH. `uv_install` is a Mac action (`brew install uv`); on Linux the fix is the phase 00 script, the only place that runs the pinned and checked installer. The env rebuild also refuses when the `bench` command is missing, for the same reason: the env is never moved aside when nothing can build the new one.
- `pull --keep-secrets` is refused under `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1` (`--for-agent`, `benchbar mcp`), the plan included and whatever `--yes` says (#186, follow up of SVC-4): a copy with production's integrations and stored passwords live is a person's decision, the tier `listen all` and `mariadb-password` use. No terminal is required, because the Mac app and the Windows tray run the pull with `--yes --json` after the person ticked the box, and neither sets the mode. As with the other person guards, a process that leaves the variable out is not stopped: a rail against the documented agent modes, not a boundary.
- `site restore --neutralise` runs pull's neutralise step (the one list in `neutralise.sh`, Administrator's login kept) after the restore and the encryption key, before the scheduler resumes and before migrate (#186). A set is "outside" when `--from` is a path whose folder is neither the backup folder of a site of this bench nor under benchbar's own backups (compared with `-ef`, so a link does not change it); a stamp is always the bench's own. Outside without the flag: a person gets a warning in the plan (text and `warnings[]`) and may go on, a non interactive run gets an error in the plan (`errors[]`, `can_apply: false`), so the MCP plan tool already says it. A `pull --keep-staging` folder (`<bench>/.benchbar/pulls`) counts as outside on purpose: it holds production's set. No new JSON key: the plan's `steps[]` has the `neutralise` step, so the existing token data covers the flag and the token of one plan does not run the other. Muting email and pausing the scheduler (pull's `dev_safety` step) are not part of the flag: it is the neutralise list, which switches the mail accounts off and drops the queued mail.
## 0.9.0: site snapshots and rollback (issue 182, part 1)

- A snapshot is bench's own backup with files, moved with `mv` (a rename inside `sites/SITE`, so no second copy of the bytes) from `private/backups` to `sites/SITE/snapshots/<stamp>[-label]/`. frappe's `delete_temp_backups` empties `private/backups` after `keep_backups_for_hours`, which is why benchbar has to keep copies there (`fl_backups_keep_begin`); a snapshot is meant to last, so it lives where nothing prunes. The folder is mode 0700: the dump holds password hashes. Taking one reuses `fl_site_backup_take`, so the older backups of the site are still kept.
- The manifest is `manifest.json` with one key per line and one app per line, so bash reads it with `sed` and `site snapshots` needs no Python. Labels cannot hold quotes, backslashes or control characters, for the same reason. The apps and commits are those of the bench's git apps (`fl_apps_all`), not of the site: the question a rollback asks is whether the bench's code moved.
- `site rollback` is `site restore` with a snapshot folder as its source (`SR_ROLLBACK=1` in `site-restore.sh`), not a second engine: the same plan, token, steps and verify. The `local_backup` step becomes `snapshot` (label `before rollback`), the plan gets a `rollback` block, and its errors and hints name `site rollback`. The snapshot step runs first, and the failure message of a rollback whose restore fails says how to use its snapshot (the undo point rules are below).
- Migrate runs only when the snapshot's app commits differ from the bench's (an app added, removed or moved), because a database dumped with the same code needs none, and a migrate on a restored site is slow and can change it. A manifest with no app list migrates (the safe side). `--no-migrate` always wins, and the verify step does not warn about a skipped migrate when benchbar chose to skip it.
- A rollback across an app change is allowed and warned, not refused: going back after a bad app update is exactly the use. The warning is in the plan (`warnings`, `rollback.apps_match`, `changed_apps`) and the text plan.
- The default site is refused for an agent inside the plan (an error, so `can_apply` is false and the MCP server never records the plan), not in `mcp.py`: the CLI knows the default site (`currentsite.txt` and `FL_SITE`) and every agent path (`--for-agent`, `benchbar mcp`, `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE=1`) reaches it. A person can roll it back, and the question names it as the default site. A person is a terminal on stdin or the app's own claim (`fl_lease_person_ok`), not only the absence of `BENCHBAR_NONINTERACTIVE`: `--yes` with no terminal, as a plain shell of an agent has, is refused in the plan too. A snapshot of the default site is allowed: it only adds a folder.
- Retention reports and never deletes. The newest N (default 10, bench state key `SNAPSHOT_KEEP`, or `--keep N`) are the limit; `site snapshots` and the result of `site snapshot` list what is beyond it. Only a person's `site snapshot drop SITE --prune` removes them, and never the `before rollback` snapshot of the last rollback. A safety net that removes itself when the eleventh is taken, or when an agent takes one, would be the surprise this feature exists to prevent; the cost is a disk that fills, and the listing says so. No environment variable carries the limit, so the Windows shim forwards nothing new.
- `site snapshot drop` is a third word under `site snapshot` because the commands table resolves two words (`site snapshot` covers both). It is refused before the lock for an agent (`fl_need` "a person at a terminal"), also when `--yes` is given, and again inside the function; its `--dry-run` is open to agents, as a plan is. It removes the files of the snapshot one by one and then the folder with `rmdir`, never a recursive delete: a folder that holds anything else stays.
